What he was less grateful for, and had noticed more since coming home, was how readily the body still reached for that narrowed state on the smallest provocation — a sound outside the expected range, a movement at the edge of sight that resolved to nothing. His hands would go on running the machine correctly while some other part of him noted the deviation, judged it harmless, and filed it, all inside the span of a single pass. He could not stop the mechanism operating. He had decided some months ago not to find it troubling. It was how a body adjusted to what it had been through, and a body was not wrong to adjust.

There was one more thing he had brought home, and he thought of it now and then, without elaborating on it. The first morning he had walked back onto this floor, eight months ago, put the canvas coat on, checked the station, and felt his hands settle into work they already knew — there had been a relief in it that had nothing to do with any decision he'd made. Not relief at the familiar, precisely. Relief at being occupied by a task exactly the size of the task, no larger, with nothing left over to spend on anything else. He had not known he needed that until it happened, and the gratitude that came with it had been out of all proportion to the occasion. He had told no one. He had gone home, eaten his supper, and slept better than he had slept any night since his return. He thought of this now, briefly, the way he thought of most things at the machine — and then he went back to the work, which was where he belonged.

The contract material this run was heavier than the last, a wool-cotton blend that ran stiffer under the shuttle and wanted a higher tension to hold the count, which accounted for the whole week's trouble and which Hartner had rightly predicted would ease by the second hour. It did. The stiffness let go as the material warmed from handling, the tension dropped into its expected range, and the ache in Rowe's shoulders — the overworked ache of the morning's first hour — settled into the honest ache of the right amount of labor, the kind that reports itself without demanding anything back.

Down the row the other machines ran in their various states of correct, and he knew most of them by character the way a man knows what he lives among without being able to say when he learned it: the one six stations along that ran fractionally fast, the pair at the far end sharing a shuttle quirk neither operator had ever complained about, the careful and precise young man at the end of the row who would be very good indeed in another two years. He could not have told anyone when he had learned any of it. He simply had.

The break came at the shift's midpoint, twelve minutes, officially for maintenance, practically for standing still and drinking something. He stood at the east window, which ran the upper third of the outer wall, built for light rather than view, and from where he stood he could see sky and the tops of the buildings opposite and, if he leaned, a narrow strip of the canal. He didn't lean. He watched the sky, which had changed since the walk in — high grey cloud coming from the west, the kind that arrived faster than a man registered it arriving, while the sun still held clear in the east, which meant the lateral light on the floor still had a while to run. He drank water that tasted faintly of the pipes, which was ordinary, and thought about nothing in particular, which was the skill of a break: to let the twelve minutes stay empty rather than fill them, because a man returns to the machine differently from emptiness than from planning.

Hartner stood beside him at the glass. "Garden wants rain," he said, after a while.

Rowe considered the cloud coming in. "This afternoon."

"Won't be any use." Hartner said it the way a man reports a small, settled injustice. "Too late in the season."

Rowe had not, in truth, given much thought to what a garden wanted in autumn. "Next year, then," he said.

Hartner appeared to find this sufficient, and they finished their water and went back.

At the shift's midpoint the sun came in low from the east and caught the thread-dust — the fine particulate the machines threw constantly into the air, cotton and wool mixed with the contract material, invisible in ordinary light, a thing a man breathed all day without seeing it, known only by the specific dry catch in the throat by the end of a full run. In the lateral sun it turned visible: a golden haze at chest height across the whole floor, moving in the slow currents the machines exhaled, and thirty men working through it at full pace, none of them sparing it a glance, because sparing it a glance required stopping, and stopping required a reason.

He had stopped for it once, his first autumn on the floor, years before any of this. Greiss had redirected him with the flat palm, and he had never stopped again. But he saw it every year the angle came round to it, and he saw it now.

It put him in mind of something, the way certain qualities of light did — arriving before he could name it and gone before he needed to. Stone-dust, not thread-dust. Cold, not warm. Earlier than this, and a narrower light coming through a narrower gap, and other men moving through that other haze for reasons he had no wish to examine at his machine in the third hour of a Thursday shift. He noted it, the way he noted the weather, and let it go.

Five seconds, at the most. He was back at the machine, hands on the work, attention on the shuttle return and the thread count, the left-side hesitation running inside its expected range. The floor was loud in the good way. The cloud would deliver its rain by afternoon, and at noon he would put the wool coat back on and walk home along a canal gone a shade darker under it, past the drayman already back at the stables and the ironmonger's dog under whatever shelter it kept for wet days, and the elm above the bench would still be holding its leaves, a week yet before it let them all go at once. He would eat. He would sleep the particular shallow, edge-alert, dreamless two hours that afternoon sleep had become in France and had simply stayed that way since, the body having found no reason to change it back. In the evening he might walk, if the weather allowed, out along the far bank of the canal to the stretch of path he was fond of in autumn — a very modest ambition for a Sunday afternoon, his sister had once called it, and he had considered the assessment fair and had not disputed it.

He knew this Thursday the way he knew all the Thursdays that had come before it: completely, without drama, the plain knowledge of a life that was his and whose terms he understood. The first shift. The canvas coat. Hartner at the next machine, thinking about his garden. The bread and the tea that did its job. Greiss moving the floor with the economy of a man who lived there. The hesitation in the left-side shuttle return that was never going to be properly cured and that he had long since filed under feature rather than fault.

He was at his machine, and the morning was half done, and the sun held its lateral angle, and the thread-dust moved in it, and down the row Hartner's machine ran with the rhythm of twenty-two years, and across the floor Greiss went on between the rows, and the new material held its tension inside the expected range, and his shoulders carried the honest ache of work rightly done, and his hands knew what they were about without being told.

Nothing was wrong.

The shuttle ran. The thread fed. His hands moved without instruction, and the floor ran on around him, and the morning was exactly what a morning was.

[Beat 5]
﻿THE AUTHORIZATION

The canonical property recovery instrument arrived on her desk on a Tuesday, in the bottom third of the morning stack, filed by Canon Immaculate Tomasz Prelacze under the standard dispute-resolution authorization number and routed through the Scribe's office for copy and notarization in the ordinary course of business. It was the ninth instrument she processed that morning. It was not the kind of document that required unusual attention.

Vega read it twice anyway.

Not because there was anything wrong with it. She had been copying canonical property recovery instruments for three years, and this one was standard in every technical respect: the language correct, the authorization code valid, the Canon's seal properly affixed. She read it twice because of a habit she had developed in the past eight months that had nothing to do with accuracy - the private, unofficial, and entirely unauthorized suspicion that the sum of several ordinary documents could add up to something she did not yet have a category for.

She set it down. She picked up the next instrument in the stack, found nothing in it, and came back to the first.

-

The Scribe's office occupied the second floor of the east administrative wing - a stone room with two narrow, south-facing windows that admitted the morning sun in strips that crossed the floor and moved as the day did, predictable as the tide. The embrasures were deep enough to sit in. She had done it once, in her first year, and not again, because the stone was cold and the angle wrong and the floor was better. The walls held shelves of canonical codebooks, each volume in its assigned position, each position known to her without looking. Seven hundred years of institutional sediment. The stone absorbed light and gave it back reluctantly, the way old buildings do when they have stopped being surprised by anything the sun does.

Her desk was organized in the way that three years of the same work organizes a desk: everything in the position that minimized unnecessary motion. Blank instrument forms on the left. Incoming stack centered. Outgoing - completed and sealed - on the right. The codebook open to the section she was currently working, weighted flat by a smooth grey stone she had carried since her first posting. Not a talisman. She did not attach meaning to objects. It was the right weight, it fit her grip, and it had never once failed to hold a page against a draft.

Pinned to the shelf edge above the desk, in ink gone the color of weak tea, someone long before her had written a line she had stopped seeing sometime in her first year, the way you stop seeing anything nailed to a wall you pass sixty times a day: *An accurate copy outlives the men who lied to fill it.* Nobody had ever told her who wrote it. She had never asked. It had become part of the shelf.

The lamp was one she had replaced herself two days prior, because the maintenance rotation used a smaller wick than she preferred, and the smaller wick produced a light that made her eyes ache by midafternoon, and her eyes were going to be occupied through midafternoon. She kept a stock of the correct wicks in the bottom drawer. She had asked, in her first year, twice, for the rotation to be updated to reflect her preference. Both requests had been received with great courtesy and no result. She had stopped asking. She kept the stock.

She had developed, in three years of becoming good at this, the thing that accompanies any sufficiently practiced skill past the point of requiring conscious management: the capacity to do it without thinking, which freed her attention for the thing adjacent to it. The adjacent thing was the text.

This was not, she understood, part of the Scribe's official function. The official function was accurate copying and notarization. The text was not her concern.

She had stopped believing this approximately eight months ago.

-

The canonical property recovery instrument for the Oculus Memoriae read as follows:

*By authority of the Canon Immaculate, exercising plenary jurisdiction in matters of Relic provenance and custodial accountability under the Liturgy's founding compact with the House of Ocipheus:*

*Whereas the Oculus Memoriae (Registered Relic, Ocipheus Station, Year 397, Inventory Reference O-001) has been held under a vigil custodian arrangement requiring renewal of custodial certification every fifty years, and*

*Whereas the certification dated Year 1097 (Keeper Fen Aldric, countersigned Canon Marta Veyne, Canonical Registry Reference CV-1097-88) was rendered without continuous confirming authority upon the death of Canon Marta Veyne in Year 1119, and*

*Whereas the Canon Immaculate, appointed Year 1134, has identified the absence of continuous countersigning authority as a material lapse in the custodial certification chain under provisions governing uninterrupted Relic accountability:*

*Therefore, let this instrument authorize the Canon Immaculate to initiate review of the Oculus Memoriae's current custodial status and, if review finds the certification lapse material to uninterrupted accountability, to take such steps as canonical law provides for the resolution of custodial disputes in Relic provenance.*

*Seal of the Canon Immaculate. This eighteenth day, Month of the High Autumn, Year 1138.*

Vega read it twice.

The Year 1097 certification had been properly executed by Keeper Fen Aldric and countersigned by Canon Marta Veyne and filed in the canonical registry the same year. Canon Marta Veyne had died in Year 1119, nineteen years after the certification, not before it. A certifying authority who died after executing a document did not thereby unsign it. The certification was valid from the date of execution. It remained valid. The provisions governing uninterrupted Relic accountability applied to the currency of the certification cycle - whether a new certification was presently due - not to the continued living availability of whoever had countersigned forty-one years prior. The next certification was due in Year 1147. Nine years hence.

There was no lapse. What the instrument called a gap was the ordinary interval between certifications, the interval in which, per the founding compact itself, no certification was required. The argument depended on reading continuous countersigning authority as a condition of ongoing validity rather than a requirement satisfied at the moment of certification. That was not what the provisions said. It was a reading the provisions could survive if nobody checked. A careful reading, with the relevant dates in front of it, could not survive it at all.

She had copied the custodial accountability provisions for three years. She knew the section the way you know a road walked in every weather - by its texture underfoot, not by consulting a map. She knew what the provisions said. She knew what this instrument was doing to them.

She did not note it on the instrument. Her function was accurate copying and notarization. She copied it correctly, in the precise canonical hand three years had built into her wrist, affixed her notary seal in the correct position, and set it in the outgoing stack.

She picked up the next instrument.

-

At midday, the activity in the courtyard below her window stopped being routine.

She had watched that courtyard for three years and knew its ordinary: the maintenance crew's wheelbarrow against the east wall on its scheduled days, the filing clerks taking air at midmorning, the Descent Corps courier who came through at the same hour every week and had learned, over years of the same route, the exact line through the flagstones that avoided the gate requiring a key. She knew the courtyard's pace and population through every season. In three years she had not once seen it set up for a Great Scrying.

The primary frame was four meters across. She knew the scale of the portable certification units - she had watched those raised for vigil-tests, for maintenance activations, for the routine assessments the smaller installations required. This was not that. The Catalyst housing was a different material, denser, the color of deep charcoal rather than the grey-brown of a standard frame, and the anchoring crew drove pinning rods into the flagstone joints with the particular attention of people expecting real membrane pressure. Nobody anchored like that for a demonstration.

Two apparatus crews, eight people, working with no ceremony and considerable efficiency. A Descent Corps evaluator she did not recognize stood to one side with a clipboard and a field notation kit - the kind carried in a pack, built for use without a stable surface, not the kind kept on a desk. Three Corps soldiers in full field dress, not station secondment, two of them holding an iron guide-frame she recognized from the warehouse transit records she filed occasionally - the bracket assembly used for moving large constructs through spaces not built for them. It was pre-positioned. They had known what they were going to need before they needed it.

She watched, because watching was correct when the courtyard was doing something she would need to register accurately once the summary crossed her desk.

The apparatus activated. The air at the frame's center lost the character of ordinary courtyard air and became instead a surface - membranous, fleshy in a way she had never once let herself say aloud, gone translucent and dark-veined the way skin goes when something presses it from underneath. She had seen the aperture perhaps fifty times. It was never quite the same twice, and it was always the same in the one way that mattered: the moment the wound opened and the gold showed through, you knew. The light that came through it was not the courtyard's light. It was light that had crossed from somewhere else's morning or afternoon or season and arrived here slightly wrong, warmed amber at the edges the way the whole aperture always was, wet-looking even from a second-floor window. The aperture held for perhaps thirty seconds, and then it closed the way a cut closes over, and by evening no one standing in that courtyard would be able to point to the place in the air where it had been.

She did not see the construct clearly - the aperture sat at ground level and the thing came through below her sightline, and for a breath the flagstones under the frame went dark and slick, as if the stone itself had gone wet and then thought better of it. She saw the evaluator write without looking up. She saw the two guide-frame soldiers step back and reposition left, which told her the thing was larger than they had rigged for, or offset from where they had expected it. She saw the third soldier start toward it and then stop, the posture of someone given a clear instruction and new information in the same second.

Something cold crossed her chest, low, under the ribs. Not fear - she was watching from a window, nothing in the courtyard could reach her - but the cold of the aperture itself, which was not in the room and which she felt anyway, the way a change in pressure registers in the body before the mind calls it weather. She had felt it faintly, before, near vigil-tests. She had never felt it this plainly from a second-floor window.

They moved it toward the south service gate. She could track it partway - a shadow crossing the flagstones at a scale that matched none of the standard equipment she had ever seen carried through that courtyard. The guide-frame soldiers walked alongside it now, hands off the bracket, the posture of escort rather than control. It went through the gate and was gone.

Before she stepped back from the window she noted where the gate led: the maintenance passage to three storage halls on the administrative complex's south side. Two were in regular use; she had filed their rotation records herself. The third had been locked for eleven years. She had filed the lock authorization in her first year, under the previous senior Scribe's hand - a routine structural-assessment lock. The assessment had concluded in Year 1133, per the resolution notation she had copied as a junior apprentice. The hall had never been reopened.

It was open now.

The assessment summary arrived an hour later, standard routing from the Descent Corps evaluator for canonical registration as an official Station activity record. The heading read: *War Construct Evaluation - Sphere 42 Origin - Designation: WC-Eirene (provisional).* She copied it accurately. The summary described a construct of substantial dimension - she copied the measurements without particular attention - evaluated for load capacity, command responsiveness, and field deployment suitability under the standard Descent Corps rubric. The evaluator's finding: *suitable for forward staging, pending secondary structural assessment under expanded-force parameters.*

She noted the word *provisional*. Constructs received permanent designations once confirmed for deployment; she had filed six such instruments in three years. Provisional meant the designation was held pending the secondary assessment. It also meant the question of how many was not yet resolved.

WC-Eirene. One entry among many. She filed it in the outgoing stack and picked up the next.

-

The transit suspension arrived in the afternoon, buried in a batch authorization covering operational adjustments to field personnel deployment across the High Autumn period. Eleven pages. She read batch authorizations of this kind often enough to move through them at pace, assessing each clause for copy accuracy, not slowing unless something required it.

She slowed on page eight.

Templar field designations ran T-prefix, posting-station identifier, sequential number - a system whose entire value was its simplicity. You could process a deployment record, assess a field authority question, revoke or reinstate a mandate, without the Knight's given name entering the authorization chain until the revision was fully processed and the affected party formally notified. The designation carried the operational information. The name sat adjacent to it, in a different registry, behind a different door. Her function did not require her to open that door.

Dame Lyra of Ocipheus. Templar. Three infusions. Field authority suspended pending canonical review of relevant certification.

The review cited was the same review - she recognized the authorization number; she had copied its origin that morning. The property recovery instrument and the transit suspension were the same operation, filed through different tracks so the connection would not be visible to anyone reading either document alone. She had done this three years. She understood what documents looked like when they were built to be read separately.

Ordinarily that would have been the end of it. But a suspension of field authority is not self-executing. It has to be served, and canonical law does not permit service on a designation - a document cannot be handed to a number. The courier copy required a name, and the name appeared on the third page, in the standard delivery-block format she had copied perhaps two hundred times in three years without once needing it to mean anything:

*To be delivered, upon return to posting, in person, to the following: Dame Lyra of Ocipheus, Knight Templar, three infusions, Ocipheus Station, in accordance with Article Nine of the Field Authority Code.*

She did not read it a third time. She did not need to.

Above the desk, in ink gone the color of weak tea: *An accurate copy outlives the men who lied to fill it.* She had stopped seeing it in her first year. She saw it now, for exactly as long as it took her to stop looking at it, and then she stood up.

She stood at her desk for a moment with no task in front of her, which had not happened in three years.

Then she sat back down, because there was nothing on her feet that was not also true of her sitting down, and finished copying the batch. She copied the transit suspension accurately, in the same hand she had used on the eight instruments before it. She affixed her notary seal. She did not let the pen slow. Three years of practice exist for exactly this - the capacity to do the correct thing with a hand that no longer entirely belongs to you.

She added the batch to the outgoing stack.

-

In the afternoon she carried the day's outgoing stack to the distribution window in the administrative corridor, where the clerk who had taken it from her perhaps three hundred times over three years said, "Busy day."

"Nine authorizations, one batch, one assessment registration." She handed the stack over in the order she always used - outgoing separated from reference copies, notary seals facing the same direction. Habit older than thinking about it. "The property instrument needs a cross-reference notation to this afternoon's batch. Same canonical review number. They're the same operation filed separately."

He looked at her for a moment before he wrote it down - not, she thought, because he doubted her, but because cross-referencing two filings in the distribution record sat outside the standard intake notation and he wanted to be precise about it. "Do you want this flagged to the Canon's filing office?"

"It's informational. The operation resolves under whichever instrument reaches completion first."

He noted it. She went back upstairs and did not look at him again, because there was nothing further to say that her face could afford to say.

-

The amber of late High Autumn light came through the south windows at the angle of five o'clock and lit the papers on her desk sideways, showing the vellum's texture - the small variations in the surface you could see in the right light and mostly felt with your fingers, the grain of a canonical instrument prepared with the proper compound. She had looked at that texture for three years.

The property recovery instrument was in the outgoing stack. The transit suspension was in the outgoing stack. The war construct evaluation was in the outgoing stack. Three documents. One operation. She had written *no action indicated* into enough folios over three years to know exactly what that phrase was built to hold, and exactly how much it could not hold, and none of that was going to help her now, so she set the thought aside for a day that had room for it.

The custodial certification gap for the Oculus Memoriae did not exist under the provisions the instrument cited. The transit suspension covering the same operational period carried her sister's name. The war construct designation was provisional and had been moved into a hall locked for eleven years and locked no longer.

Eirene. The eastern Wall checkpoint bore that name. It was also the name of the last Sinter champion, the Pallor Knight who had held the eastern pass during the Year 830 emergence, whose account of what she had seen in the zone had been submitted to the canonical record and processed, per a reference she had crossed twice in three years, as a standing observation with no current response required. She did not know the account's content. It was not in the general archive. She knew only its filing status, because she had seen the notation with her own hand, more than once.

*No action indicated.*

She thought about why an operation built to review a Relic's custodial certification would also need to guarantee that the Knight responsible for that Relic's vigil could not be in the field while the review proceeded. She did not write the answer down. She was not required to write down everything she understood, only what she was told to copy, and this was the difference the vow had never quite specified and that she had spent eight months discovering the shape of on her own.

She was furniture. She had known this about her function for three years and had not minded it, because furniture was load-bearing and the institution needed load-bearing things and she was suited to the work. She had not previously considered that furniture remembers everything that happens in the room, in order, accurately, whether or not it is asked to.

-

Her sister had left three days before.

The note had been on the scribing table at the Station when she packed her kit and rode out for the central office - the portable table she used on contract there, folded now into a case two hundred kilometers south of this desk. She had not brought the note. She was not the kind of person who kept paper for sentiment. She kept the memory and left the page.

Lyra's hand was the hand of someone who had spent thirty years taking field notes in poor light in small spaces: dense, lateral, the letters compressed the way a mind compresses what a medium cannot easily hold. Not a considered hand. A fast one. The pen had barely lifted between strokes.

*Back in three days. Don't eat the station bread.*

The station bread had been underproofed roughly two-thirds of the time for as long as Vega had made the contract visit, which was three years. Lyra had registered a formal complaint with the Station kitchen twice, in the measured tone of someone who believed the record should reflect a problem even when the problem would never be addressed. She had eaten the bread anyway. You ate what was in front of you.

It was the fourth day. Four days was within normal range for an eastern-sector observation - Lyra had come back from postings inside a day before, and stayed away twice that long when a resolution demanded it. Six days would begin to sit outside the range. Six days had not happened yet.

She thought about the suspension instead - about the fact that it had been authorized before the property review was filed to justify it, and that the review itself would not find a lapse where none existed, not if anyone competent read the dates, and that competence had evidently not been required of whoever would act on the finding. She thought about the hall that had been locked eleven years and now held something with a provisional name identical to a checkpoint and a dead champion's account that had been filed and never answered.

She thought about her sister riding back into a posting where her own field authority no longer legally existed, and not knowing it, because the courier had not yet caught her, because couriers took days and operations did not wait for couriers to finish their work before beginning.

She did not know what to do with any of this that her vow permitted. She knew what her vow required, and she had already done it, in the correct hand, with the seal in the correct position, and that was the part she could not put down for the rest of the evening no matter how she tried.

-

She ate in the common room at the table she usually sat at and said the things that were said in common rooms - the coming cold, the provision cycle's allocation schedule, whether the east corridor's heating element would be repaired before first frost or, as in each of the last two winters, after it. She listened. She added an observation where she had one. She ate the bread, which was dense and a little sour and not what she would have chosen, and thought that she would give a great deal, just now, for a complaint about bread to be the worst thing in her possession.

She went to her quarters. She lay on her bed with her hands folded on her chest and looked at the ceiling in the dark, which was what she did when she was processing something that did not fit any category she owned.

Her categories were good. Three years of careful, attentive work in an institution that had been generating canonical instruments since before the Houses held their current names had built her categories, and they were sound, and they had covered everything she had ever been asked to file. The trouble was not the categories. The trouble was that what had arrived on her desk this morning and afternoon, in specifically illuminating order, did not belong to any category that existed yet. It belonged to a different kind of record - the kind that did not begin with *By authority of the Canon Immaculate* and end with a notary seal, the kind that began with what she had actually seen and what she understood when she held the day's three documents together instead of apart.

She lay in the dark and thought about the construct crossing the courtyard with the gait of something built to receive one direction and hold it precisely until told to stop, and she thought about *forward staging* as a phrase inside a finding, and she thought about a hall locked eleven years standing open now, and she thought about her sister's name typed into a delivery block on the third page of a document that would not reach her for days.

She thought, once more, about the line above her desk, and understood - not for the first time, but for the first time in a way that would not let her put it down again - that an accurate copy outlives the men who lied to fill it only if someone keeps the copy. She intended to keep hers.

She went to sleep badly, and in the morning she got up and went to work.

[Beat 6]
﻿THE TAKING

MYRMIDON INTAKE RECORD - STATION VERLAINE NORTH - THE THAW, YEAR 1138
Origin: Sphere 31. Batch 12 of the Thaw rotation.
Designation issued: M-1018883.
Prior catalogue entry: none. Prior membrane history: none.
Oath of compact administered at time of processing. Designee's comprehension of oath contents: not assessed.
Status: Received. Transferred to conscript regiment, eastern barracks. No follow-up required.

-

The thread-dust catches the light off the high windows the way it always does at this hour, the morning half-done, the machines running at full weight, and Soren Rowe - Ted to the men who had marched beside him and were mostly not living men any longer, Rowe to everyone at the mill because a surname was all a loom floor required of you - does not look at it. Nine years he has been not-looking at it. The thread-dust has become weather. It is not a thing you notice any more than you notice the air agreeing to be breathed.

His hands know the shuttle. This morning they know it better than usual: two turns of the secondary feed nut before dawn, a correction Greiss would call unauthorised and would be right to call unauthorised, and the machine has run clean for two hours on the strength of it, which is two hours longer than any adjustment of his has ever held. Past the halfway mark of the shift now, the morning tipping downhill toward the dinner bell. Hartner at the next frame has gone somewhere else in his head entirely - the particular quality of Hartner's stillness that means he is thinking about the allotment, though it is not the growing season and there is nothing in the allotment to think about except what he intends to plant in it, which Rowe has never asked him about because the asking would want more words than the two of them typically spend on each other, and the answer, in any case, is probably not meant for him.

It was cold on the canal road in the dark, the ironmonger's terrier going off at nothing behind its gate the way it always does, his coat buttoned to the throat against air that had the particular raw bite the city gets in the thaw, before the frost has properly let go of it. Marte's counter, the same stool, the same bowl, coffee black enough to argue with. Home eight months from France and the mill has taken him back without asking him anything about where he had been, which is the mercy of machinery: it does not care what a man has seen, only whether his hands still know the work. His hands still know the work.

The machine under his hands is running right. This is the entire content of the morning, and it is enough.

Then the floor stops being a floor.

He will spend the rest of his life circling that sentence and will never improve on it, though it says almost nothing of what happened. Nothing precedes it. No sound that does not belong to the loom floor, no light that is the wrong colour, no smell, no warning any sense he owns is equipped to register as a warning. One instant his hands are on the shuttle and the machine is under them the way it has been under them for nine years. The next instant there is no machine, no hands he can locate, and the direction that has always, without argument, been beneath his boots has simply declined to continue being beneath his boots.

It is not pain. He will insist on this afterward, in the dark, turning it over the way you turn over a coin you cannot place the value of: it was not pain first. Pain is a report the body files about the body, and for one long unbearable interval he has no body to file a report from. What he has instead is the specific, appalling sensation of being drawn. Not falling - falling has a direction, falling has a floor waiting somewhere beneath it to be arrived at eventually - but drawn, sideways and up and through, the way a hook set deep in a trout's lip takes the whole fish out of the river in a single motion the fish never sees coming because the fish, until that moment, did not know it was a thing suspended in something. He has lived thirty-one years inside his own skin the way a fish lives in water: entirely, unquestioningly, without once understanding that a man is also a thing that can be taken out of the medium he is suspended in. Something reaches into the warm dark he has occupied since before he had a word for himself - into the shuttle-hand, the coal-smoke lungs, the whole unexamined fact of being a person located at a place - and closes on him, and lifts.

He goes out the way a lamp goes out. Not into a darker room. Out of the having of a room at all.

-

After the pluck there is no after, for a long while, in any sense that a clock would recognise.

There is a spark, and the spark is aware, in the diminished and terrible way a spark can be aware of anything, that it is a spark and not a man, and that it is suspended in something warm and gold, thick as mead poured slow, the colour of lamplight caught in a bottle of honey-water, with no bottom he can locate and no roof and no wall to put a hand against and confirm the existence of a hand. It clings where he passes through it the way birth-fluid clings, and seals again behind him, seamless, as if the wound of his passing had already started to heal before he was all the way through it. No up. He tries for up out of old habit, the reflex of thirty-one years of having a head that assumes a direction for the sky, and finds nothing the reflex can attach itself to. No cold, because cold requires a surface for the cold to be felt against, and he no longer has one. No sound, because sound is air moving, and there is no air here to move - only the slow, honeyed drag of the medium itself, unhurried, indifferent, the way water is indifferent to the thing drowning in it.

There is, instead, wreckage.

It drifts past him the way the debris of a wrecked barge drifts down a slow river, unhurried, indifferent to being watched, gone amber and half-dissolved in the warm water that carries it: not pictures, nothing so orderly as pictures, but the actual broken matter of a life he recognises as having belonged to him a very short time ago. The loom floor turns past, thread-dust and all, tumbling slowly end over end like a thing thrown from a height. The canal - the terrier, the dark, the particular cold of the stone bridge parapet under his glove - turns past it, close enough to reach for and already receding before the reaching means anything. France comes past in fragments that hurt in a way nothing else in the gold hurts: chalk mud sucking at a boot, the flat crack of something close enough to feel in the teeth, a hand that was Corbett's hand and then was not a hand any longer, and none of it in order, none of it asking to be understood, all of it simply going by. And his mother's kitchen - the oilcloth on the table, worn pale at the corner where her elbow went every evening, the kettle's particular two-note whistle, bread cooling under a cloth on a Thursday not unlike this one - turns slowly out of reach the way the furthest pieces do, and he understands, with the clean useless clarity a spark is apparently still capable of, that he is watching himself come apart, and that no part of it will wait for him to say goodbye to it properly.

He tries to reach for the kitchen. There is nothing to reach with. There is only the gold, closing soft over the place the reaching happened, the way water closes over the place a hand was pulled out of it, and underneath the closing a horror with no better name yet than this: that he is unborn again, a spark adrift in some other world's blood, with no body anywhere in it built to hold him.

-

Then there is weight, and with the weight, a kind of dryness - the gold he had been drifting in gone all at once, the way water sheets off a hand hauled up out of a bucket in one motion, though there is no skin left underneath for him to feel it go.

Wrong weight - heavier in the shoulders than a man has any business being heavy, lighter somewhere in the chest where a man expects to carry something, the proportions of himself redrawn by a hand that was working from an entirely different set of specifications. He tries to open his eyes and there is a sensation of aperture rather than eyelid, a single oval widening onto grey light the way a shutter opens onto a lens, and what it shows him is a yard. Packed earth. A high grey sky doing nothing in particular. A line of others like himself, oil-black and jointed and identical, stretching to either side further than the aperture will turn to show him.

He tries to stand and the body answers him half a beat late, the way a echo answers a shout after the shout has already stopped meaning anything, and when it does answer it tells him nothing back. No warmth returning up through the soles of feet that are not feet. No ache in shoulders that carried a rifle across half of France and are carrying nothing now but do not say so, one way or the other, because they have no way of saying anything at all. No breath. He understands this last one slowest and worst: he is trying to breathe, out of the same blind animal habit that reached for up in the gold, and there is no rise, no fall, no air moving anywhere inside him to be moved, because there is no inside for it to move in. A body that reports nothing is not a numb body. A numb body is still telling you something, even if the something is an absence. This tells him nothing at all, in either direction, and the not-telling is its own particular species of horror, one he has no word ready for and will spend the coming months building one out of whatever the Caul's language eventually allows him.

He tries to scream.

Something happens in the place a scream would have started. Air is moved - he can register that much, a mechanism somewhere in the jointed throat doing its designated work - and what comes out is not his voice. It is not any voice he would recognise as belonging to a living thing. It is a short flat tone, ugly and functional, the sound a thing makes when a thing is required to make a sound and has been given no better instrument to make it with. He hears it and understands, in the same instant, two facts that will not separate from each other again for as long as he exists: that the sound came from him, and that it was not his.

-

They are called forward by number.

He understands this at once - understands every word of it, cleanly and completely, the way he will understand every word said to him from this moment forward, because whatever the shell is has already begun rendering the Caul into sense before he can want it not to - and the understanding brings him nothing. It does not soften the yard, or the line, or what the line is for. A robed figure with a board is working down the ranks, and the line is shortening from one end and lengthening with silence at the other. Each one ahead of him receives a string of words that mean exactly what they say and steps forward, or is walked forward by a hand on the shoulder if it does not step forward on its own, and is led away through a gate he cannot see the far side of.

Then it is his turn, and the sound comes at him, precise, unhurried, the tone of a woman who has said this exact sentence some large number of times before and expects to say it some large number of times again before the day is through.

*"Em One Oh One Triple Eight. Step forward."*

He understands every word of it, whole and immediate, the way he now understands everything said in his hearing - there is no lag in the sense, no fog to push through, only a flat and total comprehension arriving a half-beat ahead of anything in him that could call itself a will. The numbers land first and hardest, because arithmetic needs no permission from the rest of a sentence to mean what it means - and he understands, with a lurch that has nothing left in him to lurch with and does it anyway, that the numbers are his. Not his name. Not Rowe, not Ted, not Soren, not any of the three men he has been to three different sets of people who loved him or served beside him or fed him bread on a Thursday morning. A string of digits assigned to a batch, in a rotation, at a station, on a form he will never read and could not read if he held it in hands that could hold anything. He has been given the one thing in this entire golden impossible transaction that is unambiguously, exclusively his, and it is a number, and he hates it, and his body is already stepping forward before the hating has finished arriving, because refusing was never one of the choices on offer - only which face he wears while he answers.

He is walked through the gate. He does not look back at the line, because the aperture does not turn that way unless he tells it to, and he has not yet learned that he is permitted to tell it anything at all.

-

The barracks give him nothing worth keeping and the drill gives him less, and none of it matters beside the gunship.

He is put aboard it without ceremony, in the manner of a crate being loaded, and finds himself - the finding takes some while, the shell answering his intention consistently a half-beat behind the intention itself, a lag he will spend seven months learning to out-think rather than outrun - seated inside a turret ring on the gunship's flank, designated, he gathers from the numeral stencilled at the housing's base, the thirty-fourth of some greater number of identical rings. Turret #34. He does not know what a gunship of this kind is for beyond the plain evidence of it: engines somewhere forward doing a great deal of work, the deck thrumming with a vibration that reaches him not through skin, which he no longer has, but through the frame itself, a borrowed sensation standing in for the true one the way a man feels a passing train through a platform rail.

The engagement, when it comes, is brief and comes from below: shapes on the ground, moving, a river-crossing crowded with them, and the mechanism built into him - not his mechanism, the shell's, the thing Verlaine wired into the housing and calls a fire-control routine - presents him with a solution. Range. Bearing. A tone in the aperture that means *loose*. He understands the word the instant it lands on him, the way he understands every word now, and understanding does no favours here either; it only means the horror arrives with nothing lost in translation. Underneath the word is the same arithmetic that told him his own number was his own number, offered now toward a different end.

He does not fire.

It is not a decision he arrives at through any process he could afterward describe to a court, if there were a court, if the shell were the kind of thing courts concerned themselves with. It is closer to the shuttle-hand knowing, before the thread has an opinion about it, that the tension wants easing: something in him that predates the number, predates the yard, predates even France, looks at the shapes on the ground and finds that whatever the fire-control routine is prepared to call them, he is not prepared to call them that, and the readiness in the mechanism simply does not travel the last half-beat into his hands. Ted Rowe, who put a bayonet through nothing in four years because the opportunity and the willingness never once arrived together, sits inside a jointed black shell above a river he does not know the name of, and Turret #34 does not fire.

The tone in the aperture changes and asks again. Turret #34 does not fire.

Afterward - the engagement thinning out below, the gunship holding its line, other turrets along the flank having done what his did not - the only sense the shell gives him honestly, the only report that arrives without the half-beat lag, without the flatness, without the sense of being told about his own body by a stranger, is smell. Burnt ozone, sharp and mineral, the firing gear in the housing beside him having cooked itself somewhere in the routine's second and third askings, a smell that reaches him the way the terrier's bark used to reach him on the canal road, immediate and unmistakably real. It is the one true thing his new body will tell him all day, and it is the smell of the machinery's own small failure, not his.

Nothing is done to him for it. Nobody comes to the turret ring to ask Turret #34 why Turret #34 declined the solution offered three times in succession. A different robed figure, further down the ship, makes a note on a board very like the boards from the yard, and the note is brief, and the note is the entire proceeding. He understands this too, eventually, in the barracks, from men further along in their own numbers than he is in his: you do not court-martial a tool. A tool that will not do the work is not a criminal. It is a fault, logged and scheduled, and the schedule has only the one remedy for a fault that repeats.

You do not court-martial a tool.

You scrap it.

[Beat 7]
﻿VERLAINE ENTRY - THE LAW DOESN'T APPLY HERE

The third morning in the passes Lyra woke with her jaw clenched, because the cold had found the gap between her gorget and her chin-strap in the night and taken up residence there. She did not start fires this high. The Vigil line's observation posts ran east as well as north, and whatever her current standing with Verlaine's border authority turned out to be, she did not need to clarify it with a smoke column visible for twenty kilometers.

She stood in the grey early light and counted the day's water. Four containers filled at the last stream crossing - a seep-fed pool two days back, slow and cold, not enough for what she needed but enough for what she'd had. Two containers were empty now. One was down to its last third. The fourth had gone to the Heloth courier's cooling intake, which had priority, because without the courier she was walking the western descent in full plate with the Rod of a seventy-year-old scryer and whatever remained of her field kit.

One container, one-third full. Two days of descent ahead, by Orim's reckoning.

He was already at his courier's side, pressing Catalyst sensitizer into his fingertips with the method she'd now watched twice - palm flat to palm, the preparation absorbed through the skin, a twenty-minute window of amplified membrane perception bought at a price of nausea he never mentioned and she'd noticed anyway.

"Nothing within the Rod's range last night," he said, without looking at her. "Whoever is ahead of us has a different route, or a faster pace."

"Or they crossed the watershed before the snow window closed and they're already down in the valley."

"That too." He wiped his fingertips on his coat. "We have a problem."

"Water."

"The pool at the lower crossing will be dry. Last significant rain in these passes was the snowmelt in the ninth month. Next is usually first snowfall in the eleventh. We are in the tenth." He looked east along the cut of pale rock and winter-dead grass that was the only geography either of them could see. "The drainage on the far side of the watershed divide goes down into Verlaine. There should be water there. In the ninth month. Not in the tenth. The passes have been dry since the melt."

She had known this. She had been building toward knowing it clearly for two days, the knowledge shaped by the containers going lighter and a specific dryness at the back of her throat that a third of a container would not fix. At altitude the air took the moisture out of you before you felt the lack. She had been feeling the lack since yesterday.

"How far to the Verlaine valley floor from the divide?"

"Two days, perhaps, at mountain pace." He looked at the courier. "The machines are adequate. We are less so."

She put on her pack. The courier came to alert state when she laid her hand on its housing - the warm vibration through her palm, the chemical readiness of it. Thirty-six hours of reserve, roughly, at mountain pace. She and Orim had less than that.

They moved.

-

The pass narrowed enough in places that the couriers went single file and Lyra leaned in toward the rock face to keep her pack clear of the outward edge, where the path dropped into the dry drainage below. She looked down once, noted it, and stopped looking.

Orim catalogued things as he walked. This was how she was coming to know him - not in conversation, but in the running commentary of a man who had spent fifty years moving through terrain and had developed the specific habit of noting what he passed. The grooves cut into the rock face to her left were the marks of a rope-guide system, pre-Vigil, eighth or ninth century by the oxidation. Someone had cut handholds and rope-anchors across this face for travelers who needed them. The rope was gone. The grooves remained.

"There was a customs house at the watershed divide, sixth century," he said. "Not Verlaine's - Verlaine didn't exist yet in any form we'd recognize. A local arrangement. You paid a family that had held the pass for four generations, and you crossed, and the record of your crossing went into a notation system nobody outside the pass has been able to read since the ninth century, when the family died and the notation died with them. The Bressant archive still cites the pass-crossing fee as precedent for the current transit charge, and the notation system as its record. The precedent rests on documents no one can verify. Verlaine finds this satisfying."

"Do you know how much the fee was?"

"No one does. That's rather the point."

She filed it - not dismissively, but as data she'd know where to find when she needed it - and kept moving.

-

They reached the watershed divide at midday.

It was marked by a stone cairn, recent, no more than fifty years by the weathering, and by the abrupt change in terrain beyond it: the eastern face pale rock and dead scrub, the western face darker stone, more sheltered, the scrub still brown-dry but with the give of soil that would hold water when the rains finally came. Still dry. The sense of descent ahead. Verlaine somewhere below the cloud-line that lay flat against the valley floor.

She had been watching Orim since the third hour of the morning, watching how he watched the terrain, and how he watched it had changed since they'd passed the last seep-fed hollow four kilometers back. Not alarm. A noting. The Rod was slung at his back and he'd touched it twice in the last two hours without seeming to know he was doing it.

He stopped at the cairn.

She stopped. The couriers stopped behind them, the two machines standing with the patient readiness of things that did not understand why they weren't moving but had no pressing opinion about it either way.

He unslung the Rod.

Forty centimeters of dense dark mineral compound in a plain iron housing, the grip wound in old cord rewound at some point - she could see where the new winding started, a shade off the old wear. Seven grooves cut into the shaft at irregular intervals. He ran his fingers along them without looking, the way a person finds a familiar door handle in the dark. The fourth groove. His thumb stopped there, rotated slightly, moved on.

"What are you looking for?" she said.

"I'm not looking yet. I know what I'm looking for. I'm finding the address."

He wet two fingertips from the small Catalyst vial at his belt, pressed them together at his sternum, took two deliberate breaths. His eyes went somewhere else - not unfocused, differently focused, the way eyes look when they're seeing something that isn't in front of them.

She had seen the full-installation scry at the Ocipheus Station archive: the apparatus crews, the wound held open by thirty years of careful damage to the same site, the membrane worn soft and responsive by decades of the same hands. She had found that comforting, the way she found all controlled methodology comforting - the specificity of it, the predictability. This was nothing like it. This was one person's hands, a portable length of mineral, and fifty years of knowing exactly what to look for.

It lasted perhaps three minutes. His eyes came back.

"There's a Sphere-08 location roughly three hundred meters below us," he said. "Below the rock face to the west. A lake - deep, cold, freshwater. The pressure differential at the membrane boundary is consistent with significant depth." He looked at a natural hollow in the stone to their left, waist-deep, a meter and a half across, open at the top. "I need you to stand behind the courier."

She looked at the hollow. She looked at the Rod. She looked at the hollow again.

"You're going to open the membrane."

"I am going to open the membrane." He crouched at the hollow and pressed the Rod's mineral tip against the stone at its lowest point, where any liquid would collect. "It will hold for perhaps twenty seconds. Possibly thirty. I cannot extend it and I cannot close it early." He glanced back. "The water will be cold. There will be some force behind it. Please stand behind the courier."

"The Liturgy classifies this as -"

"Unauthorized Membrane Perforation, Class Three. Yes." He found the trigger with the flat of his finger. "The Liturgy classifies a great many things. Please stand behind the courier."

She stood behind the courier.

He depressed the trigger.

The membrane answered the way she had only read about in research notation: the stone's own skin rose at the mineral tip, translucent, fleshy at the edges, a window into nothing that was also, somehow, surface. Threaded through it, dark veins fine as hairline cracks in old stone, pulsed once, twice, a third time as pressure built from the far side. She had read descriptions of the material. Reading a description was not the same as watching it try to close and fail - thin, alive, straining to heal the way a cut palm strains to close, and unable to, because the Catalyst was still holding the wound open against its will.

The veins went dark.

The surface bulged.

The surface parted.

For one breath before the water came, there was only the wound itself: gold, warm, weeping at the edges the color of light through honey, the sea that runs between all the worlds pressing at the tear the way blood wells before it runs. Then the lake behind it found the opening and the color was gone, drowned under everything the far side had to give. Water hit the hollow hard enough to splash back against Orim's braced arm, and kept coming - not a trickle, the sustained pressure of a lake with a great deal of depth behind it, told the door was open. Cold water, darker than water should look, the darkness of something with no bottom her eyes could find. The smell arrived with it: wet stone, mineral cold, the specific weight of something that had sat still and pressured in the dark for a very long time, and under it, gone as soon as she noticed it, a warmer, sweeter note that did not belong to any lake.

She counted under her breath.

The hollow filled.

At twenty-two seconds the membrane closed - no drama, no sound, the translucency thinning, the veins fading, the surface sealing the way a wound closes when the pressure comes off. The bulge deflated. The aperture was gone. Water pooled and ran along the rock's natural drainage to a lower shelf, where it pooled again.

Orim stayed crouched, his arm wrapped in his own coat where the water had struck it, looking at the hollow the way a man checks an outcome against an expected result.

She uncapped her first container. The water was cold enough to feel through the metal before it was half full. Her hands ached by the third container. She filled the fourth anyway.

When she'd finished he had moved to the overflow shelf and sat against the stone, drinking from a container she'd filled without being asked. He didn't speak. She topped off the couriers' supplemental intake, which didn't need it but got it anyway, and drank, and felt the cold go all the way down, and felt the altitude headache that had been building since morning begin, after ten minutes or so, to loosen.

The Rod's mineral tip, wrapped in his coat, was radiating heat she could see even through the cloth - not dramatically, but the fabric lay differently over it than it would over something merely cold.

"You've done this before," she said.

"I know which lakes are deep."

He drank again and didn't say anything further.

She let the silence have the time it was owed. A researcher asks a question and gets data. The data was: he had done this before; he knew which Spheres were which; he knew exactly what a deep lake's pressure differential produced against a membrane; and he had known all of it for fifty years. This was not the first time. It was also, from the precise way he'd phrased it - *I know which lakes are deep*, not *I have done this* - probably not the first time he'd been asked.

"Thank you," she said.

"We'll need it before the valley floor." He stood, rewrapped the Rod, and resettled it across his back with the care of a man handling something that had cost him to use. Not regret. Accounting. "The descent begins past the cairn."

-

What changed on the way down was the air first - growing heavier with every hour of lost altitude, the specific thinness that had sat on her chest since the first day in the passes finally unwinding. The dry smell of altitude gave way to something wetter and darker: old soil, cold vegetable matter, and somewhere below it, fog lying against the lowlands. The rock went from pale limestone to grey-green with lichen in the sheltered folds. Silverthorn hedges along the upper field margins caught the fog and made a low sound in the wind, not quite a tone, more a resonance felt in the sternum before it reached the ear. The first roofs she saw were thatched in the Verlaine high-country style - alternating rows, staggered for drainage, not the single-direction lay she knew from Ocipheus. Smoke from one chimney. A cow in a yard. A child who looked at the Heloth couriers from the gate and went inside without looking at the riders.

By the second afternoon the passes had given way to border scrubland, and the border scrubland to wine country - three centuries of cut hillside, terrace stepped back from terrace behind retaining walls of fitted limestone the deepvine roots had grown through until stone and root held each other up. The vines ran in rows along every terrace, autumn foliage a Sphere-import purple-black striped with bands of native yellow where the transplant hadn't taken, a hillside legible as a record of how far the variety had spread in each generation.

She had spent the descent studying the western border the way a researcher studies a problem - systematically, without preference for the answer. The formal crossing was a place called Pontecote: two hundred meters of checkpoint wall, a flag tower, a garrison of fifteen Verlaine border soldiers whose job was processing cattle traffic out of the Loera hill-farms and turning back anyone without the right papers.

She didn't have the right papers. She didn't have the right authority. She had a sealed letter from the Ocipheus Keeper that the Canon had already neutralized, a dissolution blade she hadn't drawn, and a Heloth courier running thirty-six hours into its chemical reserves and starting to show it in a marginal unevenness on the left-side gait.

Pontecote was not where she crossed.

The goat path ran three kilometers north of it, through scrubland Verlaine hadn't bothered to checkpoint because nothing of value had ever come through it except goats. Goats didn't need transit papers. Neither, for the next forty seconds, would she.

The ground was damp from two days of rain and the courier's load-stability protocol kept recalculating for terrain that wasn't difficult so much as irregular - a slight hesitation in the gait she'd learned to read as the machine thinking. She didn't push it. At the path's highest point the Verlaine border was a low stone wall, waist-high, the kind that says *this is where the jurisdiction changes*, not the kind that says *we will stop you*. She took it at a step-through. The courier followed with the particular patience of a machine that had no opinion about property lines.

On the far side, she was in Verlaine without authorization.

Her body registered it the way it registered other practical facts - the weight of armor in the third hour of wearing it, the taste of the air before rain. No guilt. Just the information that the protections she operated under at home - the compact authority that made another House's soldiers hesitate before engaging a Knight, thirty years of assuming the Liturgy's sanction made her harder to stop - did not hold here. She was a large woman with a dissolution blade on a tired Heloth courier, in Verlaine without papers, and the only thing between her and a formal response from Verlaine's border cavalry was whatever speed she could make before the observation-post network noticed her.

She had, by her own estimate, until the first post sighted her.

That was now.

-

The signal mirror caught the morning light - a flash, directional, down into the valley - at the exact moment she saw the post: eleven meters up on the fourth terrace, a box of cut stone with a slit-window and a mirror housing on the south face. The flash was instantaneous with her recognizing it. The watcher had been waiting. Not for her specifically - for anyone who crossed at the goat path, which people apparently did, because otherwise Verlaine wouldn't have bothered posting a relay to cover it.

She put the courier to the climb protocol.

The next post triggered as she passed it. Then the one after that. She watched the mirrors flash in relay without slowing, the alert outrunning her. The cavalry, when it came, was faster than she had estimated.

They came around the upper switchback three terraces above - not mounted in the conventional sense. Heloth freight models, three meters at the shoulder, riders sitting high on the shoulder housings with a clean sightline over the terrace walls in both directions. A practical adaptation: freight models could take the terrace approaches that horse cavalry couldn't, and the shoulder-riders were high enough to fire over obstacles that would have blocked a ground engagement. She had read this in Verlaine's deployment literature. There was a gap, it turned out, between reading about something and watching three freight Heloths round a switchback at a pace that said the relay had reached the depot forty minutes ago and they'd been moving since.

"Pontis," she said. Not to anyone. To herself, as a decision.

The Pontis barge landing was half a kilometer below, and a river barge was faster over this ground and this season than anything Verlaine could put on the bank roads - she knew the river-speed tables in the Gyldir Shore Accord's commercial freight section by heart. If she made the barge, the cavalry would have to coordinate a landing intercept downriver, which meant that landing would be alerted before she arrived, which meant she would arrive to Verlaine border authority with a stamped log and no transit documentation. Which was the position she was in regardless.

"There is," Orim said from behind her, not slowing, "a treaty that technically obliges them to let us pass unmolested. The Year 831 agreement predates the suspension mechanism by roughly four hundred years."

"Does anyone back there know that?"

"Almost certainly not. It rarely helps to be correct about a document no one present has read."

She filed that too, the way she'd learned to file most things Orim told her: information that would have bought something ten minutes ago, delivered now with the same unhurried precision he gave everything, as if the shape of the sentence mattered more to him than the moment it landed in.

She put the courier to the descent.

The switchback path wasn't built for courier speed. The machine ran its own gait protocols rather than taking instruction, doing what it was built to do - getting a difficult load down difficult terrain without losing it - and what it was built to do was not comfortable for the load. She held the grip bar across her chest with both hands, kept her weight low, and watched the valley come up.

The formal challenge came from behind her - one rider, one shout, Verlaine border protocol requiring a single challenge before pursuit engaged in earnest. She didn't answer it. She wasn't required to stop for a challenge she had the standing to ignore, and whether the cavalry commander had been briefed on that standing was something she would not learn until she was either on the barge or not.

She was on the barge.

-

The Pontis barge operator had the look of a man who'd spent a career reading commercial river traffic and was actively revising his morning's assessment of it. He examined the sealed authority with the care of someone encountering it for the first time, stamped it, and did not ask about the cavalry visible on the upper switchback, which was professional of him.

The courier folded into rest-state in the cargo space, steam pressure managed, reserves on standby. She paid the crossing fee. The barge pushed off.

Behind her, on the Pontis bank, the cavalry reached the landing a beat too late. One of the shoulder-riders had a signal mirror. The relay went north, toward the Bressant landing, which would have a full description of her before the barge did.

She went into the cargo space and sat beside the courier's warm housing.

-

Verlaine river-country in autumn had a quality she hadn't anticipated, which was density - not natural density, the density of a landscape under continuous documentation. Every village on the Veil River had an archival post. Every archival post had a flag. Green for commercial cargo, yellow for passenger crossings, red for anything moving under special authority. As the barge went north the red flags went up at each post in turn, the change taking roughly thirty seconds from sighting to notation to raised flag. She was being logged in real time by an administrative apparatus with no authority to stop her and no intention of letting her passage go unrecorded.

Orim came to sit beside her at the cargo hatch in the third hour, having spent the crossing on the forward deck doing the thing he did when he was thinking - looking at nothing in particular with forty years of practice at finding something there.

"You're counting the flags," he said.

"Fourteen since Pontis."

"There's a cluster at the Archival Gate. That's the border town - historically significant, three posts, they all flag independently." He looked at the passing bank. "Named for Arnault d'Archival, who crossed this river in Year 312 in pursuit of a Calyx raiding party, planted his House mark on the far bank, and declared the river the border. The Calyx disagreed. The river disagreed the following spring by flooding and moving the bank fifteen meters. D'Archival's mark is now on the wrong side of the water it was meant to define. Verlaine still charges crossing tolls off his original notation of where the mark stood. The mark is underwater. The notation remains."

She had known the Accord. She had not known d'Archival. "You've been on this river before."

"Four times. Three verification contracts and once in my own interest." He paused. "The Treachery Quarter, in Bressant - the old merchant district - is called that for the Year 634 occupation. Verlaine merchants sold supply routes to Ocipheus forces during the occupation, then sold the same merchants' names to the Verlaine resistance once the occupation failed. It's been Treachery Quarter for six hundred years. They're proud of it." He glanced at her. "This is a country that takes its record-keeping seriously. The record matters here more than the fact. Bear that in mind."

"What are they watching for," she said, "if not us specifically?"

"Whether we are who the documents say we are. The documents say we are a Compact pursuit under Ocipheus authority. The documents are correct. What isn't in the documents is that the authority was suspended by the same institution that issued it." He nodded, once, and went back to looking at nothing.

She remained at the hatch and watched Verlaine document her passage through its territory without her permission, which she had entered without Verlaine's permission, which seemed, on the whole, fair.

-

Bressant arrived in the early afternoon - a dense city, accumulated around a function, in this case the grain warehouse district handling Verlaine's upriver agricultural output. The buildings pressed together against the hillside behind, leaving the landing frontage wide and practical. The Veil ran sixty meters across here, swift and dark with autumn rain-depth.

The Bressant landing's flag was red.

Two border soldiers waited at the landing approach - young, formal, doing this correctly because they knew it would be examined later. She showed the sealed authority. They logged her in with the precision of people who intended to do it right if it took all day.

"You have approximately forty minutes," the senior of the two said, "before interior command can coordinate a formal review response." He said it the way people say useful things within the limits of what they're authorized to say.

"Thank you," she said.

The courier came off the barge and went to active protocol. She had forty minutes.

-

The route into the warehouse district ran her past the cathedral, and the route past the cathedral ran her past the intake station.

It occupied what had once been a canonical administrative annex - stone-walled, high-windowed, the vaulted ceiling the Liturgy favored when it needed functional space adjacent to ecclesiastical authority, clerestory light falling onto a work floor that whoever had repurposed it for Verlaine's conscription operations had not bothered to redesign. They had simply put trestle tables under the arches and begun filing.

She rode past slowly enough to see through the open door.

A Verlaine intake officer sat at the central table with a ledger and an ink register, working a stack of documents with the unhurried efficiency of someone three weeks into the same task. Beside the ledger, a stamp for the designation block - the M-prefix and intake serial, applied to every conscript document the instant the notation was complete. Beside the stamp, three completed files in a wooden box, corners aligned, waiting for a clerk to carry them to the garrison archive.

Three people had been through that table this morning.

Against the left wall, a rack: five bolt-action rifles, clean and maintained. A sixth space, empty - the sixth rifle presumably on the officer's back, or in the anteroom. Same manufacture as the checkpoint guards' equipment, she noted, and kept riding.

She could not read the files in the box from the road. She did not need to. She had handled enough Compact liaison paper to recognize the stock and the fold - Verlaine military intake, standard format - and she had known, administratively, what that format meant since she was twenty-two, which was when she had first encountered the phrase *M-intake authorization, Sphere-origin conscript, Year-stamp* in a coordination brief and filed it, correctly, as terminology. The form designation was what she had encountered. The three files in the wooden box were what the form designation meant, and the distance between those two facts was not one she examined for long, because there was no room in forty minutes for it.

The cathedral's north face rose to her left, Verlaine's canonical structure built for throughput where Ocipheus's was built for vigil - records entered and exited, people processed, the high windows sized for reading documents correctly rather than for sitting with them for thirty years.

She found the road that ran along the grain district's south face and took it.

-

The southern approach was a narrow passage between two storage buildings, stone-floored, sheltered from the wind. She walked it slowly.

Three sets of boot-prints in the settled grit along the wall, datable roughly by layer depth - the deepest three or four days old, overlaid by two days of weather. All three had been here three days back. The most recent layer showed two sets. The third was gone.

The third print was lighter than the other two: narrower heel, longer toe-box. Not Caul-standard equipment.

Someone had separated from the party between the Vigil line crossing and Bressant. Two had continued north. The third was somewhere south, or east, or simply not continuing on this route. She checked the direction of the remaining pair. North. The river barges running north out of Bressant served the Atrament rail junction at the border. She already knew where that led.

One of the three who had taken the Relic was no longer where she needed them to be. She filed it and kept moving, because the more useful question now was whether the third had gone south, or had stayed.

The lighter prints in the passage oriented toward the entrance - not a departure posture, weight forward, toward the opening, the angle of someone watching for arrivals rather than preparing to leave. Someone had waited here for hours. The most recent trace showed they had not yet walked away.

She checked her time. Twenty minutes.

The main warehouse access ran along the district's north side - loading bays, freight doors, the formal commercial entrance. She didn't use it. She went around to the service door on the east face, the one the loading crews used and the supervisors didn't watch in the middle of the day, because the middle of the day was when the loading crews were outside working the barge transfers.

Inside: the smell of grain, warm and dusty, the particular smell of a great deal of dry material in an enclosed space. The floor ran in sections, sealed bins in long rows under the main span, a smaller room at the back for the premium contract lots. She moved through without hurrying, reading the bins as she passed. Standard rope seals, the kind the Bressant archive system required. All intact.

She reached the fourth row.

On the third bin from the left wall, the rope seal was broken - not cut. Worked loose from the peg, carefully, the kind of careful that took time and left the rope itself intact enough to be pushed back into approximate position. It would have read as sealed to anyone who wasn't examining every seal in the room. She had been.

She stood in front of the bin. She had had forty minutes when she'd entered the district. She had perhaps fifteen now.

She put her hand on the latch.

[Beat 8]
﻿NO ACTION INDICATED

The copy desk faced north, which in winter meant it faced a column of pale light off the courtyard stones that produced no warmth and did nothing useful except confirm the time of day. She had spent three years at various desks in Liturgy copy offices and they all faced somewhere inconvenient; the canonical administration had been built for permanence and ceremony, not for the people who worked in it.
