This room was a high-ceilinged box that held the cold the way stone did, two windows on the north face, one door, a row of shelves built for an old filing order that someone had abandoned before her posting and replaced with a different one she was still learning. Six weeks. She had spent the first three learning the lamp angle and the second three doing it automatically, the way a person learns to walk with a bad leg: efficiently, without grace, without complaint.

Her right shoulder ached by the second hour every day. The middle knuckle on her right hand stiffened by mid-morning and had to be pressed back sharply against the joint - one click, release, done - before she could reload the nib and continue. These were not complaints. They were conditions. She had been a Scribe for three years and she had learned the difference.

The instruments arrived at the third bell, as they always did. The runner - a junior canon, fourteen years old, still wearing the expression of someone who had not yet learned what the documents he carried said - set the stack on the corner of her desk with the particular care of a boy who had been told the documents were important without being told what that meant. In the first weeks of the posting he had asked, out of some trained sociable reflex, whether she required anything else. He had stopped asking in the third week, in the way a person stops asking a question once they understand, without being told, that the answer will be no and that asking it is a small performance neither party has the energy for. She had not asked him why. She was fairly confident she knew.

Six weeks was enough time to learn what a stack of instruments contained by looking at it: the weight, the binding method, whether the lead sheet was written in the Canon's hand or a secretary's. The lead sheet this morning was in the Canon's hand. She had learned, by accumulation rather than by being told, what that meant - not that an office had processed the instruments below, but that they had been reviewed, personally, at the highest level before they reached her desk. She noted the stack's weight: heavier than yesterday's. In the first weeks of the posting she had read every instrument before she began copying; now she read them in order, one at a time, completing each before she touched the next. Accumulation was the danger. Read everything at once and she would begin to understand the shape of it before she had language for the shape, and then the act of copying would start to feel like something other than what it was supposed to be. She set the notation sheet aside and started with the second instrument.

---

The Loera document was formal, carefully constructed, written by someone who understood he was arguing upward through an institution that did not share his interests. She recognized the structure: the long diplomatic preamble, the supporting observations arranged in descending order of provability, the careful selection of words meant to make the argument easier to accept. The delegate had written *deterioration* where another writer might have written *encroachment.* Encroachment implied an actor; deterioration implied a process. Processes could be managed. Actors had to be confronted. She had seen this before in inter-House correspondence - the gradual reduction of a request to its irreducible minimum, the careful trimming of everything that gave the Liturgy grounds to refuse. The delegate asked only for acknowledgment: a record that someone in the Liturgy had read the report and noted that the western boundary of the Wall showed drift over an indeterminate but non-negligible period. Not action. Not troops. Not resources. His closing paragraph read almost apologetic in its narrowness, as though he had written a larger version and then removed everything that might be refused. What remained after enough trimming was sometimes not worth refusing.

The Liturgy refused it anyway.

She loaded the nib and copied the document accurately. Copying was not reading, not exactly - not the way she had read before certification, when a document's content lived in her head for the rest of the day. Copying was a different discipline. The instrument passed through her hand and became official, and she retained the shape of it rather than the substance, the way a person retains the turns of a road walked once without retaining the specific stones. Her master had noted it in the certification assessment: *exact hand, no interpretive drift, consistent performance across instrument type.* She had thought at the time this was a compliment. She was less certain what it was now. The instrument said what the instrument said; she said it again, in the canonical hand, under the Liturgy seal, and it became official in the saying of it. That was the arrangement.

She finished the Loera copy and set it to dry.

The Carnyx document had a different texture, and she knew it before she had read three lines. Where the Loera document had the softness of a constructed argument, the Carnyx document had the density of a report. The border commander was not asking for anything except to have what he had observed placed in the record. He had been to the eastern sector himself. He had brought a methodology section and a set of survey maps folded into the back cover, and she had to clear the auxiliary shelf to spread them properly - the paper was good quality and resisted the crease, the kind ordered by someone in the eastern garrison who understood this document needed to still be readable in thirty years, when someone wanted to prove they had received the report and done nothing with it. She weighted the corners with the inkwell, the lamp, and the stone she kept for the purpose, and the maps covered the desk and part of the shelf besides.

The field notation was unfamiliar. She read the legend twice, then matched the annotation types against the survey points - three of them, along the observed boundary, each carrying its own measurement method and margin of error. She added the margins against the measurements. The margins did not account for the difference. Fifteen years of documented observation, not drift, not measurement error: three separate points confirming each other. The Wall had moved. She measured with her thumb against the boundary line at the earlier survey date and then against the current one. On a map at this scale the distance was a thumbnail's width. She had a precise thumbnail, trained to the canon's standard line width in a discipline that required precision, and what it told her was not the same thing as a small distance.

She had located, in the archive's methodological standards register, the authority the counter-brief would cite:

*Assessment Protocol for Membrane Boundary Variation, Revision Four, Article Twelve, Year 1199 revision: A boundary variation falling within one standard deviation of the fifty-year mean, as calculated by the Liturgy's approved archive methodology, constitutes acceptable variation. Observation is required. Response is at the Canon's discretion.*

The commander had measured fifteen years. The Protocol required a fifty-year mean. The standard deviation of that mean, by the Liturgy's own methodology, absorbed fifteen years of change without approaching the threshold. This was not an error in the Protocol. It was what the Protocol had been built to do.

At the end of the data block the commander had added a rate projection: *high-uncertainty, extrapolated from 15-year sample.* If the observed rate held, the boundary would reach the first civilian settlement outside the exclusion zone in approximately forty years. He had put it there knowing it would be read by a Scribe before it reached anyone with the standing to act on it. He had done what he could, which was to get it into the record, because the record was where things went to matter or be buried.

She was twenty-six. In forty years she would be sixty-six, if she lived, which she probably would, and the settlement outside the eastern exclusion zone would be inside it. She looked at the number for a long time before she began copying.

She copied it accurately and folded the maps carefully - along the existing creases, not new ones, the paper was too good for new creases - and held them a moment before she put them in the review sleeve. The survey points. The margin of error at each. The specific weight of *high-uncertainty* written by a man who understood exactly how certain he was. She sealed the sleeve and set it on the transfer stack.

---

The counter-brief arrived while she was finishing. The runner set it on the corner of the desk and was gone before she could look up; she heard the door click behind him. She had not heard him come in. She finished the last line of the Carnyx copy, set it to dry, and picked up the counter-brief.

Three paragraphs. The Canon's characteristic cadence - institutional and polished, nothing that could be cited against him in a subsequent review, every sentence defensible in isolation. The Loera sector conditions fell within acceptable variation; sector commanders were reminded that local observational data should be interpreted against the long-term baseline record, which was the Liturgy's to maintain. The eastern sector's survey methodology was noted as non-standard, and its findings would require reconciliation with the Liturgy's established baseline before any action threshold could be assessed. The Canon did not say what the action thresholds were. She had not seen them specified in any instrument she had copied in six weeks.

The final line: *No action indicated at this time.*

She had been writing this line for three years. She had written it for sector correspondence and maintenance requests and inter-House petitions and resource-allocation appeals and land-dispute referrals and medical-supply authorizations from the Wall rotation posts. She had written it for garrison-strength reviews and water-quality reports and census tabulations from the outer zones. She had written it so many times that she had developed the particular caution of not writing it until she was in the disposition field, the way a person learns not to write a word she has been writing all day in the wrong place.

She applied the notation to both the Loera and Carnyx instruments. She folded the counter-brief and added it to the Loera file, and set the sealed sleeve on the transfer stack. It would go to the archive at the end of the day, catalogued under *Review Deferred: Sector Correspondence,* filed between a supply reassessment from three months ago and a quarterly water-usage report from the eastern garrison. Nobody would look at it again until someone needed to prove they had read it. That was what it would be for.

The stack was higher than yesterday's. Tomorrow it would be higher than today's.

---

The WC-Eirene units came through the courtyard in the middle of the afternoon.

She had moved to the window to stretch her hand - the middle knuckle had stiffened again, earlier than usual - when she heard them before she saw them: iron in deliberate motion on stone, a low consistent percussion, lower than the Heloths on the city roads and slower and in a different register altogether. The gait of something heavier. Something not built for speed.

The first unit came through the gate, and she counted as the rest followed. Twelve. Taller than a tall man, iron housings built wide and low at the shoulder for impact absorption rather than deflection, the tracking assembly that served as a head set lower than a man's head and canted slightly forward, rotating through the slow deliberate arc she recognized from the evaluation summaries: active acquisition, registering the space around it. The handlers walked alongside rather than ahead - she had filed enough summaries to know why. A handler in an active unit's forward registration field created a compliance conflict the unit was built to resolve by following rather than being followed. Walking alongside was correct procedure. Moving at maintenance speed - not operational pace, not inert - was correct procedure. Twelve units crossing a courtyard in file, led by handlers who were doing everything by the book, was not, on its own, alarming. She noted it with the same reflex she noted instrument volume and lead-sheet weight and the Canon's cadence. She noted everything. That was the office.

The volume had tripled in six weeks. She had filed the first evaluation summary under a provisional designation, origin notation sphere-42; by the third summary the provisional flag had been dropped. She had filed seven since. She had not been tracking the increase against anything, because tracking accumulations against each other was not within her assignment.

The units turned at the loading-dock approach and moved toward the south utility wing, handlers holding their sidewalk positions through the turn. She watched until the last unit cleared the corner and the courtyard stood empty again except for the food-service cart and, at the far end, a Heloth courier idling in the shade with the particular stillness of a machine that was waiting rather than switched off.

She knew the south utility wing. She had been fourteen years old when the lock authorization for its staging hall came through the copy desk where she was doing her junior apprentice hours. A senior Scribe had handed it to her because it was a straightforward maintenance lock - an instrument that required documentation but not judgment, the appropriate kind for an apprentice who was still learning the judgment part. She had copied it in the hand she was still correcting then, the one that pressed too hard on the downstrokes because she had not yet learned to let the weight do the work, and she had filed it and not thought about it again. The authorization specified no end date, which was unusual. She had not known then what unusual looked like. That had been eleven years ago.

The staging hall had not appeared in any instrument she had copied in six weeks.

It was not locked now.

She returned to her desk and made no annotation. The courtyard was not an instrument category she had been given to work from. She finished the maintenance authorization for the south utility wing - expanded storage calibration, lower west section, the fourth of the week, the fifth if she counted last Thursday's, which she did, because she counted everything - and added it to the stack.

---

She went to the registry room in the late afternoon, when the light in the main office had gone gray enough that she would need to angle the lamp again and she was tired of angling the lamp.

She had a legitimate purpose, or told herself so going in. Her sister's name had appeared in thirty-one instruments she had processed in six weeks. Each was individually within the scope of her assignment; together they mapped to an operational structure she had been copying without being given the shape of it. Understanding the structure was not intervention. Knowing what she was recording was what let her record it accurately. She was a Scribe. Accuracy was the virtue she had been certified to practice. She signed the access log with her name, the time, and *cross-reference, canonical processing* in the purpose field, and went to find the Ocipheus muster.

The Templar deployment registry was a bound volume, thick-spined, the pages dense with the specific notation of active service records - house of origin, posting date, current station, active assignment, any suspended authorities. Not House soldiers, not conscripts: the Liturgy's own field corps, the ones who had chosen the institutional mission over every other claim on them. Each entry carried a name and a rank. The Liturgy did not reduce its Templars to a number, whatever it did to its machines; it kept that distinction, formally, whatever else it failed to keep. She had filed volumes like this in her apprentice years without reading them, and the filing had taught her the structure well enough to move through it now. The room smelled of old ink and the cold that comes off stone kept deliberately cold - a different quality of cold than the main office's, older, the smell of documents sealed and opened and resealed many times over. She found the Ocipheus section and turned the pages carefully, using the flat of her finger rather than the tip; the paper had the dryness of decades in an unheated room.

She had known the name the instant she saw it, six weeks ago, before the ink under her own hand had finished drying. What she had not had - what she had come to this room to find - was the shape the name fit inside: the weight of thirty-one instruments in the same authorization chain, the structure with Ocipheus at one end and the Canon at the other and, in between, a suspended transit authority that none of the instruments she had copied troubled itself to explain. She had been carrying the name for six weeks the way a Scribe carries the shape of an instrument before she has finished reading it - present, felt, refusing to resolve into language - and she had allowed herself to keep processing accurately rather than following the shape to wherever it led. That had been a choice. She was looking now at what the choice had produced.

She found the entry.

*Dame Lyra, Ocipheus. Templar. Current status: deployment, active. Transit authority suspended, Canon's instrument -* and the reference number she had copied and filed and cross-referenced and cited in three subsequent instruments. She had handled that number thirty-one times.

Posting date, three years prior - the same cycle in which she herself had been certified and taken her first desk. They had been in different cities since. They had written letters. Her sister had told her, every time she visited Ocipheus Station, that the station bread was not worth eating, and Vega had made not listening a habit in return, the way a person does not follow advice that comes from someone who loves her enough to have an opinion about her breakfast.

She had been at this desk eating a roll of that same bread the morning the transit suspension arrived, six weeks ago. The runner had brought three instruments. She had read her sister's name before her hand had finished the second line of the copy, and she had put the roll down for a reason she had not let herself name at the time. The suspension was the second instrument. She processed it anyway, in under an hour, sealed the canonical original, filed it with her working copy, and moved to the third instrument, because that was the discipline and the discipline was what she had.

She had known the whole time. She had copied it anyway.

She stood in the registry room a long time, long enough to notice how cold it was and how still she had gone in it, both hands gripping the spine of the volume, her right middle knuckle pressed against the binding without her having decided to press it. She released it. She did not find having known, and having copied it anyway, any more consoling than not-knowing would have been. She replaced the volume. She had made no annotations. Her name was in the access log. She returned to the copy office.

---

The instruments she had copied that day were dry. She aligned them and moved them to the transfer stack; the runner would collect them in the morning and carry them to the sealed archive, where they would become canonical and unalterable, the official record of what had been decided. She would not see most of them again.

She kept working copies. Every Scribe did - not canonical, not official, nothing the Liturgy needed to acknowledge or could be held accountable for, just her own hand, her own reference, in the box in the lower left drawer. It was heavier than it had been at the start of the posting.

The transit suspension from six weeks ago sat near the bottom. She found it by the texture of the page - she had pressed harder than usual that morning, the nib had been cold, the lines ran slightly heavier than her standard hand. She had not noticed at the time. She noticed now.

She set it on the desk and picked up her pen. She did not know why, exactly, except that her hands had been doing something all day - copying, measuring, folding, unsealing, resealing - and hands accustomed to doing something reach for what is nearest when the day runs out. She wrote her sister's name in the margin, in the space between the header and the body text, in the larger hand she had used before the canonical script and the certification, the one she still used in letters: a little more room in the vowels, the first letter held a beat longer.

She wrote the name.

Then she drew a line through it.

This did not remove it. The name stayed under the line, the ink only slightly darker where the stroke crossed the letters, the letters still in the shape she had known since she was old enough to know her sister's name - because a line drawn through ink does not take away what is underneath. It only says that someone drew a line through it. She could see it. She would always be able to see it.

She put the working copy back in the box and closed the drawer.

Outside the window the courtyard was empty. The Heloth was gone. Whatever waited in the south utility wing's staging hall was in it now, unlocked for the first time in eleven years, delivered by handlers following correct procedure. In the morning the runner would come, and she would know something about the day's business by the weight of the stack before she read the lead sheet, because six weeks had trained the reflex, and the volume had gone on increasing at a rate she had not been tracking against anything, because tracking accumulations against each other was not within her assignment.

The Carnyx maps were in the review sleeve on the transfer stack, bound for a shelf between a supply reassessment and a water-usage report, filed until someone needed to prove the Liturgy had read them. In her own head the survey points stayed where she had put them: three positions along the eastern boundary, the margin of error at each, the line at both dates, the rate projection in the commander's careful field hand. *High-uncertainty, extrapolated from 15-year sample.* Forty years.

She was the record.

She had understood this in the abstract at the start of the posting, when a senior Scribe explained what canonical notarization was and what it meant to make a thing official: the instrument said what the instrument said, you said it again in the canonical hand, and it became true in the saying of it. That was the arrangement. That was the whole of the office.

She was understanding it differently now. There were things she had said in the canonical hand that she was carrying in her own head alongside the things she was authorized to carry, and neither set was separable from her body, and her body would go on existing after the transfer stack had been sealed into the archive and made official and unreachable. The Carnyx commander had put his projection in the record because the record was where things went to matter or be buried, and he had done what he could. She had done what she was authorized to do: copy it accurately and send it to be buried. The measurements lived in her head now, beside her sister's name under a line that had not erased it. Neither had a canonical form. The archive kept no instrument class for them, no disposition field, no space to enter the four words she had been writing for three years.

The institution had built the arrangement with that in mind, she thought - a Scribe who would be furniture, who would remember nothing she was not told to remember. It did not seem to have considered what happened when the furniture began to understand what it was recording. Or it had considered this, and the arrangement was working exactly as designed.

She did not know which of those was worse.

She angled the lamp for the last time that day and covered the ink.

[Beat 9]
﻿SPHERE 31 - ROWE FOUND

The warehouse district of Bressant sat where three things met: the Veil River's low north bank, the road down from the eastern passes, and three hundred years of grain dust settling into the mud between them. The buildings were timber-and-stone, kept up the way a working district keeps up things it takes no pride in beyond function. Rope lines of cargo tags ran pole to pole between the rows, and half of them had gone soft in the damp and were illegible, and nobody had replaced them, because the carters knew what they were loading and a tag was a bureaucratic courtesy Bressant tolerated only as far as it had to. It was the kind of place that had learned, under thirty years of Verlaine mobilization, not to ask questions about who moved through it before dawn.

Orim found a wall that suited him. North-facing, always, when he was scrying for something that had come south to north; he had never found a satisfactory reason why the orientation helped and had stopped needing one forty years ago.

He took the tincture vial from his breast pocket, unstopped it by feel, touched two fingers to the opening, and counted to three. Not more than three. That lesson had cost him a week of nausea before he was thirty, and he had never needed to relearn it. He pressed his fingertips to his sternum, breathed twice, slow and even, and looked at nothing.

What came back was the trace of the Relic - four days old, thinning, a warmth like light through honey - running north through the market quarter, the tannery walk, the small goods brokers' floor, to the second barge dock, where it went cold and discontinuous. A transfer, or interference, or both. He could not see past it. He came back south along the same line, hunting the place it had diverged, and found it at the grain-storage cluster at the road's end, where the warmth had turned off the main track and stopped.

He held the second thing he found there longer than he needed to.

It was not the Relic. It was residue laid into the caul itself, and it was old - the accumulated kind, the kind that took months to lay down, not days - and it was wrong in a way he took a moment to name even to himself. Verlaine's standing units carried residue too; every rifleman in the muster yard was a mind Scried out of somewhere and pressed into a shell, and the caul noted each of them once and then, within a year, stopped noting them at all, because a mind that has been made to stop remembering hard leaves nothing behind to find. This residue had not stopped. It sat in the grain-storage cluster the way a wound sits under a bandage that never quite closes - raw, present, still being pressed on from the inside.

He opened his eyes.

Lyra was beside him, watching the warehouses, and had been standing without moving since he'd begun, which was the correct thing to do and which she had never once needed telling.

"The Relic passed through. Four days. The trace ends at the second dock - they took a commercial barge north." He looked at the grain cluster. "There's something else in that district. Old residue. Not the standing-army kind. Those go quiet within the year - they stop remembering hard enough to leave a mark. This one hasn't stopped."

Lyra looked at the warehouse. "A person."

"A mind." He put the vial away. "Whatever's wearing it forgot to let him forget."

She was already moving toward the warehouse.

-

The door at the north end had a duty lock, a rotating iron bar, and the bar was not thrown. She read this as she read most things: the person inside had not locked it, because locking it from within meant crossing the floor and reaching through a latch gap, and a locked door in a working district drew exactly the kind of attention an unlocked one did not. She lifted the bar and went in.

Grain smell, warm and dusty. High ceiling; grey morning light through gap-boards, falling in strips across the tops of thirty-six oak bins and leaving the lower half of the room in shadow. She walked the eastern row, reading the floor - dust undisturbed on the west side, disturbed and then swept, once, on the east, the sweeping concentrated around the last four bins. Someone had wanted to reduce the record of himself. He had not done it perfectly. Nobody ever did.

She stopped at the last bin.

The hinges had been oiled recently - she could see the gloss of it on the iron, the specific shine of a job done days ago rather than years ago. Someone had cared whether these hinges made a sound when they moved. She put her hand flat on the lid, held it there, and lifted the latch.

The smell that came up was not grain. Underneath the grain was something mineral and faint, a smell she had catalogued exactly once, two years past, in a cross-reference exercise she had not expected to need again: the ozone-tang of overworked firing gear, old and cold now, baked into whatever it touched and never quite airing out. She had read the phrase *residual discharge signature, Verlaine military chassis* in a footnote and moved on. She did not move on from it now.

She waited three seconds, because in three seconds anyone startled in a closed dark space makes a sound. What she heard instead was a small, even ticking - the sound of something cooling by increments, deliberately, the way a held breath goes out in measured units instead of all at once. It was not breath. There was no chest behind it to rise. But it kept the rhythm of one, the way a hand keeps time to a song it can no longer hear.

He was in the back left corner, knees drawn, shoulders against the bin wall, a bolt-action rifle across his lap with both hands on it. Not raised. Present. Black, jointed plate, riveted at shoulder and elbow like a beetle's carapace, built small - small enough that his knees, drawn up, still cleared the lid by a hand's width. Where a face should have been there was a single oval receptor, dark, unlit, giving nothing back.

He had known she was there before the latch. He had heard her stop, the pause before her hand found the lid. He had decided, in that pause, to hold still.

She had thirty years in the Order and the thing she trusted first was her own body's read, before her mind sorted anything into categories. Her body said: contained. Watchful. Not going to move first. The specific quality of something that had learned, somewhere, that moving first was how you ended up scrapped.

She moved her hand away from her belt, deliberately, and held it open at her side.

The receptor tracked the movement. Not a face to read - no brow, no set of a jaw, nothing that told her what a person behind it was deciding. Only the small mechanical adjustment of the lens, following her hand and then her own eyes, or the place her eyes would have been if he'd had a way of knowing where a face was on someone he could not read either.

She went down on one knee. Slowly - fast movements in small dark spaces were the wrong choice - and with her hand still open, still away from the blade. Kneeling, she was shorter than him by the only measure available: she had three infusions and a Templar's height and she had come into his hiding place uninvited, and the one thing she could hand back was the difference in scale. So she handed it back.

She extended her palm. Fingers curled inward. The gesture that meant *come.*

He did not come. He did not refuse. He read the whole of it - the hand, the blade still sheathed, the fact that she had come alone, the lid she was holding open with her other arm - the way a careful man reads an unfamiliar mechanism before he touches it. He looked at the rifle in his lap. He looked at her hand. He looked at the lid, and held his attention there a moment longer than anywhere else, weighing it: was the open lid an exit, or was it something she could bring down on him the instant he stood.

The cold ran down the inside of her raised arm. She waited.

Then something in the angle of him changed - not relaxing, not trust, but arrival, the shape a decision makes once it has been made - and he set the rifle down against the bin wall, both hands visible, and stood.

Standing, he was a head shorter than her. He held himself with the plain accounting of someone who knew the fact and was not performing anything about it. He reached for the rifle again, and she felt the pull toward her belt before she'd decided to feel it, thirty years of training moving faster than her judgment - and he saw it, and stopped, hand on the sling, and looked at her with the whole of his stillness saying: *the rifle comes. This is where you decide.*

She breathed once. She nodded.

He put the sling over his shoulder.

She lowered the lid, gestured toward the door, and he moved when she moved, keeping one body-width between them - not submission, the professional spacing of someone who wanted room enough to do something else if something else became necessary. In the main floor she held up one finger, pointed at the door, held the finger up again. *One person, ahead.* His hand found the sling and settled there. Ready. Not anticipating.

She knocked twice on the door frame before she opened it.

-

Orim was in the passage, back to the entrance, watching the loading yard. He did not turn. "Eleven minutes before the manifest closes."

"The trace goes north from the second dock." She looked at the automaton beside her, still reading Orim's stance the way he had read hers - the hands away from the knife at the belt, the fact that he had turned at the sound of the door and not at the sound of a person. "Whatever you found in there. It's what you said."

Orim turned around and looked at him with the attention he gave everything small: complete, unhurried, the minor scryer's orientation toward the specific rather than the general. He took in the rifle. The riveted plate. The worn place at the left knuckle-joint where a hand had gripped it too many times in the dark. Stamped along the collar housing, in the flattened block-lettering Verlaine used for everything it did not intend to explain: **M-1018883.**

"M-101," Orim said, reading it the way he read most things, aloud, for the record.

Something happened in the automaton's stillness that had not happened for her hand or her blade or the open lid. He straightened, minutely, the way a man straightens at his own name spoken by a stranger - and then, in a voice that came out of somewhere in the chassis's throat, flat, metallic, and entirely without inflection, he said it back.

"M-101."

It was the only sound he had made since she'd found him. It came out correct, drilled, unhesitating - the one thing in this world that was unambiguously his, offered the way a captured soldier gives rank and serial number and nothing else, because it is the one truth they cannot make him regret telling.

Ardea came around the north corner, looked at him, looked at the rifle, looked at Lyra. "You're bringing it," she said. Not a question - the tone of a professional absorbing new information and rebuilding her operating plan around it in real time.

"Him," Lyra said. "Yes."

Ardea took this in stride, the way she took most things. "The courier carries four poorly."

"He walks."

"He walks well," Orim said, before Ardea could ask the obvious question. "A Myrmidon shell doesn't tire. It dents, or it doesn't. It never needs to sit down."

Ardea looked at the automaton for a second longer than she needed to. "That's not a comfort."

"No," Orim agreed.

Lyra had read the intake literature the way she read all research literature: as a problem, categorized, filed, professionally distant. *An M-prefix serial brands a Myrmidon* - a mind Scried out of a living person in some other Sphere, hauled up wet through the warm gold between worlds, and installed, without ceremony, in a shell built to be replaced rather than repaired. Not a captured man. A stolen one, wearing the only body Verlaine had thought worth spending on him. She had read the phrase *complete linguistic disjunction upon initial Sphere crossing* in a footnote and found it interesting the way footnotes were interesting. She was standing in a warehouse passage in Bressant with the footnote's subject a meter away from her, having just given her, without prompting, the one word he owned outright, and she found she had nothing filed for what that was actually like.

"Eight minutes," Ardea said.

"We'll call him that," Lyra said. "M-101. Until he tells us different."

Orim glanced at her - not disagreement, something closer to noting that she had chosen the harder, more honest option, when a softer one had been sitting right there for the taking. She could have given him a name of her own invention, the way you name a stray, and it would have cost her nothing and given him nothing either. She had decided instead to use the one true thing stamped on him, which was uglier and, she thought, more his.

M-101 had followed every word of it - the naming, the reasoning underneath it, Ardea's clock-counting, the whole exchange three strangers had conducted a stride away from him as though he were freight that happened to have opinions. He had followed the warehouse before that. He had followed the latch, and Orim's reading of the collar, and every word since. What he did with any of it he kept to himself. He kept it to himself a moment longer.

Then he didn't.

"It'll do."

The voice came out of the chassis's throat again - flat, metal-toned, the same instrument that had given his number back to Orim a minute before. But this was not one drilled syllable, offered the way rank and serial number are offered and nothing else. It was a full sentence, unhurried, in the plain vowels of a man from somewhere none of them had ever set foot, each word set down deliberately, like he had all the time there was and none of it to waste proving so.

Ardea went still in a different way than she had been still a moment before.

Lyra heard, underneath the sentence, the exact shape of the thing she had just been thinking about - *complete linguistic disjunction upon initial Sphere crossing* - and understood, hearing him, that whoever had written that phrase had never stood in a room with the thing it claimed to describe. There was no disjunction. There never had been. He had understood the warehouse. He had understood Orim reading the stamp off his own collar. He had understood Ardea calling him "it" twice in the space of one breath and had let both land without comment, filing them the way he filed everything else, and had simply not, until this exact moment, elected to answer any of it.

"You speak," Ardea said. Not a question. Something closer to an objection lodged a beat too late to do any good.

"I do." The receptor did not turn to find her when he said it; it was already on her. "Gave the number first. Seemed the truer thing to lead with."

Nobody said anything for a stretch of time that ran a half-second too long to be comfortable. The flatness of the delivery did the same work twice: it was the voice of a man choosing his words with care, and it was, to ears that had not yet learned better, indistinguishable from the voice of a slow mind reciting something it had been taught. Lyra filed the difference before she trusted herself to speak again.

"Seven minutes," Ardea said, recovering the only piece of the moment she still had authority over, and was already moving.

-

Getting him onto the Heloth courier took three things: showing him what it was, showing him where to hold, confirming that he understood the second one meant something and was not merely obedience. He approached the machine's shoulder housing with the specific care of someone who already knew what machines could do and was working out this one's category - not fear, appraisal. He looked at the joint construction a long moment before he put his hand where hers had been, and he did not look at it again after that. He watched the terrain instead, the whole way out of the district, both hands on the bar, weight distributed correctly, the posture of someone who had done this kind of thing, or something enough like it, before.

He did not tire on the run to the dock. She noted this the way she noted everything now, adding it to a growing file she had not intended to start.

The *Houndstooth* was loading grain at the second dock, bound north on the ordinary commercial run to the Atrament processing centers - unremarkable, seventeen barges a week at this season, the manifest stamp worn shallow from four years of the same clerk's hand. She bought passage for four under a Calyx guild seal she'd held since a posting six years back, paid in credit vouchers, and the dockworkers gave M-101 the look they gave anyone unfamiliar - brief, categorical - and went back to loading grain.

Below, the cargo hold smelled of river mud and old rope, rope hammocks along one bulkhead, a bench along the other, a small iron stove bolted to the forward bulkhead behind a mesh screen - the one open flame a grain barge would tolerate below decks, and tolerate only barely. Lyra took the bench. Ardea took the hammock farthest from the hatch. Orim found a crate by the porthole, the position that let him watch the river and think, which he appeared to do at the same time and without particular effort at either.

M-101 stood at the foot of the companionway a moment, reading the space the way she had learned to watch him read every space: the hammocks, the hatch, the positions of three strangers already distributed through the hold, the corner that gave him the longest view of the only exit. He found it - against the casks, farthest from the hatch - and sat, and set the rifle across his knees, and the oval receptor dimmed, not dark, not lit, something between.

The barge moved. She felt it in her inner ear, the barely perceptible unhinging from the shore, and she opened her notebook to a clean page and did not yet know what she meant to write there.

-

The stove burned low and orange behind its mesh, and for the first hour of true dark the only sound in the hold was the river against the hull and Ardea's slow, even breathing in the hammock and Orim turning a page of nothing in particular, and M-101 sitting motionless against the casks with the receptor holding its half-dim, not entirely gone out.

She watched it for longer than she meant to.

He did not sleep. She understood this slowly, the way you understand a fact your body has been reporting for an hour before your mind agrees to receive it: a person drifts, a person startles, a person shifts their weight and resettles and eventually needs water or the rail or simply to move. He did none of these things. The receptor held its ember-dim for as long as she watched it and gave no further sign, and somewhere in that unbroken stillness she stopped thinking of it as rest and started thinking of it as something closer to waiting.

-

What the shell would not tell her, the fire told him.

Not sleep. She would only have a word for what she was watching much later, when someone finally explained it to her in language, and the language would only confirm what her body had already decided that first night without any help from anyone: a shell like his did not rest, because rest is a thing a body sets down into, and Verlaine had built nothing into him capable of setting anything down. What happened instead - on the first night since the yard quiet enough, and still enough, and safe enough that the idling routines finally dropped toward whatever the shell called low power - was not gentler than sleep. It was worse. There was nothing to wake him from, because nothing in him had gone anywhere to be woken from. The mind in the housing was simply left, for the first time since the taking, with nothing left to attend to except itself. And itself was a groove worn so deep by three months of running that idling did not smooth it over. It found the groove at once, the way a wheel finds a rut it has already cut, and it began again from the beginning, because beginning again was the only shape it had left to make.

He sat in the orange half-dark of a stove he could not feel the heat of, motionless to every eye that might have been watching, and behind the receptor a man who did not exist anywhere the receptor could show her was living it again, in full and exact sentences, in a register nobody in this hold would ever hear:

*Not pain, first. Displacement. Like a hook set through the mouth of something that had never known it was suspended in warm gold water until the water was gone, and him with it, going up.*

*Then no body, no up, no edge to the dark, only the wreckage of a life turning slowly past him out of reach - debris, not pictures - and then weight, wrong weight, and a line of them, black and identical, and a voice reading a number off a sheet the way a clerk reads a manifest, and him stepping forward because the number, ugly as it was, was the only thing in the yard that belonged to him and to no one else.*

*Then - always then, the groove narrowing here the way it always narrowed here, as if this were the part of it that had never finished happening - a gunship, and a ring in its flank stencilled with a number that was not his number. Turret #34. Below it, a river crossing, crowded with shapes that moved the way living things move. And the mechanism Verlaine had wired into the housing beside whatever he still was offering him, patient, unhurried, exactly as it had offered it the first time and every time since, a solution. Range. Bearing. A tone that meant one thing only. Loose.*

*He does not fire.*

*The tone comes again - no impatience in it, because the thing offering the solution has no impatience to spend, only correctness, only the arithmetic repeated - and he does not fire.*

*A third time. The groove always finds a third time, precise, unhurried, forever arriving at the same three, and forever he does not fire.*

*Then - the one honest thing the shell has ever told him, the only report that has ever reached him whole, without the half-beat lag, without the flatness, without the sense of being informed about his own body by a stranger - the smell. Burnt ozone. Cooked wire and scorched housing. The firing gear seizing on itself in the ring beside him rather than do the one thing it exists to do to the shapes at the river. It is the single part of that entire day that told him the truth.*

*Nobody comes to the turret ring after. A robed figure further down the ship makes a note on a board, brief, and the note is the whole of the proceeding, and somewhere in the barracks afterward he will learn the shape of that note before anyone troubles to say it to his face: you do not court-martial a tool. You scrap it.*

*The tone offers the solution again. It always offers the solution again. There is no floor to this room either, and no door out of it, because the loop does not know that it has already ended - ending is not a thing that was ever built into it.*

Across the hold, in the part of the night that belonged to the living, Lyra saw none of that and all of it. What reached her was smaller, and came without translation: the receptor's ember-dim, holding steady the way it had held for an hour, and then - guttering. Not going dark. Flickering, fast, three small drops in the low red glow, evenly spaced, gone again before she could be certain she had seen them at all. Somewhere in the shoulder joint a servo ticked once, faint, the sound a lock makes testing itself against a door it already knows is barred. And under the river-sound and Ardea's breathing, so thin she nearly decided she had invented it, a thread of the same mineral smell she had catalogued in the grain bin that morning - ozone, faint, present, gone before it could rightly be called a smell at all.

She did not know what any of it meant. She only knew, watching it, the particular cold certainty of standing outside a room where something was happening that she had no language yet to name, and that whatever it was, it was not rest, and it was not nothing, and it repeated - three flickers, stillness, and then, a long while later, three more, evenly spaced as the first, the way a held thing repeats when it has not been permitted to put itself down.

The fire ticked in its screen. The river went on being a river. None of the words reached her, and none of the pictures, and no part of the number screaming under the number he had given Orim for his own. What reached her was only the guttering and the small dead tick of the servo and the ozone-thread under the woodsmoke, and it was not much, and it was enough to keep her watching him long after watching him had stopped being required of her.

-

She closed her notebook without having written anything, and looked at him across the hold - the dim ember of the receptor, the rifle across his knees, the stillness that was not rest - and understood, in the plain unornamented way she understood most things that mattered, that she had come into a grain warehouse expecting to find a person hiding from a war, and had instead pulled out something the war had already finished with and thrown away, and that it had not stopped thinking the entire time it had been thrown away, and that this - not the theft, not the ten-day lead the Pallor squad had on them, not the barge or the border or the smoke that would be waiting for them on the Atrament horizon by morning - was the thing she was going to have to find room for. It had a voice. It had chosen, twice now, exactly when to use it. That was not a small thing to have learned about a machine on the first day of knowing it.

Orim, without opening his eyes, said from his crate by the porthole: "He's not sleeping."

"No."

"Good," he said. "Someone should keep the first watch who isn't tired."

She looked at M-101 a while longer. Then she picked her notebook back up, because there was work to be done, and it seemed to her, watching him watch nothing in the almost-dark, like the only thing between them that was currently honest.

[Beat 10]
﻿?SEVEN MONTHS

The great hall had stone arches, the vaulting starting thirty feet up where the walls gathered themselves into ribs and carried the weight of a roof someone had built to last and which had, in fact, lasted - long enough that whoever built it was gone and whoever used it now had no interest in what it had been for. Rows of narrow bunks ran wall to wall, wood-frame, two high, straw mattresses under dark wool blankets, the floor cleared of whatever it had once held and given over entirely to housing a great many bodies with minimum expenditure of either space or comfort.

On the far wall: a rack of bolt-action rifles, standing where he supposed pole-arms once had.

He smelled the place before he understood any single thing about it - metal-oil and wet wool and the particular sourness of men who had not been warm enough for long enough. He was shown a bunk. He put down the kit he'd been handed and lay on it and looked at the arches overhead and thought, in his own language, in full sentences, that he did not know where he was, and that not knowing was going to be the condition of things now, the way weather was a condition, something to be managed rather than solved.

The first word that mattered was *kal.* Stop. He understood it the instant it was said - the shell had given him the whole of the language before he'd taken ten steps in the new body, complete and instant, nothing rationed - but understanding a word and commanding a body somebody else had built to obey it in the same half-second were not yet the same skill.

He learned the gap between the two from the gap between moving and impact - from continuing to walk when everyone else in the column had already stopped, and from the hand that closed around his collar and hauled him sideways into a wall hard enough that the impact came to him not as pain exactly but as a jolt through the frame, a half-beat of delay between the shove and his body's registering of it, as if the message had gone somewhere else first and only reached him on the return trip. The men nearby made a sound. Not laughter. Recognition. *That one will learn.* He filed it as possibly encouraging.

After *kal* came the equipment, because equipment had to work or someone's attention would land on the man holding it, and the man holding it was reliably him. *Kest* - the lock on the action housing. *Vurt* - the sling. *Reml* - the r carried back in the throat, a sound his own mouth had never needed before, though the shell made it for him without being asked - the cleaning kit: pull-through rod, oil bottle, small brush for the breach face. He understood every one of those words the moment he first heard them said. What took the rest of the week was matching a body he'd had for eleven days to objects it had never held: which motion was the lock and which was the sling, done fast enough and clean enough that nobody's attention came looking for the man holding them wrong.

By the end of the first week he could load, clean, and stand to inspection without being corrected twice for the same fault.

---

There was a particular quality to the confusion in some of the others, and once he knew to look for it he found it everywhere. Not the ordinary confusion of a man wondering why he'd been ordered to do something pointless - a confusion with a shape, a grievance behind it. This was different: confusion with no category to file itself under. He recognized it because he had it himself, and because once, crossing the washroom at the end of a corridor, he had caught the same look passing over the blank dark oval where his own face should have been, in the tin sheet someone had hung for a mirror and never bothered to polish. He did not linger on this. There was nothing further to be done with it. He noted it, the way he noted a great many things now, and moved on, because moving on was the only motion available to him that cost nothing.

Some of the others had lost the look, or buried it deep enough that it only surfaced at night. There was one - Sphere 22, by the number stamped where a unit designation went, the only name Rowe had for him - who had been in Verlaine two years. His Caul came out even and unhesitating. He took his ration without looking at it. He was good at filling the gaps when Rowe fell silent mid-order rather than answer, and he was extremely good at being somewhere else when things went wrong. There was no face to read fatigue or cunning into - the chassis gave nothing away, black and identical to every other chassis in the hall - but the motion gave everything away, if you watched long enough. Sphere 22 moved like a man who had stopped being surprised. Rowe did not judge this. He filed it for later use, the way he filed most things now, in a growing archive with no obvious purpose except that a mind needed somewhere to put what it saw.

There was also Sphere 14 and Sphere 8, glimpsed more than known - a nod across the mess line, a shared five minutes on latrine detail, gestures rather than sentences. He would not have called any of them friends. He would have said, if he'd been willing to say much of anything yet, that they were people the same thing had happened to, which was not friendship but was not nothing.

---

On the third day they moved him to the range.

He drew a rifle from the day issue and his hand went to the bolt before the trainer had finished demonstrating on the man beside him. Lift, draw back, seat the round, drive forward, lock down. He did not decide to do this. The motion arrived the way *kal* had arrived - ahead of thought, the shell's half-beat lag catching up to an intention that had already, somewhere behind it, been executed a thousand times in a body that no longer existed.

He knew this mechanism. Not this model - the lock housing was wrong for what he knew, the magazine cleaner than anything on the diagram pinned in the ironmonger's window on Cress Street - but the family of it, the idea. His world had drawn the blueprint. This world had cut the finished part. He had the Caul for all of this - for *blueprint*, for *prototype*, for *descent* - the shell had given him every word he'd need to explain the whole strange genealogy of the rifle in his hands, the day it took his voice and gave it back changed. He said none of it. There was no one present he trusted with a sentence that size, and admitting he recognized a weapon's ancestry was the kind of fact, once spoken, he would never get to take back.

The trainer looked at a man standing at ready with a loaded rifle he had not been taught to load, said something that in any language would have meant *when did this happen*, and went to fetch a second trainer. They turned the rifle over between them, running fingers along the housing with the specific attention of men handling something they had not expected. He waited. Later he found, stamped into the receiver near the action end, an angular notation that took him four months of studying the marks men leave on hardware to finally read whole: SPHERE-SOURCE PATTERN - DESCENT CORPS AUTHORITY - NOT FOR CIVILIAN REGISTRY. The institution had known exactly where the weapon's ancestry lay. It had stamped that knowledge into the metal and issued the rifle anyway, and worried, in writing, about distribution rather than origin. He put this in a box he did not open. Some thoughts, in a body running on a limited account, you held rather than spent.

They put him in the front row for the rest of range day. The word the trainer used for him afterward had an approving shape. He filed it under things he wasn't going to explain and moved on.

---

The man from the close world came in the second month, off a transfer batch from the eastern intake station.

There was no reading a trade off a chassis built identical to every other chassis in the hall - no callus, no forearm, nothing to see. But motion was not identical. This one's hands went to a tool the way hands go that have spent a working life at a bench: an economy in the wrist, no wasted travel, the discipline surviving the change of hand the way *kal* had survived the change of everything else. Whatever age he'd been, in whatever body he used to have, the tempo said *not new to this.*

In the dark after lights, Rowe said something in his own language, not expecting anything, only to hear himself say it - and the shell carried it out flat and finished, the way it carried out everything he let it carry. The other one turned toward the sound and answered, and the two flat voices understood each other completely, on the first exchange, the way any two shells in the hall would have understood each other, because the gift underneath the chassis did not care whose original tongue it had been built from.

It was not the understanding that took a week. Understanding was free - the shell had handed it to both of them the day it took their voices, and neither of them had done a thing to earn it. What took the week was everything the understanding made possible, and neither of them was in a hurry to use.

They spent the evenings of that week finding out how much of home the other one was willing to say out loud, one careful admission at a time - the mill, the bench, the machine floor, traded like men trading cigarettes across a wire, each one held out and taken and not repaid immediately, because repaying too fast would have meant it hadn't cost anything.

The first time Rowe laughed since he'd woken into any of this was over nothing worth the name - some small, stupid overlap, each of them having assumed for a week that the other was a different kind of prisoner than he was, the mistake unravelling in under a minute once neither had a reason left to guard it, and it should not have been funny, and it was.

The man had been at a machine floor when it happened to him too. He had not chosen to come, and did not understand what he had been brought here for. This was not a surprise. It was confirmation, which was a different animal - it did not change anything, only put weight underneath what he already believed. He thought, once, of the German prisoners in the camp east of Arras, men from the Ruhr and Brandenburg standing behind wire in a country whose language they had not chosen either. He had walked past that camp twice in France and not thought much of it at the time. He thought about it now, noted the thought, and put it where he could find it again.

---

By the third month he could follow orders instantly and without the half-beat of resistance that used to cost him a shove into a wall - not because he understood them any better than he had the first week, he had understood every one of them complete and immediate from the day the shell first spoke, but because he had stopped making it wait on his consent before it carried the compliance out. Conflating the two - understanding and consent - was the mistake the trainers made about him for months, and the mistake worked in his favor exactly as long as it lasted. The name of his own rifle was still the only word in the whole language he said with anything like ease.

He had the Caul for *loyalty.* For *betrayal,* for *injustice,* for *conscription* - the shell had given him the whole architecture of it the day it took his voice, complete and instant, nothing withheld. What he did not have was standing to use any of it. A man could own the word *betrayal* outright and still have no right to it, in a war he had been dropped into without a side. He had asked Sphere 22, once, carefully, where a particular man from the barracks had gone, and been given a word back - *solen* - and understood exactly what it meant before the shell finished saying it, though nobody explained it and nobody needed to.

---

The order to advance came on a road that was not a good road, in weather that had committed to being bad. Low cloud. The smell of rain that hadn't started yet but had made up its mind. Mud spreading in from the margins the way mud does once it decides to.

He reached the ridge with the rest of the column and looked down into the valley.

Two forces, converging from different angles toward the same low ground his own column was descending into. He did not need the tactical picture to read the geometry - a man who had spent ten years at power looms understood fixed paths and moving parts, and what happened at the point where paths that were not supposed to cross did anyway. He did not know whose flag either force carried, if either carried one. He did not know what either side was fighting for. He did not know, past the plain fact of an order, what he himself was fighting for.

He had nothing on either side that was his. No officer whose authority he'd chosen. No allegiance he'd made with his eyes open - only an oath taken on a day he barely remembered as his own, in a voice that hadn't been fully his either.

The engagement started below with a sound he knew before he'd finished hearing it. A bolt mechanism makes the same report regardless of which country machined it; the Caul had not changed the sound just because he was no longer anywhere near home.

He turned around.

He walked back up the road while the column went forward and down, the noise of it first close and then receding, and nobody stopped him, because everyone forward of him had somewhere more urgent to be. He walked until the sound was behind him, and then distant, and then gone, and kept walking anyway, north, because north was away, and away was, for the first time in three months, a decision that was entirely his.

---

Bressant was large enough to disappear into, the way any city is if you understand that anonymity is mostly a function of volume. He found the grain district by smell before he found it by sight - dry warmth, faintly sweet, the slow exhale of bulk material settling in a closed space, close enough in principle to the cotton floor at home that some very old part of him relaxed at it without being asked to.

He slept three nights in the first warehouse he found, then moved when the section he'd claimed showed signs of regular inspection, and moved again the week after that, learning the district the only way it could be learned: which yards kept irregular schedules, which foremen watched the perimeter and which didn't, which loading crews looked up. He looked plausible. He had looked plausible his whole working life, compact and unremarkable in a way that made him easy to overlook, and overlooked was now the entire strategy.

He did not need to eat, not in the sense the word insisted on. Whatever kept the chassis running was not hunger and would never announce itself as hunger. And yet some old, stubborn part of him went looking for food anyway out of habit, the way a man reaches for a door handle in a house he no longer lives in - took what passed for it when a warehouse offered spillage, drank from the runoff channels between the yards, because his body had spent thirty-odd years being fed on a schedule and had not yet been told, in any language it trusted, that the arrangement was over.

He kept the rifle.

This was not practical. It was heavy, needed keeping dry, and was not the kind of thing a district laborer carried, which meant concealing it added difficulty to every entrance and exit he made. He kept it anyway, because it was the one thing here he had never once been uncertain about. Everything else - the language, the oath, the intake forms, the box he did not open - had come to him in conditions of partial understanding. The rifle was comprehension without conditions. He had recognized it before anyone handed it to him. He was not going to set it down now.

---

He had said almost nothing of Caul unprompted in seven months - not ordered out of him, not repeated back to a trainer, not issued at all. The shell had given him the whole language complete on the first day, every word at once, nothing rationed and nothing withheld; what was scarce was never the vocabulary. It was the willingness. What follows were not words he happened to have picked up. They were words he had chosen, on his own account, to spend.

*Faeb.* Factory. Said once, to no one, staring through the window of a textile concern in the western quarter - a different mill from his own, organized on a logic he recognized anyway, and worth naming out loud for exactly that reason.

*Fume.* Smoke. Said the instant he saw it rising off a smoldering rafter in a loading yard, word and sight arriving together.

*Morn.* Morning. Said once, standing in formation before the whistle went, the first day he understood that his body was going to keep doing this whether he agreed to it or not.

*Solen.* Gone. What Sphere 22 had told him, once, about a man from the barracks, and what he had said back to himself afterward, quietly, as if repeating a fact made it belong to him rather than to whoever had handed it over.

He said them sometimes, in the dark, not to communicate anything to anyone. In his own language - which was still the language his thoughts arrived in first, still the language his memory kept its rooms in - these were things he had understood long before he'd ever said a Caul shape for them at all. Saying the Caul back to himself in the dark was not practice. He had nothing left to practice. It was closer to proof: that what he'd named existed here the way it had existed at home, and that he - not the shell, not the intake officer who had stamped him M-1018883 - had been the one to decide it was worth naming.

His own tongue was still his. He remembered everything - there was no mercy in it and no blur - but there were places now where the Caul arrived first when he reached for something to say, simply because the Caul had been used yesterday and his own words had sat unused for seven months. He did not find this comforting. He noted it and put it in the box with the rest.

---

He had found the wheat bin in the eastern warehouse three days ago, and stayed a second night past when he should have moved, and would stay a third for the same reason he always did: moving carried its own risk, and staying carried another, and in seven months he had never once worked out which of the two risks was smaller.

The grain gave off warmth as it settled - some slow chemistry in the kernels working itself loose, a fact he had no name for in any language but recognized on contact. It pressed against him at chest height, close on both sides. He noted the warmth and did not know what to do with the noting of it, since registering a temperature and being warmed by one had stopped, somewhere in the last seven months, being the same operation.

Outside, the loading yard ran its morning cycle - heavy foot traffic thinning toward midday, the grind of the hoist-wheels still an hour off. He could read the time of day from the sound alone now, a kind of knowledge that accumulates without ever announcing when it arrived.

He repeated the words once, not aloud. *Faeb. Fume. Morn. Solen.*

Then the boards above the warehouse shifted.

He felt the weight of it before the sound reached him - timber taking a load at one discrete point, not the general creak of a building working against wind, not the hoist-wheels, which weren't due. Something applied. Something placed.

He held still.

The sound came a beat later: careful, which was its own kind of loud to anyone who had spent a season learning what care sounded like from the inside of a grain bin. Not a supervisor's tread - supervisors walked with the confidence of people who belonged. Not a work crew, who moved in clusters and didn't trouble themselves with quiet.

Single. Careful. Coming toward the section he was in.

In the dark, in the press of the warm grain, his hand - the black jointed thing that answered him a half-beat late and had answered him faithfully for seven months regardless - found the rifle.

[Beat 11]
﻿THE RIVER - FACTORY / SMOKE / MORNING / GONE

The barge was called the *Houndstooth* and smelled accordingly. Below decks, behind the casks, Lyra had found a corner between a stack of oilskin-wrapped bales and the outer hull where river water moved just inches from her ear. Orim had negotiated the passage in Bressant, at a chandler's back room, over a transaction she had not been present for and had not asked about. She had learned, in the three days since they'd pulled Rowe out of a grain bin in the warehouse district, that questioning the terms of Orim's arrangements was not strictly prohibited but was consistently unproductive. She had stopped asking on the morning of the second day. Orim had noticed. He had said nothing. She catalogued the silence as confirmation and rolled her coat under her head.

He was at the porthole when she settled-hands inside his coat, watching the river in the specific patient way of someone who had decided, long ago, that darkness was not inherently stable and that having a witness cost nothing and had saved him twice. She had known three people in her life with that particular quality. All three had also been cautious, specific, and very difficult to kill.

Rowe had already powered down against the far hull, boots on, hands half-closed around nothing in the way of a unit that had idled in inadvisable places long enough that the posture had become a default setting rather than a choice. She noted the boots. She noted the hands. She filed both beside the other things she'd been accumulating since Bressant-the way he moved through unfamiliar rooms, the way he watched people before he let people watch him, the particular quality of his attention when something surprised him, a door opening briefly in a face that otherwise kept its own counsel. She had no comfortable category for the collection. She made a provisional one and put everything into it.

She slept four hours. When she woke, the gray at the porthole had thinned at its edges-not yet morning, but the first hour of a morning that had not committed to being one. Her shoulder had locked. Not the right, which had taken the second infusion badly and always announced itself first with a grinding complaint, but the left, the old courier's groove worn into the muscle over twenty years of dispatch work, before she'd taken her vows and stopped carrying messages and started being one. The left shoulder had never entirely forgiven her for the new height and new density the infusions had asked of a body that hadn't been consulted about geometry. She worked the joint with her fingers until it grudgingly recalled that rotation was available to it, then sat up and found the manifest waiting on the nearest cask.

---

The days on the barge established their shapes by the second one.

Orim catalogued the hold the way he catalogued everything-touching the casks, reading their labels, weighing the bales in his hands and setting them down again with the expression of a man filing something into a category too small for the object. He also, without announcement, began talking to Rowe.

She missed the start of it. She looked up from her notebook to find it already under way: Orim naming a cask's contents, asking a plain question about it-what it weighed in his own trade, what his people would have used it for-and Rowe answering in three or four flat words, correct, complete, and no more than the question required. Orim nodded, moved to the next cask, and asked something else, small and concrete, the way a man tests a lock by trying the easiest key first.

She watched for ten minutes and went back to her own work. She could hear it continuing at the far end of the hold-question, short answer, a pause exactly as long as it took Rowe to decide how much of the true thing to hand over, then the next question-with a rhythm that had nothing to do with teaching and everything to do with patience. Orim was curious about everything and needed Rowe to eventually tell him what he knew; between those two facts a very careful interrogator, gentle enough not to feel like one, had apparently assembled itself.

Her own notebook ran a different kind of ledger. Not words-he had all the words, flat and toneless and exact, from the first hour the chassis had powered up in the grain bin and startled a dockworker into dropping a crate. What she tracked instead was the shape of what he'd give and what he wouldn't: a column for questions asked, a column for the answer he provided, a third, usually the longest, for the ones he let sit until the silence itself became the answer. The columns diverged instructively. He was more exact than most men she'd debriefed and more withholding than any of them, and the two facts sat together without contradiction, the way a soldier's rank and serial number sit beside everything he declines to say about the unit.

By the time she closed the notebook that first day he had answered nine of Orim's questions outright and let four go unanswered. She had learned nothing new about his language and a great deal about his discipline.

---

She found him at the porthole that afternoon, while Orim slept against the hull with the slow, even breathing of a man making up a debt.

Rowe lay flat with his chin on his forearms, watching the passing bank through the lower edge of the glass. Entirely still. She took up a position at the far end of the hold, where the angle let her see both the porthole and the side of his face, and watched him watch it.

The Veil River in its lowland reaches ran through country she knew the way she knew a form she'd filed a hundred times: checkpoint positions, road conditions in autumn, water quality at the midpoint wells. The banks here had gone soft and low, cut by old drainage channels into long straight fields the river bent gently around rather than through, poplar windbreaks standing in files along the fieldlines, a church tower showing gray above a stand of elm two bends ahead and then gone again behind the next rise. She had passed this stretch three times in twelve years. She had filed it as transit, noted, no further action required, and had never once looked at it the way she was looking at it now, which was through him.

He was not doing anything she recognized as useful. Not alarm. Not assessment. A sustained, absorbed attention, the kind you paid to something you were trying to reconcile with something you already knew and could not quite manage. His gaze tracked the poplar line as the barge went by it, and something in his face went still in a way that had nothing to do with rest.

He said nothing.

That was the whole of it. The door came open behind his eyes for the length of a breath-she was getting better at seeing it happen, though no better at seeing what was behind it-and then it closed again, the way it always closed, before she could take an inventory. He had the words, she knew by now, for whatever he was looking at; he simply was not spending them. He did not name what he'd seen. He did not point. He watched the poplars fall away behind the barge's wake with the particular grief of a man revisiting a photograph he had chosen not to pass around the room, and then he put his chin back down on his forearms and the moment was over, filed wherever he filed such things, behind whatever door he'd just closed.

She had no notation for it. She noted that she didn't, and put it in the provisional category next to the boots and the hands.

---

That night the sound came first.

Third hour past dark. She had been sleeping in two-hour intervals and was in the shallow end of the second when the vibration reached the hull-not through air, through water and keel-wood, a low rhythmic grinding her sleeping body registered before her waking mind did. She sat up. Orim was already at the porthole.

"Rail yards," he said, without turning. "A kilometer north. Night freight."

She went and stood beside him. The river ahead was dark, and then it was not-a flash from the north moving south, blue-white, sharp-edged, nothing like fire, which climbed and spread. More like a signal handed down a long corridor faster than any one thing was moving it. The flash lit a slice of the river-dock pylons, shallow-draft barges at anchor, flat black water-and then went dark, and the next flash, further south, showed none of it, carrying the signal on.

She knew what it was without being told. She had read the documentation once, in an idle hour: chromatophore-scaled river fauna, established in the Veil for two hundred years, reactive to vibration. She had read it and had not understood, until this exact moment, what it looked like. The documentation had not conveyed that part.

Rowe had come alert somewhere in the first pulses. She found him at her right, close to the glass, watching the north.

"Fire," he said, flat and certain, in the tone of a man naming the nearest thing he had to compare it to, and wrong.

"No," Orim said, and gave the actual name of it instead-a proper noun, three syllables, the kind of word a place invents only after it has needed to invent it many times. She wrote it down before she was fully aware of writing, the notebook already open in her lap, the motion automatic. The Caul had thought this thing worth naming specifically, which she filed immediately as useful.

Rowe said it back once, exact, the way the shell rendered everything exact, and did not ask what it meant beyond what he'd already worked out from the light and the sound. Orim named the fish stock upriver that fed on it, and the guild that held the netting rights past the third bend, and Rowe took both facts with the same unhurried care he gave anything about this world he hadn't yet had reason to learn-not the care of a man for whom speech was difficult, but the care of a man cataloguing a country he intended to survive.

The river went dark and stayed dark. The grinding faded north.
