She closed the notebook, then opened it again and added a line beneath the new word: *river-flash, chromatophore-scaled-pull origin-Sphere documentation at next archive.* There was always origin-Sphere documentation. The Caul documented everything. That, too, she filed, and did not yet know what to do with.

---

The manifest had come the first night, left on the cask nearest her head while she slept, by the barge master or by Orim, whose ability to move through enclosed spaces without disturbing anything was, she had concluded, not incidental.

She read it in order, which was not how she usually read a manifest-she turned to the back pages first, out of habit, because commercial cargo ran bow to aft, highest value listed earliest, then grain, livestock, textiles, and at the very end the small parcels that wanted no notice. The *Houndstooth's* last four pages were unremarkable: salt fish in wax paper, a Calyx smithy's mechanical springs, a physician's bag the manifest called *sundries, medical* and which was certainly something more specific than that, but itemizing it correctly would have cost the physicians' guild more than the discretion was worth.

She turned to page eight.

Six Scry data blocks. She counted them twice. Six was not an unusual number-the guilds had normalized data blocks into freight documentation during her grandmother's grandmother's time, arguing, successfully, that information was information, that the Vigil's older requirement-a Templar signature before every transfer, the blocks kept apart from ordinary cargo-was administrative weight the commercial current could no longer carry. The Vigil had not had the stomach for the fight. Now the blocks rode between grain and wine in every manifest going north on the Veil, and the old signature requirement was a historical footnote in a filing system she had worked inside for fifteen years without once looking up why it had changed.

The first block: Sphere 14, agricultural, contact duration 0.3 seconds. Standard-a third of a second of some other world's weather on some other world's field, the look itself already gone the instant the membrane closed over it, nothing left of it anywhere in the Caul except what a scryer's hand had written down before it could vanish the way every scry vanished. The third: a Sphere 9 dyeing method, 0.8 seconds, longer, unremarkable. She had read blocks like these for fifteen years. She had filed her dislike of them long ago under *professional distaste, noted, proceed*, and had proceeded every time without examining what proceeding meant.

The fifth block stopped her.

*Origin Sphere: [designation by number only, no catalogue entry]. Method: [contact, surface membrane, 1.4 seconds]. Data class: manufacturing, secondary. Subject: spring-steel alloying method (notation format: see attached sample). Observed at production facility. Transfer to: Henkin Consolidated Arms, Atrament branch.*

The notation sample was attached-a half-sheet of heavy paper, folded once, a column of symbols she had seen before. She had been seeing it, she realized, for twenty years. In commercial code. In freight documentation, on this same river, a hundred times over. She had believed it was Calyx's standard freight notation because she had never had a reason to believe otherwise-because the category she kept for *freight notation* had never once intersected with any other category in a way that required her to ask where it came from.

Rowe was three meters away, curled against the far hull, the faint uneven ticking of a shell cooling down somewhere behind his plating. His hands, even idle, were closed around nothing in the specific way of a unit that had recently been holding something and expected to need it again.

The notation on the attached sample matched the notation on his rifle's receiver plate.

She folded the manifest closed. Then she opened it again, because folding it did not change what was written on page twelve, and she had not become a researcher by declining to read things. She read all six blocks. The sixth-*Origin Sphere: [same designation, no catalogue entry], 2.1 seconds, manufacturing, primary, large-scale*-gave her a category to put this in. It did not have clean edges. She filed it anyway and sat with the corner of the manifest pressed against her knee, thinking about edges.

"Six," Orim said, from the porthole.

"I counted."

"The fifth and sixth are from the same sphere. The one without a number."

"Yes."

"The notation on the sample sheet." He paused, in the particular way Orim paused when he had already said the thing he meant and was waiting to find out whether she'd understood it.

She had understood it.

"How long have you known?"

"Since we found him." He turned from the glass, wearing the look he got when he was about to say something that sounded like a joke and wasn't quite one. "I've been looking at small things for sixty years. When a man reacts to a notation format, I notice. It's my gift."

His gift was Scrying, minor-class, contractual-which meant that at fifteen he had pressed his palm to a membrane he had not been told was alive until afterward, felt it give and warm under his hand like something holding its breath, and watched the small burn of contact close over and scar shut by the time he'd walked home that evening-a wound like any other, healed like any other, no mark left to show the guild recorder what it had cost him-and had done so eleven more times over the following six months, until the contractual minimum was satisfied. The Calyx guild paid his pension for it. He had never volunteered whether it had hurt. She had never asked.

"It might be coincidence," she said.

"It might," he agreed. "So might the rifle."

The rifle sat in the barge master's strong-chest two decks above. She'd had it looked at in Bressant's harbor quarter by two dockyard smiths Orim knew, sixty years of combined experience between them, and both had told her-separately, without conferring-that they had never seen anything like it, that the metal was something they had no name for, that the notation on the receiver plate belonged to no system either man recognized. The second smith had said, *It's very precisely made*, in the tone of a man who had expected to be unimpressed and had not managed it.

---

She talked with Rowe for three hours a day. It had been Orim's suggestion, delivered as a professional opinion and understood by all three of them as an order. *If you don't get him talking,* he'd said, *you will spend the rest of this assignment guessing. I am too old to guess.*

The three hours were never consecutive. They assembled themselves out of whatever the barge's schedule, Ardea's watch rotation, and Rowe's unpredictable idle hours allowed. He gave her the same designation and soldier's account he'd given at Bressant, refined only by repetition-rank, unit, the plainest facts of the taking-and past that, for the better part of the first day, very little. She asked him where he was from. He said he had been a soldier. She asked what he had done before that. He said he had been a soldier a long time. It was not evasion in the sense of a lie; it was a man deciding, question by question, which door in the house he was willing to open, and closing every other one without apology.

She learned the shape of him instead of the content. He answered anything factual and present-tense without hesitation-the barge's heading, the hour, the weather. He answered anything about his own body and its function-what the shell could and couldn't do, how long it ran without rest-with the same flat completeness. He turned every question about *before* into a shorter answer than the question had asked for, and did it so cleanly that it took her two days to notice he was doing it on purpose.

He did not answer carelessly. She could see the pause before he spoke-not the pause of a man searching for a word, but the pause of a man deciding how much of a true thing he could afford to hand over before it cost him something he wasn't ready to spend. She had met three people in her life who weighed their words that way. One had become a negotiator for the Templar Compact. The other two were dead, under circumstances that had nothing to do with how carefully they'd spoken, and she noted the trait regardless.

She had made him, the first night, a page in her own hand: the barge's route out of Bressant, the names of the two stops between the harbor and Atrament, the little she knew of what waited at each, because he'd have no way to know any of it otherwise and she had not weighed too carefully whether it was standard protocol to hand a found man from an unknown Sphere her own working notes. He had carried the page folded in his coat for two days before she noticed. He held it a long moment before looking up and saying, flat and toneless and entirely clear: "You made this for me."

She understood the sentence exactly-the shell left nothing to guess at-and had not expected it to land the way it landed, and had not expected her own response to go anywhere but the *noted, filed, proceed* category.

It had not gone there.

She did not examine the alternative category. She noted that it existed. She continued working.

---

At the second hour past midnight, on the third night of the passage, Rowe came alert with a long creak of boards and his boots on the planking, and she was awake before he reached her corner.

He sat against the adjacent cask. Not close enough to be a problem. Close enough to be an intention.

She'd left the lamp burning low, which she did whenever she expected interruption, which was whenever she was somewhere she didn't know. He looked worse in lamplight than he had in the grain bin at Bressant. The scoring along his cheek-plate-a dent taken before she'd found him-had dulled from a bright fresh scrape to the flatter, oxidized cast metal took on after days of exposure, and the rim of his oval receptor had gathered a fine grit in its seams that no amount of wiping quite lifted. There was no age to put on him and no scale that would have held one. What she had instead was wear, and there was more of it on him now than there had been in the grain bin.

He had the manifest. She didn't remember him taking it-she'd left it closed on the cask beside her, the sliver of wood still marking page twelve-and filed the quiet borrowing the same way she'd filed the boots.

He held it out to her, open to the page she'd marked.

"I'll tell you four words," he said. Flat. Exact. The voice the shell gave him, the one that made people mistake care for slowness the first week and never again after. "Not the rest of it. Not tonight."

She said nothing. She uncapped her pen.

He looked at the hold around them a long moment-the casks, the bales, the enclosed wooden structure of the thing-and, she thought, at something beyond it that was also enclosed, also structured, built around a function that was not the function of a river barge.

"Factory," he said.

She wrote it in the left column, across from nothing, because there was nothing yet to put beside it.

He was quiet long enough that she nearly asked. Then: "Smoke."

She wrote that too, and looked up, and found him watching the page rather than her, the particular attention of a man checking that a thing he'd said out loud had actually survived the trip to paper.

The third word took longer than the first two together. He looked through the porthole at the gray beginning to be something other than absolute dark-the interval between night and morning that had no clean name in most of the languages she knew.

"Morning," he said, and something in his voice on that one word was different from the other two, though she could not yet have said in what way, only that she filed the difference and meant to come back to it.

He said the fourth word immediately after, flatly, in the tone of a thing held in the mouth too long and needing, finally, to leave it.

"Gone."

He sat with his hands on his knees and looked at the four words on the page, and she watched him decide something in the way she had learned to watch him decide things, which was to look at his own hands first, because his face had had more practice at stillness.

*Factory. Smoke. Morning. Gone.*

"That's the whole of it," he said. "Tonight."

There was more sentence than that. She had heard enough of him by now to know the shape of a thing being handed over deliberately incomplete-four words offered the way a man hands over a single photograph from a stack he has no intention of showing the rest of. He was giving her the corner of a document so she would know the document existed.

She looked at the four words. She looked at the notation on the sample sheet, still open on the facing page. She looked at the Scry block description above it: *Origin Sphere: [designation by number only, no catalogue entry]. Observed at production facility.*

She looked at Rowe.

He was already watching her, with the careful stillness of a man who had spent a great deal of time being watched by people who held authority over his circumstances, and had learned to be very quiet when that happened, because quiet men were easier to release than men who got noticed.

"A factory," she said. "Yours."

Not a question, though she left it room to be answered like one.

He nodded once. Not resistance-the specific tension of a man confirming something he had been trying, in his own economy, to say for longer than this conversation had existed.

"Smoke," she said, and watched his face for the same thing she'd watched for on the second word, the thing she still didn't have a name for. "What kind."

He was quiet a moment. "The kind that comes from the process," he said. Then, after a pause long enough that she understood it cost him something: "And the kind that's what's left when the process is finished with a place. I don't know which one I mean. Both, maybe."

She drew a line under the word and set a question mark beside it. Not his uncertainty-her own, about which half of the sentence she was allowed to ask for next.

"Morning," she said. "You've told me a factory, and smoke. Why morning."

He didn't answer that one. He looked at the porthole, then at her, then down at his own hands, and the silence went on long enough that she understood it for what it was: not confusion, refusal. She had asked for the fifth word before he was ready to give the fourth.

She took the pen back and didn't press it.

"You came from there," she said, very slowly. Not a guess. A statement she was giving him room to correct if it was wrong.

She did not point at anything. She didn't need to.

"Yes," he said.

---

She didn't sleep again.

She worked the notebook until the porthole light was clear enough to read by without the lamp, and by then she had four words, a manifest page, and three subcategories that would need revising. People did this-the same fact approached from different distances, wearing different weight depending on how close you'd let someone stand to it. She had debriefed frightened men and grieving men and men who lied without knowing they were lying, and they all showed some version of the pattern. She was beginning to know what she was looking for in this one. That was different from knowing what she'd find, but it was something.

Ardea came below at first light to count the provisions, looked at Lyra, then at Rowe-idled somewhere around the fifth hour, hands finally open-then back at Lyra, with the expression of someone who had several questions and had correctly decided none of them were hers yet.

"We'll see Atrament inside of four hours," Ardea said.

"I know."

"You look like you haven't slept."

"I've slept." Not untruthfully.

Ardea accepted it with the professional grace of a woman who knew when a superior wanted to be left with her own thoughts, and went to count provisions.

Orim appeared an hour later with a cup of something handed down without comment. She drank it. Not quite tea, not quite anything else-the thing he made when he thought someone needed warming and didn't want to make a performance of caring about it. She'd had it twice before in her life. Both times had turned out, once she'd had enough distance to describe them at all, to be bad.

"Four words," she said.

"I heard."

"You were supposed to be sleeping."

"I was listening. It's different." He settled his back against the hull. His joints cracked when he moved; he'd stopped noticing years ago. "Four words that go together like a sentence."

"The shape of a sentence. I don't have the rest of it yet."

"Factory," he said. "Smoke. Morning. Gone."

"Yes."

He was quiet a moment. "In my first contracted Scry session, the membrane I was put in contact with was the size of my palm. I was told it was a developed specimen. An old one. They didn't tell me what that meant until later." He looked out at the river. "I was given nineteen questions to hold in mind during contact. I managed twelve before the session was called. The guild recorder filed the results immediately; I was not permitted to review them." He paused. "Standard practice. I never found it strange until recently."

Lyra said nothing.

"The notation on the manifest," he said. "The same as his rifle."

"Yes."

"Someone documented that sphere very thoroughly." He did not say *before.* He did not say *before it burned.* He looked at the porthole, then at Rowe's idled shape, then at the manifest in her lap. "Thorough documentation. Filed in the commercial freight system. Sitting between grain and wine in every barge manifest going north for-" He stopped. "How long does a guild archive hold its records?"

"Sixty years," she said. "Older than that, they're at the central repository in Verlaine."

"Sixty years," he said. "At minimum." He turned back to the glass. "Good documentation," he said, in the tone he used for things that were not good at all.

---

The sky changed before the smell did.

She noticed it while Orim slept and Rowe's receptor stayed dark-the gray she'd watched for three days had shifted in tone rather than temperature, a faint yellowish cast, as though the air between the barge and the horizon had been filtered through something warmer and sustained and industrial before it reached her. She noted it, filed it, said nothing.

Twenty minutes later the smell arrived. Coal first-not coal in storage, cold and damp, but coal that had burned long enough to pass through all its stages and settle into the char-and-mineral note of a furnace in continuous operation. Behind it, rendered fat, tallow reduced to its parts for candle and lubricant. Behind that, something sharper: hot metal, the mineral edge she had catalogued the first time she'd made this passage twelve years ago and filed as *Atrament entry signature, industrial, note for future reference.* She noted it again. It sat in the same category. It did not sit with the same weight.

Rowe came up from his corner when the smell changed. He was at the porthole before she turned-not crowding, simply present, the way he was present when something demanded it of him. The ticking under his plating had slowed. His face was doing the thing it did over the manifest, the door blown open for the length of a breath, closed again before she could take stock of what stood behind it.

The sound arrived before the stacks did. She felt it in her sternum first-a low continuous vibration, not rhythmic like the rail yards but constant, the acoustic signature of processes that did not pause for dark or morning or season. It separated, as they drew closer, into its parts: the percussion of the forges, regular as a pulse; a higher mechanical whine that had run long enough to find its frequency and settle there; and beneath both a ground-note that was less a sound than a pressure against the ribs, the kind of thing a body registered before the ears did.

Then the stacks appeared at the river's bend-dark verticals against the yellowed sky, eight of them, then nine, then more as the bend opened onto the full waterfront. Taller than she remembered from twelve years back. Or the same height, and she was only reading them differently now, carrying different things than she had then. The smoke coming off them was the color of the sky itself, already absorbed into it, indistinguishable at any distance she could measure, moving south the same direction the barge was moving, so that she could not say where the stacks ended and the sky began.

Rowe stood very still beside her.

She did not turn to look at his face. She had the shape of the sentence already; she did not need his face to confirm it. She let the shape sit in the provisional category, beside the boots and the hands and the poplar line and the word he'd said to no one at the passing bank, and did not look at any of it too directly before she had the architecture to hold it.

*Factory. Smoke. Morning. Gone.*

She held the pen and did not write.

The smell was fully on them now, coal and fat and hot metal, familiar as thirty years of this work. The sound had gotten inside the hull. The sky above the stacks was the color it had always been when she arrived at Atrament from the south with a task that needed doing and not enough hours to be careful in.

She looked down at the notebook, at the four words on the open page, and did not write anything beneath them. There was nothing to guess at. Whatever came next, he already had the words for it; the only question was whether the stacks now filling the porthole would be enough to spend one more of them on.

She held the page up and showed it to him anyway, the way you hold up a door you're not asking someone to walk through, only to notice.

He looked at the four words. He looked at the stacks. Something in him that had been carefully, professionally held for a long time went briefly uncontrolled-she was getting better at seeing it happen-and then controlled itself again. He took the pen and, without being asked, wrote a fifth word beneath the others, small and precise and entirely legible.

*Home.*

She did not ask him to explain it. She filed it beside the other four, in the same provisional category, and did not press for the rest of the sentence.

One word given freely. The rest still his to keep.

The stacks kept coming. The smell thickened. Lyra sat with her notebook, working the puzzle of him the way she worked everything-methodically, without hurry, noting what would not fit an existing category and building a new one, narrow and provisional, to be revised the moment there was more data.

The new categories were multiplying.

She noted this. She filed it. She kept working.

[Beat 12]
﻿THE ROAD - DESERTERS AND WAR-DOGS

The *Houndstooth* put them off at the Atrament wharf an hour past dawn, and Ardea stayed behind at the customs shed to argue the barge master's declared manifest down to something the dockmaster would stamp without opening page twelve. Lyra had not asked her to. Ardea had simply said, "Someone should," and gone, and the logic of it - a Templar's own transit papers drew more attention from a river clerk than a scout's did - had not been worth contesting on a wharf full of men who wanted the barge unloaded before the tide clock came due. She would catch them on the road by midday, or she would not, and either way the manifest would be someone else's problem for an hour, which Lyra had learned to count as a small mercy and take without comment.

Rowe had his rifle back before he had his boots properly dry - first thing off the gangplank, the barge master's strong-chest opened without ceremony, the weapon returned into his hands with the particular briskness of a man settling an account. He did not check it in front of anyone. He checked it the way she'd come to recognize as his own private liturgy, three motions performed in the same order every time, bolt drawn back a finger's width and eased forward again, a weight shifted twice against his shoulder to confirm the sling sat where he'd left it. Then it was slung, and he was Rowe again, and not a man briefly incomplete.

The road out of the wharf district ran flat for the first two hours, farmland gone to seed on both sides - fields that had been worked within living memory and were not being worked now, drainage ditches silting, a scarecrow leaning at the angle of something nobody had come back to straighten. Poplar windbreaks stood in files along property lines that no longer had owners present to defend them, the same trees she'd watched from the barge porthole two mornings running, close enough now that she could see the bark stripped waist-high on the roadside row - not weather, not blight. Cook fires, set against the trunks by people who had needed the wood and hadn't cared what it cost the tree to give it up.

"No garrison markers," Orim said, an hour in. He said it the way he said most things that mattered, mildly, as an observation offered for her filing rather than a warning delivered for her alarm. "There should be a boundary stone every half league on an approach this size. Atrament keeps better books than that."

"Pulled for scrap. Or never replaced."

"Either version tells you the same thing." He adjusted the strap of his pack, not because it needed adjusting. "Nobody is walking this road who officially exists."

She had already reached that conclusion on her own - the empty fields did it, and the scarecrow, and the particular quality of quiet that sat over farmland the war had stopped bothering to defend - but she let him say it anyway, because saying it aloud between the three of them was a form of confirmation she'd come to rely on more than she cared to examine. She filed it under road conditions, no further action required, and knew as she filed it that the category was wrong. There was no boundary stone. There was no garrison. There was no column ahead of them and none behind, and a road without a column, on a continent at war, was not a road. It was a stretch of ground where the law had gone somewhere else to be enforced.

Rowe stopped walking.

He did it the way she'd watched him do everything in the three days since Bressant - without preamble, without a change in his breathing she could detect, simply a cessation of forward motion that arrived a half-step before hers did, so that she registered his stillness in her own body before she'd consciously decided to match it. His head had not turned. His eyes had.

She stopped beside him. Orim, three paces ahead, took one more step out of momentum and then stopped too, without looking back to ask why - a discipline she noted and appreciated, that none of them required an explanation before they gave the moment its due.

"What," she said, low.

Rowe didn't answer in words. He lifted one hand, palm down, and lowered it slowly - the gesture she'd already filed, from the barge, under *wait* - and then, with two fingers, indicated the treeline on their left, the low ridge on their right, and the road itself, in that order, a survey conducted in under three seconds.

She looked where he'd looked and saw a hedgerow, overgrown, and a scarp of raised ground on the far side thick with bramble, and a road that narrowed between the two for perhaps forty meters before opening again into the fields beyond. She had walked a hundred roads like it. She had never once stopped to ask why a road narrowed where it did, because a road narrowing was a fact about drainage or old field boundaries, not a fact about anything that mattered.

Rowe was not looking at the road. He was looking at the hedge, and at a specific gap in it, low, at ankle height, where the growth had been disturbed and had not yet grown back to match the rest of the line. He looked at the raised ground opposite, where the bramble broke twice in patterns too regular to be animal trails. He looked, last, at the birds - she followed his eyes there without understanding why until she noticed, the way you notice a held breath only when it's released, that there had been no birds for the better part of a quarter hour. Fields like this held larks. She had not registered their absence until Rowe made her register it, by registering nothing else.

He said one word. His own language, not hers - she didn't have this one yet - but she didn't need the translation. He was pointing at the two gaps in the bramble and the one in the hedge, and his hand, flat, traced a shape in the air between them: a rough horseshoe, open toward the road, closed on both flanks.

"A funnel," Orim said. He had come back the three paces without appearing to hurry. He was looking at the same gaps Rowe had found, and something in his face had gone still in the specific way of a man revising an estimate downward. "You're certain."

Rowe didn't answer that either. He unslung the rifle. That was the whole of his answer, and it was sufficient.

"How many," Lyra said.

He held up four fingers on one hand. Then, after a moment's further study of the ground - the depth of the trail-breaks in the bramble, she guessed, or some other sign she didn't have the eye for - he added a thumb from the other. Five, or more than four and he wasn't certain past that. He had learned to Caul numbers on the barge alongside everything else, one through ten, taught by Orim over a cask of dried fish; he could have said it. He chose the hand instead, and she understood, watching him do it, that speed mattered more to him right now than practicing a language he might not live to keep improving.

"We could go around," she said. It was not a serious proposal. She made it because the alternative - walking forward into a shape a soldier from another world had read off broken bramble and absent birds - deserved at least the courtesy of a discarded option before she committed to it.

"The field on the right is drainage ditch for two hundred meters before it firms," Orim said. "The one on the left backs onto the river cut. Around costs us an hour we don't have if Ardea's catching us by the ford, and it announces to whoever built that funnel that we saw it, which trades one problem for a worse one." He was already easing his pack strap loose, freeing his shoulder, his other hand finding the Rod at his belt and resting there without drawing it. "We go through. We go through the way he tells us to."

Rowe looked at her. Not a question - a confirmation that she'd understood the shape of what came next, delivered in the particular stillness of his face that she had learned, over three days, functioned as most of his vocabulary before he'd had any words at all.

She drew the Dissolution Blade. She settled the Vigil Shield against her forearm, feeling the small, familiar weight of it find its balance. "Through," she said, and it was not a question either.

---

He put them where he wanted them without a word of Caul and without needing one - a hand on her shoulder, firm, that turned her four degrees toward the hedge side; a flat palm toward Orim that meant *behind*, or close enough that Orim read it correctly and dropped a half-step back and to the left. Rowe took the road's center, the rifle up, and walked forward at a pace that was not the pace of a man in a hurry to reach the ambush and not the pace of a man trying to avoid it. He walked at the pace the ground gave him, watching the gaps.

Nothing happened for twenty meters, which she understood - filing it even as her pulse climbed - to be the worst possible sign. A trap that sprang immediately was amateur work. A trap that let you get most of the way through before it closed was the work of men who had done this before, on other roads, against other travelers who had not had Rowe.

At the twenty-fifth meter, the man in the bramble rose to shoot, and Rowe already had him.

She did not see him aim. She had watched the rifle come up smoothly on the walk in and had assumed, as much as she'd assumed anything in the last twenty meters, that he was watching the ground the way he'd read it from further back - but the muzzle had tracked the gap in the bramble the entire time, had been resting on it before there was anything there to rest on, and when the shape rose - a man, thin, a mismatched coat with the collar tabs cut away, a musket coming up to his shoulder - the shot took him under the collarbone before the musket had cleared level. He went backward into the bramble he'd been hiding in and did not rise again. The bolt came back, the spent case arced clear, the bolt went forward, and Rowe had not broken stride.

That was the last quiet second of it.

Two more came off the raised ground on the right - not shooting, closing, one with an old cavalry saber that had been somebody's before it was his and one with a woodsman's axe held wrong, choked up too high on the haft by a man who had learned to swing it for trees and not for people. Lyra went to meet them because meeting them was the geometry Rowe's positioning had built for her - she was closest, on the hedge flank he'd turned her toward, and the shield came up into the saber's first cut and ate it, the shock running up her arm into the old courier's shoulder that never forgave her anything, and she stepped inside the man's reach before he'd recovered his balance from the parry and put the blade into him low, under the ribs, angled up, the way thirty years of Templar drill had built into her hands so thoroughly that she was three motions past the kill before the part of her that filed things had finished registering that she'd done it.

He made a sound. Not a word. She had heard the sound before, from other men, on other roads, in the two engagements her career had actually required of her rather than the twenty-eight years of transit and archive work around them, and she had never found a category narrow enough to hold it and never expected to.

The axe-man swung high, overcommitted, the blow meant for a stance she'd already left. She took the shield to the haft rather than the blade - cheaper, and it cost him his footing rather than her arm - and put him down with the flat of the blade across the side of his skull, hard enough that he did not get up, and she did not check whether he would get up again later, because the dogs had arrived.

Three of them, coming low and fast out of the same bramble the second bandit had used, and they were not fed and they were not sane and they had been, at some point she could reconstruct only in fragments from the collars still hanging half-rotted around two of their necks, somebody's trained hounds - war-dogs, the kind that ran message lines or worried at a column's flank in the old doctrine, before whatever unit had owned them stopped feeding them and let them go, or died around them and left them masterless on a road where the only meals left were travelers.

The first one went for Orim.

She saw it happen and could not close the distance in time to matter - Orim was three meters clear, the Rod half-drawn from his belt, his other hand coming up in the instinctive, useless gesture of a man who had spent sixty years cataloguing threats from a comfortable remove and had never needed his own body to answer one directly. The dog took his forearm instead of his throat, which she understood later was the only reason he kept the arm at all - he'd gotten it up in time to trade the wrong thing for the right one - and the sound he made when the teeth closed was worse than any of the men's had been, because it was Orim's, and she had three days of him being the steadiest thing on the barge to set against it.

Rowe shot the second dog off its line before it reached her, a snap shot she would not have believed possible from a standing bolt-action if she had not watched him do it, and worked the bolt again with the same economy, and did not look to see whether the shot had been clean, because the third dog was already past him and going for the throat of the man he'd have needed both hands to fight.

She reached Orim's dog with the blade already moving and did not go for a killing stroke first. She went for the jaw, the pommel driven hard into the hinge of it the way the Old Watch manuals - she thought of them absurdly, in the half-second it took, thought of a story she'd half-heard once about a Templar and a creek ford and a thing with too many joints, thought *inside the reach* without knowing where the phrase had come from - the pommel into the hinge to break the grip before the grip broke Orim's arm past using, and the dog let go with a yelp that had a note of confused pain in it more animal than malicious, more wounded thing than monster, and she killed it on the second stroke because there was no second option available to her, and hated, distantly, filing it even as she did it, that this one had not deserved what had been done to it any more than Orim had.

The third dog got Rowe's coat and not his throat, its teeth finding cloth and the plating beneath rather than skin, and he took the impact of it against his chest and used the fall to bring the rifle butt down onto its skull with a sound she would remember longer than she wanted to. It did not get up.

Then it was quiet, which was its own kind of violence, arriving as abruptly as the noise had.

---

Two men fled - she counted the ones down, four, and Rowe had said five or more, and the arithmetic did not reconcile cleanly and never would, because two sets of footsteps were already receding fast toward the river cut and she was not going to chase them across broken ground with Orim's arm doing what it was doing.

It was doing quite a lot.

She got his sleeve back and found the punctures deep enough to want binding and not so deep that binding wouldn't hold them, four points where the teeth had gone in and a long tear where he'd wrenched free before she reached him, the blood coming fast and dark and not arterial, which she noted with the specific gratitude of a woman who had learned to distinguish the two kinds under conditions worse than this one. She bound it with the field wrap from her own kit, tight, and he sat very still and let her, which told her more about the pain than anything he said, because Orim narrated everything he could stand to narrate and had said nothing since the dog let go.

"That will need a physician's needle," she said. "Not field wrap. Not for long."

"I'm aware," he said. His voice had gone thin at the edges. "I have had worse from a membrane. I have simply had it more slowly, which I recommend, if you are given the choice."

Rowe checked the fallen - not for pulses, she realized, watching him move between the bodies with the same economy he'd used to walk the funnel, but for weapons, ammunition, anything that told him what he needed to know about who these men had been before they were dead men on a road. He crouched over the one he'd shot first and went still.

The musket was old. Not old the way the cavalry saber was old, a family piece carried past its usefulness - old the way a thing looked when it had been built for a war that had ended and pressed back into service for one that hadn't. But the bayonet lug fixed beneath the barrel was not a Caul pattern. She'd seen enough requisitioned arms in Vigil inventories over the years to know a Compact-standard mounting when she saw one, and this was not it - a socket design, a specific angle of the locking ring, details she couldn't have named the significance of and Rowe plainly could, because he'd gone still over it the way he went still over poplar lines and factory smoke, the door behind his eyes coming open for the length of a breath.

He said nothing. He set the musket down exactly where he'd found it, muzzle away from the body, the particular care of a man returning something to a shelf rather than discarding it, and did not look at her, and she did not ask.

She had a category open for it already. She put it there and left the edges unfinished, the way she'd left the edges unfinished on the poplars and the Fire word and the four syllables he still hadn't given her a Caul translation for. Some things he carried in silence. She had stopped believing, three days ago, that silence meant nothing was there.

The other bodies gave up less. A haversack with dried meat gone green at one corner and a waterskin half-full; a folding knife nobody would miss; a hand-drawn map, badly made, marking three checkpoint positions along the Atrament approach roads with dates beside two of them, the more recent date eleven days old. She took the map. She left the meat, which she didn't need and didn't want, and the knife, which Orim already had two of. She counted the coin in the haversack - eleven pieces, mixed mint, none of it Atrament currency - and left that too, because taking a dead deserter's pay from his own pocket was a transaction she had no stomach for today, whatever the Vigil's ledgers might have said about salvage rights on a road with no jurisdiction to claim them.

"The map's useful," Orim said, watching her fold it. His voice had steadied, some. "Checkpoint positions eleven days stale are still checkpoint positions. Roads don't move as fast as armies do."

"It's not much."

"It's more than we walked in with." He flexed the bound arm experimentally and stopped when it told him not to. "That is generally the arithmetic on a road like this one. You do not win anything here. You survive it, and you carry out whatever happened to be lying on the ground when it was over."

She looked at the four dead men and the three dead dogs and did not, this time, try to find the category. Some things did not compress. She had learned that on the barge, with the manifest, and she was learning it again now, on a stretch of road with no boundary stone and no garrison and no name she'd ever bothered to write down, and she suspected she would go on learning it for as long as this errand asked anything further of her.

Rowe stood at the road's center where he'd first stopped walking, reloading the rifle from the small store of cartridges in his coat, his hands steady in a way the rest of him plainly was not - she had watched him check his own weapon three times in three days and understood, by now, that the ritual was load-bearing, that a man performed the same three motions in the same order because the order was the only thing left standing after everything else had been asked to give.

"How many rounds," she asked him, in Caul, slow.

He held up fingers. Counted them against his coat pocket. Answered her with a number that was smaller than she wanted it to be, and neither of them said anything further about it, because there was nothing to say that changed the number.

They walked on within the half hour, Orim's arm bound tight against his side, the two of them slower than they had been and Rowe watching the treelines the rest of the way with an attention that did not relax again before the ground began to rise. Ardea caught them at the ford, an hour later than she'd promised and unbothered by it, and took one look at Orim's arm and one look at the four bodies they'd left behind them on the road, unburied, because there was no one left on this stretch of ground whose job it was to bury them and the party had a war to outpace.

"Bandits," Lyra said, before Ardea asked.

"Deserters," Ardea said, correcting her without malice, the way a scout corrected a Templar on ground the Templar hadn't walked as many times. "There's a difference. Bandits choose it."

Lyra thought about the musket's bayonet lug, and the door that had opened and closed behind Rowe's eyes, and the coat collar cut clean of its tabs on the first man he'd killed, and did not argue the distinction.

She filed it instead, in the category that was getting fuller by the hour, and did not yet know what to do with it, and kept walking, because the highland road was still ahead of them, and past it, somewhere in a valley that had not yet made its sound heard, an army was standing at ease and waiting on an order that had not yet come.

[Beat 13]
﻿?ATRAMENT - THE FORGE KINGDOM

The sound was wrong before anything else was.

They had been on the highland road three hours, the rail line running below them in the valley where it curved south around the granite bluff before swinging north again toward the Schmalburg basin. The road ran above the rail, older than it, older than the Compact by the evidence of its own construction - dressed stone in courses, the mortar long since gone gray and crumbling at the joints, the stone itself still seated true. This was the quality of old work on the major approaches: built by people who had intended to move armies, not goods, and who had not considered that the armies might one day be replaced by freight schedules. The road held well under the weight of four people who were not a siege.

Three hours in, Lyra heard the thunder.

Not weather. The sky above the northern highlands was the flat white of high autumn overcast, the kind that holds no storm and only promises that the cold will get worse before it improves. Not weather, and not any natural sound: a deep, continuous vibration coming from somewhere ahead and below, a sustained bass note that the body registered before the ears would confirm it. She had been feeling it in the stone under her boots for the better part of a quarter hour before she understood it was not her own fatigue.

"We're getting close," Orim said.

He was ahead of her on the road, moving in the steady, unhurried way of a man who has covered a great deal of ground over fifty years and made his peace with the pace it costs. He had not looked back. He had not slowed.

"You've been here before."

"I have crossed the Atrament border in eight directions over forty years," he said. "The sound is the same in all of them. You stop hearing it after the second day. Then you leave, and you stop not-hearing it, and the quiet sounds wrong for a week." He stepped around a gap in the stone facing where a spring had colonized the joint. "Or so I'm told. I've never stayed long enough for the second part."

The smell came next. Not the smell of any one thing yet - the category of it. Metal worked past the temperature it wanted to be worked at. Stone asked to survive conditions stone was never built for. Something chemical underneath both that coated the back of the throat without being quite unpleasant, the smell of a place that had been doing one specific thing for a very long time and had stopped noticing it did it.

Rowe breathed it once, testing, the way a man tests bathwater with two fingers before he commits the rest of his hand. He did not do it again. It was the Black Country on a still morning - a hundred chimneys agreeing on the same weather, coke-smoke and quenched iron, a smell he had walked out of an army recruiting office and straight back into eight years and one continent ago. He said nothing. Lyra had learned by then that the things worth his saying and the things he had words for did not reliably overlap.

Ardea walked at Lyra's other shoulder, scarf pulled over the lower half of her face against the highland cold, watching the road ahead with the specific posture of someone who has learned that cold does less damage if you decline to acknowledge it. Rowe fell in behind them both, watching the smoke - visible for the better part of an hour now, not rising from any single chimney but sitting over the next ridge as a general discoloration, a permanent grey-brown stain the overcast could not fully absorb.

The valley opened below them when the road crested the ridge.

Lyra stopped.

She was not a person who stopped for things. She had been trained out of it - the vigil's long-watch discipline, the Templar's field doctrine on maintaining movement in unfamiliar ground, thirty years of applying both - and the discipline was sound, and she had followed it every day since. You cataloged as you moved. You did not give the landscape a performance of your reaction.

She stopped.

Below the ridge the valley dropped in a series of terraces, and on the largest of them, a league distant, stood a fortification. That was the correct word for its shape - corner towers, curtain wall, the thickened stone of a structure whose builders had assumed threat from every direction at once. The walls had kept something out for a long time, or kept something in, which was often the same wall doing the same job twice.

It was also, at the same time, a factory.

She had read the Compact's language for this - *integrated manufacturing installation, castle-form adaptive reuse, Compact Year 837, ongoing modification per Hearth Council decree* - and had built an image from the words: a factory grown up beside a castle, or fitted inside one, some transitional state where you could still find the seam.

There was no seam. The curtain wall was the factory wall. The corner towers had been cored out and fitted with furnace stacks that rose straight up out of what had been the battlements, the old parapet stone now serving as collar-rings for iron chimneys thick as well shafts. Smoke stood over three of the four in a column that leaned southeast in the highland wind, steady enough that it had made its own local weather. The wall itself had been cut through in six places she could count from the ridge, openings wide enough to drive a loaded freight wagon through - not windows, not gates, just apertures sized to whatever the process needed moved in and out of it. Raw material entered on the east face. Finished product left on the west. The wall did its job as a factory wall and made no pretense of doing anything else.

This was not a castle with a mill grown up around it. It was a forge that had once needed walls, and had simply kept them.

There was nothing apologetic in it. That was the part she had not anticipated. The architecture did not excuse itself. The castle had been a castle. It was now also a forge. Both were true of the same stone, and the stone had no opinion either way.

"Year 841," Orim said, arriving beside her. "The Hearth Table authorized the first interior conversion under the manufacturing expansion protocols. Before that it was a garrison. Before the garrison, a watchtower against the valley floor. Before that, something the Atrament record won't discuss, which usually means there was an argument about who built it and nobody won."

"How old."

"The base courses read pre-Compact. Possibly pre-House. Someone was keeping watch from this ridge before the Houses existed." He looked at the stacks. "They keep a different watch from it now."

The sound resolved into its parts the closer they came: the repetitive thud of forging hammers at a cadence too even to be human hands; the deeper sustained note of furnaces at full heat; a rhythmic compression from somewhere inside the walls she couldn't place from outside. All of it running at once. All of it running without pause.

Below the fortification, in the yards between the outer perimeter and the approach road, the Heloths moved.

She had worked with Heloths at the Ocipheus Station - the vigil's courier models, lean single-rider constructs built to cross open ground without complaint. You developed a working sense of one if you rode it long enough, not fondness exactly, but familiarity with a machine built to do one thing well and decline everything else. The Heloths in the yards below were built for a different job: heavy-cargo variants, wide and low, the arm-housings fitted for cradle loads instead of saddle points, running the fixed routes between the factory mouths and the freight stage with the patient repetition of something calibrated to a task and content to run it until the maintenance interval said otherwise. Three stood motionless at what she recognized, even at this distance, as servicing stations.

The stations glowed. Orange, low, bioluminescent - the ember-fungus, introduced from some other Sphere generations back, that grew wherever the Catalyst discharge left its residue and did not stop growing once it had taken hold. She had read about it in the ecological annex at Ocipheus. Reading about a fungus that lit a construct yard orange from the inside was not the same thing as standing above one and watching it burn low and steady on stone that predated the Houses.

"At night, from a ridge, you can read the whole yard by the fungus alone," Orim said. "Every station. Every wall the discharge spatters during the cleaning cycle." He paused. "That will matter later."

"When."

"When we need to see the yards after dark," he said, in the tone of a man releasing information at the rate he judged useful and not one measure sooner.

The approach road ran through three settlements between the ridge and the Schmalburg basin, and none of them were towns.

The first was three long buildings and a dormitory block. "Hearth-stations," Orim said. "Numbered by production sequence, not geography. Station Twelve isn't near Eleven or Thirteen. It's the twelfth stage of the chain." He looked at the dormitory. "Same plan across the whole valley. Efficient use of the floor."

"It's very clean," Ardea said.

He looked at her.

"I was expecting it to be dirtier. Everything that size, making everything it's making. I expected more debris."

"Debris costs," Orim said. "Debris is raw material that has left the cycle before it should have. The Hearth Table worked out, four centuries ago, that the cheapest form of cleanliness is treating waste as escaped product. Nothing lands in that yard without a value assigned to it, and the value is recovered." He watched the clean yard slide past. "There are Atrament scholars who will tell you the arrangement isn't unkind at all - that it simply weighs collective output over individual comfort, and that the people living it understand the trade and accept it."

"Do they," Ardea said. "Accept it."

"The scholars," Orim said, "are not, as a rule, the people living it."

Ardea was quiet for a moment. "That's worse, somehow."

"Yes," Orim said, and did not elaborate, and Lyra understood that this was the whole of the answer he had.

At the second settlement they stopped briefly at the edge of a servicing yard, where a heavy Heloth stood under two workers running the discharge-clearing procedure on its back housing - the same one she knew from the Ocipheus maintenance cycle, scaled up. The ember-fungus washed the whole station orange, the workers visible in it as shapes more than as people. One said something short to the other. The other laughed, briefly, the ordinary currency of anyone doing a cold routine task in company.

Ardea watched them a moment longer than the pace of the road required, in the particular way she had of looking at things she meant to remember and had not yet found a place to file.

Then she moved on, and so did Lyra.

The staging ground was at the valley junction, where the approach road met the east-west freight road and the floor widened enough for a temporary military formation to sit comfortably: flat, sufficient, close to the eastern supply route without sitting on the rail line itself.

Six supply wagons under canvas. Three infantry columns standing at ease with their kit stacked at their feet, in the specific posture of men who had been told to wait and had been waiting long enough to develop opinions about the waiting. Two heavy Heloths moved between the columns carrying loads that were not exercise weight - she could read it in the gait, the small forward compensation of a construct carrying something denser than it had been trimmed for.

She counted the wagons twice. Six, both times.

The canvas was tied down under canonical property seals - the circle-and-line pressed in red wax, the Liturgy's mark for goods in transit under inter-House review. Property under that seal was subject to inspection only by the authority that had sealed it. Which meant it was subject to inspection by no one standing on this road.

She looked at the wagons. She looked at the columns. She looked at the direction the Heloths were carrying their loads - east, toward the highland approach, the route the Vigil rotation resupply used.

Seven years in the Ocipheus archive had put every disclosed Atrament production record filed under the Compact through her hands at least once. She knew the seasonal patterns: south to Loera for export, north to the Vigil line for resupply, held in the valley for the maintenance cycles. What she was looking at was neither. The Vigil line's own supply doctrine ran five sequential stages, and you did not open stage five until the first four were positioned and confirmed. Sealed wagons at a staging ground meant the beginning of nothing. They meant the end of something that had already been moving for a while.

She had been counting supply movement since the border without understanding the shape of it. She understood the shape of it now, standing at this junction, and it did not improve on understanding.

No Compact filing she had read disclosed an exercise here. No inter-House notice accounted for this supply chain. And the class of canonical seal on those wagons required countersignature from the Canon Immaculate - not the routine cross-border stamp, but the reservation kept for operations that needed the Liturgy's formal legal presence written into them before they happened.

She filed it, next to the theft, next to the Relic's absence, next to Prelacze's signature suspending her own field authority. Next to everything she did not yet have a whole shape for, whose edges were beginning, one at a time, to show themselves.

Rowe had stopped a little apart, watching the staging ground with the stillness she had learned to read as him keeping a record without a notebook. His face carried, for longer than the usual three seconds, the look of a man for whom the present had briefly become a transparency laid over something he already carried. The columns. The equipment loads. The faces of men who had been standing at ease since some hour that morning they hadn't chosen.

Then he was watching the road again, the way he always watched it, and she did not ask.

He spoke rarely, and when he did it was clipped. *Wait. No. That way.* And one further word she was fairly sure meant *I understand,* though he deployed it in contexts that also seemed to mean *I have seen this before* and, on at least one occasion, *this is the same thing wearing different clothes.* It carried a great deal of freight for one word.

Whatever the staging ground had told him, that word would not cover it. She let it settle, and they went on.

The descent toward Schmalburg took the rest of the afternoon, the road switchbacking down through the lower terraces until the basin opened beneath them: a river confluence silted, over centuries, into a flat harbor bottom, the dockyard running the western bank in old garrison stone for what looked, from above, like half a league. Atrament's machine yards had grown to meet the dockyard wall from the landward side, filling every open ground between it and the rail line, so that the wall was now less a boundary than a hinge between two kinds of the same industry.

The Veil ran brown below it. Not mud-brown. Not storm-brown. The particular brown of something introduced rather than carried - the color of a river that had been receiving the valley's discharge for generations and had stopped pretending to be anything else. She had read the numbers on this once, in an annex, and nodded at them the way anyone nods at a statistic that is bad but not present. The river from the ridge was present.

There was construction on the dockyard wall. Not repair - new work: scaffolding at three points along the western face, dressed stone going up above the old coursing, the mortar lines even and fine in a way the garrison work below them was not. This required a hand the Heloths didn't have.

Below the scaffolding, along the wall's base, there were workers.

Not construct handlers. Not Hearth-station labor in its dormitory rotation. These moved dressed stone from the staging pile to the scaffold foot, ran the rope-and-pulley that carried the larger blocks up to the men setting the courses above, with the steady, unremarkable efficiency of people for whom the work had long since stopped being anything to remark on - not automatic, but habituated the way difficult repeated labor becomes habituated, the body finding the rhythm of the task while the rest of a person goes somewhere else and waits it out.

Lyra looked at them.

She looked at the dressed stone. She looked at the mortar lines, the fitment, the way the new courses would hold. She looked at the rope-and-pulley and the staging pile organized with the settled order of a site that had been running long enough to find its own logic.

She looked at the workers again.

She looked away.

She did not go looking for a word for it. She filed it, the way she filed the Heloths and the wagons and the columns, in the space that was getting fuller by the hour and had not, so far, produced anything she could act on before nightfall.

Orim made the decision for her when she opened her mouth to ask about entering the city that evening.

"No," he said. "Not tonight." He looked down at the dockyard, the wall, the road. "We watch it from here first. A city under pre-deployment develops an awareness. It watches who walks in." He shrugged the pack from his shoulder. "We go down when we know what it's watching for."

She considered arguing. She did not argue.

They made camp in the scrub above the road, a low fire on dry wood Ardea found wedged in a rock crevice where last season's growth had dried in place, small enough to throw no visible smoke. The cold at that height, once the sun had gone, required acknowledgment rather than refusal - it settled into the joints with the particular attention of a host who knows the guests have nowhere else to be.

They ate. They said little.

Then the dark finished arriving, and Schmalburg stopped being a city visible in its stonework and became a city visible in its glow.

Every servicing station in the dockyard's machine yards - she began counting, lost the thread, began again, gave up somewhere past a dozen as the farthest points blurred together - stood outlined in the same low orange bioluminescence, the fungus grown thick enough now to draw the whole yard's shape in light: the routes between stations worn thin where traffic kept the growth down, the walls that took the worst of the discharge spatter burning brightest of all.

In that light, the scaffolding on the dockyard wall was still visible. The workers were still there - not directly lit, but caught in the reflected orange rising off the stations below, shapes moving stone, the pulley system still turning, the work continuing past the hour Atrament's own hearth-stations rang their shift bells.

She watched the mortar going into the new courses, one stone at a time, patient, exact.

She still did not have a word for it.

"Tell me what you have," Orim said, without turning from the city.

She told him. The sequence from the border to the junction - the supply movements she'd logged without understanding, the stage numbers, the canonical class on the seals, the Vigil doctrine she'd read seven years ago in an archive and hadn't thought of since.

He listened without interrupting. When she finished, he looked at the city a while longer.

"They were never looking for the Relic," he said.

"No."

"They knew where Pallor was taking it when they arranged the theft. The destination was part of the arrangement." He watched the ember-glow on the yards. "The theft gave the Canon a legal basis for moving sealed wagons into position for a Vigil-approach operation without inter-House notice. Recovering a Liturgy asset under canonical review required the Liturgy's field authority to go looking for it. That authority is what's sitting on the valley floor tonight."

"You keep calling it an asset."

"Because that's the word the file uses." He turned his cup once in his hands, not yet drinking. "It isn't accurate. An asset does something. The Oculus doesn't do anything - not the way a blade does something, or a wagon of Catalyst does something. It looks. Something struck an aperture onto the core of Sinter longer ago than any record will name, and it never closed. Every scry I have ever taken heals shut behind me the moment I let it go - the skin seals, the sight is gone, and I keep whatever I managed to remember of it before that fades too. The Oculus never healed. It never stopped looking. Seven hundred and forty-one years of the same unbroken sight, still open right now, this hour, on whatever is growing down there and getting larger while it grows. And a scry reaches time as well as place, so the same window holds what came before it - the thing's own birth, in Year 1079, and older still, colder, a Sinter that was never a crater at all. The Canon didn't lose a weapon he can wield. He lost the one eye in the Caul that was still open."

"Then why keep it in a vault instead of looking through it. If it only watches, watching is not a crime."

"Because a vault was never where you keep a thing safe," Orim said. "It's where you keep a thing from being used. The Liturgy has been the one authority both those armies down there will still accept, for three hundred years, precisely because it has never had to answer for what it's been filing under 'no action indicated' since Year 397. Read what it's seeing and someone has to explain the silence. Easier, every time, to lose the eye that never closes than to answer for what it's still watching."

"It also required suspending the Templar assigned to the case," she said. "My transit documents would have crossed those supply routes eventually. I'd have read the stage-five sequence for what it was."

"As you have."

"I was meant to be delayed long enough that the deployment finished before I could report it."

He turned his cup slowly in his hands. "That's the charitable read."

She said nothing.

"The uncharitable read," he said, "is that you were meant to be holding the Relic when this resolves. A Templar with thirty years of documented loyalty to the Canon Immaculate, found in possession of a stolen artifact recovered from the House the Liturgy's own deployment is about to implicate, is useful to have on the record." He paused. "I mention it because it's true, not because it changes anything we're doing."

"It doesn't change anything we're doing."

"Then we go on as we were."

Rowe sat at the edge of the firelight, not quite in it, his rifle against the rock beside him, watching the valley with the stillness of a man keeping a record he had no way to write down. The ember-glow reached the tributary below the dockyard and lay orange on brown water - the specific color of this place, at this hour, doing what it had been doing for longer than any of them had been alive.

He said nothing. He had nothing yet to say it with. But he had been watching the construction on that wall a long time, and his face had settled into the look she was learning to read as recognition - not the three-second flash he gave to a familiar bend of river or a particular color of stone, but something that had come in and stayed.

She would not know what it was tonight. Perhaps not until he had enough Caul to give it to her. Perhaps not even then.

She stayed awake after the others slept, the cold a reason and the thinking the real one, and went through the sequence from the beginning: the stage-five movement, the seals, the countersignature and what it implied about timing, how far in advance an order like that had to be given, what the earlier stages must have looked like and whether she had already seen them and simply filed them wrong. She had. Three separate sightings, refiled now under their true heading. They fit.

The deployment had been running before the theft. The theft was not the start of anything - it was the last piece of a chain that was already moving, and her own chase, her own authorization, her own dispatch from Ocipheus, had been stage five of something set in motion before she'd known there was anything to set in motion.

She went through it again. It did not change.

The eastern approach was staged. The columns were positioned. The seal was countersigned, sealed under review provisions that put off any notice until the operation was already underway. All that remained was the order.

She looked at the ember-glow, steady and indifferent, fed by exhaust heat and discharge and a manufacturing night that never stopped to consider whether it should. She looked at the wall, the fine new stone going up in the dark, the workers still at it, the mortar setting in the cold.

She looked east, toward the highland approach, where the columns waited for a word that had not yet come.

When the eastern sky began, by the barest degree the eye could not quite commit to, to separate itself from the highland above it, she put her hands in her pockets and looked once more at the valley - the brown water, the orange glow still holding its heat against the coming light - and did the last of her arithmetic for the night.

First light. The harbor. Whatever the city was watching for, they would know it by noon.

Ten days behind them. The rest of the road ahead. Schmalburg below, and beyond it, an army pointed in the direction she needed to go, waiting on an order that would arrive whether or not she was there to see it.

She went through the sequence once more, to be certain she had not missed anything.

She had not.

[Beat 14]
﻿THE REVIEW SESSION

---

She set the instruments in order before the second candle burned down to its mark. Three for the session itself, one for the supplementary materials intake, one for the disposition record, one for the archival certification. The sixth she left blank until the session's shape was known. She had learned, in three years of canonical scribing, that sessions rarely ended where they began, and a blank instrument was better than the wrong one already written out.

Chamber Three was cold in the way of rooms that held important things infrequently. The stone kept its cold against itself like a resource and spent it into the air in slow degrees. The brazier at the far wall was lit, but the ceiling ate the warmth before it reached the scribing table. She could feel her fingers, which was good. She could feel them precisely in the way that meant she would not feel them much longer if she sat still.

She did not sit still. She organized the instruments. She checked the registration stamps. She sharpened a second pen and set it beside the first, because something always happened to the first pen at exactly the wrong moment, and in three years she had learned to plan for its failure rather than be surprised by it.

The convocation, which she had herself copied three days prior, sat stamped and folded at the head of the table on the Canon Immaculate's side - not because Prelacze would need to refer to it. She had attended eight sessions he presided and had learned that he did not refer to instruments once a session opened; he carried the citations in his head. It sat there because the convocation at its designated position was procedurally correct, and Prelacze ran correct sessions.

The Canon arrived before the delegates. This, too, was correct. Presiders did not enter after the people they presided over. What she had not known the first time, and had since confirmed across eight sessions, was that he arrived at precisely the point in her preparation where everything was nearly ready but the room had not yet finished being warmed. She was fairly certain this was not deliberate. She was not entirely certain.

"Scribe Athen-Moor." He said it the way he said everything to her - as acknowledgment of a correctly arranged room, not as greeting. She had concluded some time ago that this was the most his tone would ever do in her direction, and that this was fine. She was furniture. Competent furniture.

"Canon." She kept her pen in hand.

He took his place at the table's head and reviewed the convocation without sitting - a man in his sixties who moved through institutional space the way certain rivers moved through stone, not rushing, not wasting effort, occupying whatever channel the room provided. He read it once, set it down at exactly its correct position, and said, "The Loera delegate will have prepared a brief. Allow her to finish reading it before you note the time." A pause, while he looked at the high window and its inch of grey light. "She has come three days by the coast road for this."

Vega wrote it on her working copy - *allow delegate to complete reading* - then crossed it out, because it was not her assignment to record what the Canon anticipated. Only what the session produced.

The delegates arrived together, which meant they had been kept waiting in the approach corridor and brought in at the same time - standard form for a contested review. She knew the Loera delegate from the registry files: Adnora Keist, nine years cataloguing the western strait approaches for the Three-Lantern House, the navigators' assembly that ran the coastal survey for every captain who wanted a route insured. Loera did not send delegates out of principle. It sent them when the shipping lanes were at risk, and the western sector was where half of Loera's western trade ran. The Carnyx commander she knew from his intake certification, filed six weeks past: Aldec Vorn, Carnyx Territorial Authority, Vigil sector eastern designation. He carried a scroll case with four rolls inside it. She knew the count because she had processed the inventory certification when it was logged at the station gate.

They came in with the careful arrangement of people who had been told to appear collaborative, and who had each decided, separately, that this was not entirely the wrong thing to be.

She dated the session instrument. She noted the attendees. She wrote the Canon's name and designation. She prepared to copy what was said.

---

Keist read her brief in twelve minutes. She had written it out in full, as Prelacze had anticipated, and she read it without looking up - which was not what frustrated witnesses usually did. Frustrated witnesses spoke. Keist read, which meant she had already decided that her frustration was not what would stand in the canonical record, and that the document was. A person who understood that much about archives was a person worth listening to.

The brief described the western sector's deterioration in survey terms: seventeen points along the perimeter, measured at eight-month intervals across three years. Eleven of the seventeen fell outside the established baseline range. Four of the eleven exceeded the canonical threshold the Liturgy's own vigil standards defined as acceptable variation. Keist cited the document number. She cited the page. She cited the threshold value. Her people had done their work, and it showed in the specificity - a delegate who did not need to raise her voice because the numbers were already doing it for her.

Vega copied all of it. The survey points. The variances. The citation. The threshold value.

Prelacze listened without expression.

Vorn's presentation was shorter. He unrolled the maps across the table - she had cleared the space in anticipation - and said, "Eastern sector survey. Current against the fifteen-year baseline."

That was all he said. The maps were the presentation.

She was not meant to study materials while they were being presented; her station was to the side and slightly back, and her assignment was the record of what was said, not the documents themselves. But she noted two things from where she sat, because she could not help noting them. The old survey line was drawn in faded brown ink and the new line in clean black, and between the two, in the central section of the eastern survey, the gap was wider than the station courtyard was long. And in three places along the southeastern passage, the black line had moved into ground that the old survey's own legend marked *maintained transit corridor, canonical approach authorization required.*

She looked at her table and wrote: *eastern sector survey presented, four sheets, submitted for archival. Current measurement against fifteen-year baseline. See submitted materials.*

She wrote what was there. That was her assignment.

"The Carnyx petition will have the maps entered into the session record," Prelacze said. He had not looked at them. He had been looking at the window. "Scribe."

"Canon."

"Noted in the record."

"Noted."

Keist said, in the flat voice of someone who had already spent her eloquence elsewhere: "The perimeter is not stable."

Prelacze turned from the window. "The Liturgy's vigil record spans seven hundred years," he said. "In that time it has documented thirty-one instances of boundary variation exceeding baseline threshold in one or more sectors. Twenty-eight self-corrected within twenty-four months. The remaining three showed continued movement over longer periods, and all three fell within the documented range for cyclical variation, per the vigil's own standards framework." A pause. "The current measurements fall within that range."

He said it without hesitation, with the particular cadence of a man reciting something true. She supposed it was true. She had not consulted the vigil record herself and would have no cause to unless assigned its archival processing, which she was not.

Keist said, "The transit corridors-"

"Are under review. The process for contested approach territories in Vigil-adjacent sectors requires formal assessment by the Order of the Watch's sector command, submitted to the canonical registry, and processed through the standard cycle. That process has been initiated."

He did not say *I initiated it.* He said *the process has been initiated.* In three years she had come to understand that this was not the same sentence.

Vorn said nothing further. He was rolling one of the maps back up with the deliberate care of a man who had decided this was not a room in which the maps would do further work today.

"Acknowledged," Keist said. The word landed flat on the table and stayed there.

Prelacze looked at her directly for a moment. "The Loera petition's documentation is noted in the record and will inform the sector review. This session has received and registered the presented materials." Without warmth, without coldness - the register of something institutional and final. "No immediate action is indicated. Continued monitoring protocols are authorized."

She wrote: *no immediate action indicated. Continued monitoring protocols authorized.*

She wrote: *Loera and Carnyx concerns noted in the record.*

She did not write: *the maps are in the archival queue and the lines on them say the cavity is larger.* That was not her assignment. Her assignment was the record of what was determined.

---

The delegates left first. Keist did not look at Prelacze on her way out, which was a controlled thing to do. Vorn paused where the maps had been spread - they were already in Vega's hands, session materials moving to the notary at close - looked at the empty table for a moment, and went.

Prelacze signed the instruments she presented at the table, not at corridor speed. He read each one before signing, unhurried, as he had never once been hurried about a canonical instrument in eight sessions, which was either a virtue or a very specific kind of confidence, and she had not yet decided which.

"The Loera delegate's documentation," he said, not looking up from the fourth. "Complete archival processing. Sector review intake will require cross-reference."

"I'll set it up."

He signed the fourth. He looked at the fifth. "The Carnyx materials."
