"I have them."

"Archived without action."

"Yes, Canon."

He signed the fifth, handed the set back, and went.

She stood at the table with the instruments in one hand and the map case in the other. The brazier had been burning three hours and the room was warmer than it had been, though she could no longer feel it as warmth - her body had gone numb past the point where it reported heat honestly, and she trusted neither the numbness nor the report that came after it.

She sat. She set the instruments aside and opened the case.

The maps were good parchment, careful work - the kind of cartography that did not leave room to argue with the line. The old survey was faded brown, hand-drawn, dated fifteen years back in the lower corner: before the secondary monitoring protocols, before the last border reclassification, before any of the paper she had spent three years handling. The new survey was clean black ink, same scale, same survey points, each marked with a small cross and a number that matched the measurement log on the second sheet. Triangulation references. Distances in standard canonical units. A stated margin of error, and a small one. Vorn's cartographers had not left room to argue with the line either.

In the northwestern sector, the current line sat two hundred and thirty meters east of the baseline. She checked the scale. The station courtyard - she walked it daily; she knew its dimensions - would fit inside that gap once, comfortably.

In the central sector, the gap was three hundred and eighty meters. The courtyard would fit twice.

In the southeastern passage, the one the old legend marked *maintained transit corridor, canonical approach authorization required,* the current line touched the corridor's inner boundary. Did not cross it. Touched it.

Touching was not crossing. She noted this honestly, because it was a meaningful distinction if you were building a canonical argument, and the maps were not building an argument. They had measured what was there and drawn what they found.

She rolled the first sheet closed, then the second, and set them in the case. In the disposition column she wrote: *archived without action, per Canon Immaculate session determination.* She sealed the case with the Registry stamp and placed it in the archival queue.

She picked up her pen and did not write anything for a moment.

She had been good at her work before she became too good at it. She was not certain when the two states had separated - somewhere in the second year, she thought, around the time she stopped seeing only the instruments and started seeing what the instruments were about. A notary was not supposed to be about content. A notary was supposed to be about form: the correct certification, the correct stamp, the correct procedure. Her procedure was excellent. It was not her function to decide what was correct to do about a map that showed a cavity three hundred and eighty meters larger than it had been fifteen years ago. That was the Canon's function. He had exercised it. She had written it down.

She was very good at this.

---

She was in the corridor outside the administrative wing, carrying the session instruments toward the registry for formal filing, when she heard them coming from the eastern approach.

Seven soldiers. Her mind catalogued them before she had decided to let it - three years of processing complement logs and supply authorizations had left her with the specific reflex of reading equipment the way she read certification stamps, automatically, out of long habit. Heavy weather-treatment on the outer layers, Vigil-approach rated. Boot construction cut for the eastern approach roads, the design she had filed requisition instruments for herself. Kit profile she knew from the evaluation summaries she had been copying since the sixth month: Descent Corps issue. Not garrison - she knew the garrison faces, and none of these were among them. Not Carnyx insignia either, though the route they were being walked along was the Carnyx corridor, the same eastern approach Vorn had just spent an afternoon arguing was measurably wrong. Possibly Carnyx-routed. She was not certain, and she wrote it that way in her head before she wrote anything anywhere else.

The factor guiding them through the administrative wing was someone she did not know, doing a routine procedure with the specific, deliberate carefulness of a person who had been told this particular routine was not routine.

The soldiers had the look of people who had been traveling - not the soft exhaustion of a long, aimless road, but the alert exhaustion of purposeful movement: a brief, a destination, and not yet the requirement to execute either. They moved economically. They looked at the chamber doors as they passed with the reflexive situational awareness of training that ran below conscious thought, and then they looked away.

She pressed herself to the wall to let them by. The corridor was narrow, and they went close enough that she could smell the road on them - cold and dust and the dry chill of high-ground travel, the smell she associated with the maintenance authorizations she processed but did not read closely, because engineering reports were not her assignment. The last soldier looked at her as he passed. Not suspicion. The kind of awareness that clocked her, filed her, and moved on without breaking stride.

She waited until they turned the corner. Then she went on to the registry.

---

The intake log for the specialist deployment was in the pending queue on her desk, delivered that morning, before the session. She picked it up.

*SPECIALIST DEPLOYMENT - SINTER ZONE APPROACH PREPARATION.*

She held the instrument. Seven names in the personnel column, standard roster format. Equipment certification attached. Authorization chain: a single reference number she did not immediately place, which meant a series she had not encountered before, which happened sometimes with restricted categories. She wrote the number on her working copy for later.

She read the category designation again.

She had seen *Sinter Zone* before, in maintenance authorizations - the infrastructure documentation for the approach roads, the survey-marker upkeep, the periodic replacement of the distance stones the winter frost split. In that context it was a terrain designation. The engineering reports described substrate conductivity anomalies and unusual compaction in the dry, careful language of people writing about ground and declining to write about what the ground was next to.

*Approach Preparation* was not a maintenance category.

She put the intake log in her bag and went to the library.

---

The library was the warmest room at Ocipheus Station. The braziers were lit at dawn and not let die until closing, and the room held its heat in the shelves in a way stone rarely managed. She had always appreciated this. She found tonight that she could not feel it in quite the same way, which was not the room's fault.

She looked up *approach preparation* under the operational taxonomy, canonical category: specialist deployment.

The definition was not long.

*Approach preparation: pre-staging designation for operations requiring formalized entry authorization for contested or boundary-adjacent territory. Classification: pre-incursion operational. Authorization level required: Canon Immaculate plenary. Documentation required: full canonical instrument set, canonical form series CI-7 through CI-14, signed by authorized representatives of all participating Houses and operating authorities. Required countersignature: canonical notary, Ocipheus Station or designated equivalent registry.*

She read it twice. She read it a third time - not because it was unclear, but because the first reading had only started the process of settling, and three years of handling instruments had taught her that understanding a thing and having it settle into her working knowledge were two different states. She was still in the first.

*Canon Immaculate plenary.*

The full canonical authority. No escalation, no appeal, no modification by House councils or regional bodies - the standing from which the Canon Immaculate acted in the Liturgy's name without requiring consent from the people the Liturgy served. She had processed eight sessions this year, and none of them were plenary sessions: not the sector reviews, not the inter-House coordination instruments, not the resource allocations. Those were advisory standing or co-signed authority, the deliberative machinery working the way it was supposed to work. Plenary authority was something else. She had seen the reference instruments. She had never needed to look up what the category itself required.

The CI-7 through CI-14 set she knew from her certification training: threshold formalities, attestation records, operational mandates, House confirmation instruments, an operational brief filed under seal, a designated authority certification, a deployment roster. And last, the instrument that made the whole set canonical.

*CI-14: Canonical witness certification. Required countersignature: canonical notary, Ocipheus Station or designated equivalent registry.*

The notary of record was her.

She sat in the library a long time. The warmth pressed in around her. Through the narrow window near the ceiling, the afternoon was going grey and cold in the way of afternoons in the Still Watch month, when the light left early and the cold came back to take its place.

She thought about what the plenary authority had been built for. Not in the abstract - she had copied enough instruments to know the history, or the version of it the canonical codex recorded. The standing had been established in Year 412, fifteen years after the first vigil installation at Ocipheus Station. The founding text cited it directly: *the authority of the Canon Immaculate shall be sufficient, in those moments when coordinated action is required and deliberative process cannot serve, to act in the name of all Compact Houses and their peoples.* The founding text specified, further, that the authority existed because of the Sinter vigil specifically - because in its first fifteen years the vigil had shown that what sat in the basin was not resolving, and that the Compact Houses, left to their own deliberation, could not agree on a response to a thing none of them had seen directly.

The plenary authority was built for Sinter. It had been built because the vigil kept showing the Houses something none of them wanted to see, and the founding canons understood that the day would come when the response required could not wait on consensus, and so they had built a lever for that day.

He was using it.

The session notation in the archival queue said: *no immediate action indicated.*

The intake log in her bag said: *Sinter Zone Approach Preparation.*

Both were correct. Both were signed. Both had been processed on the same day, by the same notary, and neither referenced the other.

Prelacze had looked at the window during the session. He had cited seven hundred years of record. He had said *within acceptable variation,* and the session had accepted his reading because the session did not have the standing to refuse the Canon's institutional word. He was not wrong that he held the authority. She had been in the room. She had written it down.

The maps in the queue below said the southeastern line touched the transit corridor. The measurement log had a small stated margin of error and had left no room to argue.

He was not wrong that he had the authority.

She was no longer certain the authority was being used for the thing it was built for. Those were different problems, and she sat for a while with both of them, because the canonical record had a column for the first and she did not know what to do with the second.

---

She walked back to her desk. The courtyard through the window was dark - she had been longer than she thought, which was the thing about understanding something: it took time you could not account for afterward. The time went into the understanding, and you came out of it with less evening than you had gone in with.

She processed the intake log correctly. Instrument type, canonical category, processing date, instrument reference number, notary initials. In the disposition column she wrote: *received for processing, authorization chain pending, filed pending instrument-set convocation.* She placed it in the archival queue, beside the map case and the session notation, three documents in adjacent slots that did not reference one another and were all, individually, correct.

Then she opened a new working copy - her own stock, her own ink, not the canonical record - and dated the top of the page with the care she gave every date she wrote, because a date was either correct or it was wrong and there was no comfortable middle position.

*Day 14, month of the Still Watch, 3rd year canonical.*

She wrote: *Review session, Chamber Three: inter-House delegation, Loera and Carnyx. Loera delegate Adnora Keist. Carnyx commander Aldec Vorn. Presided: Canon Immaculate Tomasz Prelacze.*

She wrote: *Session determination: no immediate action indicated. Continued monitoring protocols authorized.*

She wrote: *Carnyx maps, eastern sector survey, four sheets, archived without action per session determination. Central sector: current line three hundred eighty meters east of fifteen-year baseline. Southeastern passage: current line at the edge of the maintained transit corridor designation. The cavity is larger. The maps say so plainly.*

She looked at that last sentence. She had written it in the register of a fact, the same manner she wrote measurement values and certification dates. A notary wrote what she saw. She had been in the room. She had seen the maps. The cavity was larger. The maps had said so.

She kept writing.

*Specialist deployment intake, same date: seven soldiers, Descent Corps issue, Vigil-approach equipment, traveling condition purposeful, route possibly Carnyx-corridor. Category: Sinter Zone Approach Preparation. Canonical compendium cross-reference: pre-incursion operational, Canon Immaculate plenary authorization required. Instrument set: CI-7 through CI-14. Required countersignature: canonical notary, Ocipheus Station.*

*Cumulative pattern, month one through month eight:* she wrote, and then set down the pieces she had been carrying separately for so long that writing them in sequence felt like an act of undressing. *The Oculus Memoriae recovery instrument. Dame Lyra of Ocipheus's transit suspension. Loera and Carnyx sector reports, repeating cycle, no action indicated. Vigil garrison supply authorizations, aggregate tonnage up by nearly a quarter with no corresponding rise in complement. The autumn war-construct evaluation cohort - the courtyard is empty now, but it was not empty in the autumn - status: deployment authorization pending. Today's intake log. Today's session notation.*

The Oculus Memoriae recovery instrument was the one she had processed least easily, because she had once had cause to read what the canonical compendium said the Relic actually was. Not a weapon, whatever the corridor gossip called it. Not a power source. The compendium was specific: the one instrument at Ocipheus Station that did not lose what it saw, a lens that retained rather than merely looked - seven hundred and forty-one years of the crater filed as a single continuous memory, still accumulating. The recovery instrument itself was logged under Relic provenance review, dispute resolution language, the same procedural grammar that had, four hours earlier in Chamber Three, filed a doubling cavity as no immediate action indicated. A thing that remembered everything was a hazard to an office that needed a season's worth of forgetting to keep functioning. She had written that thought once, in the margin of an older working copy, and had not let herself finish it.

She had written each of these things before, in smaller pieces, in other working copies. She had never set them down in order on a single page.

She looked at the order.

She had been, she thought, trying not to see the shape of it - not because she had never read shapes before; she had spent three years learning to read little else. But the shapes in other people's decisions had always been navigable. You could hold them at the distance the instrument gave you. This shape was not navigable the same way. This shape had her sister's name in it.

She reached into the drawer and drew out the older working copy without needing to look for it - she knew it by feel, the fold she had put in it without meaning to, the particular grain of her own stock against the different weight of a canonical instrument. Her sister's name was in the margin of the third page, where she had written it the day she found it in the transit suspension: *Lyra Athen-Moor, Templar of Ocipheus, transit suspended effective -* and the date. She had crossed it out with one horizontal stroke, not hard enough to obscure it, only hard enough to mark that she had seen it. The name was legible through the line. She had known, drawing the line, that it would stay legible. Crossing it out was not the same as pretending she had not written it, which was not the same as pretending she had not found it, which was not the same as pretending that three years ago she had not been given work that would, eventually, require her to find it.

She looked at her sister's name for a moment. She put the copy back and closed the drawer without locking it.

On the new page she added one final line.

*CI-7 through CI-14 not yet convened. When convened: canonical witness certification required. Notary required.*

*I am the notary.*

She looked at the sentence. She did not cross it out.

She capped her pen and sat a moment with her hands loose in her lap, the way she sometimes sat at the end of very long days, when the body was tired and the mind had not yet finished with something it could not put down. Outside, the courtyard was dark. Somewhere in the archival queue, the session notation and the intake log and the sealed map case sat in their adjacent, correct, unreferencing slots.

He was not wrong that he had the authority. It had been built for Sinter - built for the moment the vigil had been watching for since Year 412, so the Caul could act when the Houses could not agree quickly enough to act for themselves. He was using it for what it was built for. That was the trouble with the sentence. It was true in exactly the way that made it useless to her.

She gathered her satchel and straightened the instruments in the queue with one precise motion, the last correct thing before a much longer night. She left the lamp burning on her desk - wasteful, and she did it anyway, because there was something on that page she did not want to leave entirely in the dark.

She walked out of the registry. The corridor was cold. Behind her, on the desk, the working copy lay open to the air, ink not yet dry on the last line.

The canonical record said what it was supposed to say.

What the working copy said was still drying.

[Beat 15]
﻿?SCHMALBURG - THE RAID; THE EATERS

Three long. Two short. Then again: three long, two short.

Lyra's hand found her hilt before her mind finished the count.

The Pallor border action, three years past - she had learned the pattern from an Atrament garrison officer who had learned it from a Fjorheim field manual captured eleven years before that, in a skirmish whose name she couldn't recall and whose dead she didn't know because she hadn't been there. She knew the signal the way she knew the notation for a restricted Catalyst preparation: not because she'd made the thing herself, but because she had read enough about its effects to recognize them before they finished arriving.

Fjorheim raid.

"Close," Orim said. He was consulting no instrument. Old men who had spent fifty years scraping their gift across the surfaces of things developed a feel for distance that was more reliable than apparatus and less reliable than luck, and that was still often enough to be worth having. "East quarter. The direction of the rail depot."

The street around them had gone from foot-traffic to empty in the time it took a person to process the sound and move. Schmalburg knew this bell. Had known it for eleven hundred years, or something like it - the gorge-city had been receiving Fjorheim attention since before the battle that gave it its name, and the name itself was the insult the raiders used when they withdrew on day forty-one after the Catalyst incendiary: *small fortress, small people.* The people of Schmalburg had kept the name. Had made armor of it. Had built a civil defense protocol around it that could empty a commercial street in under a minute.

The protocol put civilians in shelters and fighters at garrison points. Both options were wrong for Lyra's party, which was neither.

The first door she tried was already sealed, bar dropped on the inside. The second was locked before she reached it. The third was a stone passage leading downward - the civilian shelter route, which was exactly what the protocol intended.

"Garrison points are east," Ardea said. Her hand was not on her hilt. Ardea Marten kept her hands quiet in a fight, which was different from what most people meant when they said calm. It was a quality Lyra had catalogued in the first week and stopped cataloguing because it became part of the background. She had been posted to Ocipheus garrison eleven years before Lyra was born. The body carried that the way it carried bone - not visibly, but in everything. "They'll want bodies at the rail depot perimeter. The rigs deploy from the eastern yard."

"The rigs deploy and we're in the way."

"The shelters fill and we lose two hours." This was Orim, who was looking at a narrow passage between two forge-houses - smoke-blackened stone on both sides, wide enough for three people if two of them breathed in. "There is a third option."

Rowe said: "The passage goes west." Not the clipped, grudging half-answers he'd given her at first - somewhere in the last two weeks something had loosened. The delivery was still flat, still toneless, the tin voice giving every word the same weight, but the sentence came unprompted and delivered with the quiet certainty of someone reporting a fact he had already confirmed. He was watching the passage with the particular attention he gave to ground, reading the geometry the way a soldier reads geometry. "I can see the light at the far end."

"Yes," Orim told him, in the tone of someone who had already planned for the light being there. "Come."

---

The gorge-streets of Schmalburg were not designed for combat or for comfort. They had been designed for the forge-industry: high-walled, close, following the ridge geology at angles that made no navigational sense until you understood they were cooling channels, not roads - the city had grown up around the heat-management problem of running twelve hundred years of alchemical-industrial work in the same stone structure. The walls were black at the base and grey above the line where the heat rose, and the air at street level in the forge quarter had a quality of pressure that was not quite sound but not quite smell either - the cumulative residue of Heloth exhaust venting through the same corridors for generations.

She heard the fighting before she reached the junction. The bolt-action rifle had a distinctive report in enclosed stone: sharper than in open ground, the crack cutting back at itself off the walls so what reached your ears was the shot and its own echo arriving half a heartbeat apart. Atrament manufacture. She knew the specific overlap from the Vigil Station's range work.

But the frequency coming around the junction was wrong. Two variants, she catalogued in the first three seconds. One was Atrament. One was not.

"Fjorheim uses a longer action," Ardea said, beside her. "Their source Sphere runs a variant. Same Sphere, different generation."

The same source Sphere. Lyra filed the thought without analyzing it because there was a crossfire developing at the street junction thirty meters ahead and the time for analysis was not this one.

She pulled back. Ardea was already back. She looked for Rowe. He was at the near wall, pressed into a recessed forge door, weapon up. He had not been told to do this. He had read the geometry of the junction and found the position himself - seven months in Verlaine, which was real training even if it had been bought with his body without his consent.

The battle rigs arrived before the crossfire resolved.

She felt them before she heard them - the vibration in the stone underfoot, the particular frequency of a Heloth steam-chemical plant working under load. Then the sound: grinding and pneumatic, the controlled hydraulic hiss on the turns, the street-scrape of iron carrying serious weight. Three of them coming up the eastern approach. Three meters of purpose-built Atrament armor, each one designed to be the last thing in an enclosed space still functional when the problem was solved. The BR-designation series. She had reviewed the program summaries for the Vigil Station's threat assessments. The rigs were not fast; they were not designed to be fast. They were designed to be indestructible in the specific architecture of Schmalburg's gorge-streets, which made them exactly as capable as the gorge-streets required.

The lead rig reached the junction and solved the first firing position - not by any mechanism she could have described precisely, but solved it in the way the rigs solved problems in gorge-street work: by being the largest and most armored thing present, and by having a pilot who knew which of those two qualities to deploy first.

The Fjorheim position changed character. Anti-armor payload, which was a calculated bet. The lead rig took the round at the wrong angle and too-close range for the round's design parameters. Not dead - the rigs didn't die from one anti-armor round, which was the point of their construction - but the impact drove something into the housing, and the rig's movement became asymmetrical. Still moving. Still working. But wrong in its left side in a way she could see even at thirty meters and through the smoke.

"Left forearm housing," Ardea said. "Round caught the coupling joint. You can see it in the stride."

She could see it now that it was named. The rig's left arm hung differently - not loose, wrong in its load-bearing.

The crossfire resolved, as crossfires do. Not with clarity or justice but with the sudden absence of one of the things making noise, replaced by a different kind of noise that was harder to categorize.

That was when the smell reached her.

---

She knew what it was before she understood why she knew it.

The Sinter crawlers' biological Catalyst compound had a specific signature - acrid and organic, the smell of the membrane's own biology being processed through a human system over years. She had read this in the Vigil Station's field reports as an academic fact, recorded by a researcher who had observed from sufficient distance to find the fact interesting rather than immediate. She had not expected to identify it by smell, from thirty meters, in the forge-city's exhaust-laden air.

She identified it.

There were four. They moved at a pace that was wrong for the space - too efficient, too certain of the ground, like something that had memorized this architecture without having used it before. They wore the layered work-cloth of Fjorheim's forge-villages, practical, nothing that announced itself, but what the cloth covered moved differently than cloth was designed to accommodate.

The leader stopped at the junction.

She was taller than multiple unsanctioned infusions without a certified practitioner should have produced - the body, without guidance, had apparently organized the change around structural necessity rather than Liturgy protocol, and what resulted was this. The Liturgy classified the result as abomination. Lyra had written that classification into her own threat assessments, four times, in the formal notation.

She had written it wrong.

The skin was wrong. Not monstrous. Not corrupted. Wrong in the specific way that something is wrong when it has been reorganized past the point where what it became still belonged to the same category as what it was. The Transmutation, taken without the prescribed attenuation between doses, had rebuilt the surface tissue into something that carried out skin's function - keeping the interior interior - but wasn't skin in the technical sense. In motion, particularly at the joint-interfaces, you could see the underlying structural work beneath it. Not organs. Architecture. The thing the body had rebuilt itself around.

But what Lyra's eye kept returning to, even at thirty meters, even through the smoke, was the neck.

The musculature had been reorganized - additional support structures grown in, visible under the skin where they stood proud in a way the standard anatomy had no room for. You could count them. Four, possibly five, where there should have been two. And the head tracked on a range of motion the human neck did not provide, without strain, without effort, like something that had always worked this way rather than something that had learned to. When the leader turned to look at the far end of the junction, her head moved through an arc that Lyra's hand instinctively wanted to record because the record would be evidence of something the Liturgy's classification system had no category for.

The Eaters had not come for the raid. The raids drew things from the Sinter perimeter's overflow cracks - membrane fauna that the quarantine never fully contained. That was what the Eaters had tracked here: the overflow creature the raid's noise had pulled through its boundary and into the city. They had followed their prey through the noise.

Lyra had known this, academically, from the field reports. What the field reports had not conveyed was the silence. The gorge-street was a noisy place - exhaust vents, bell-echo, the bass note of the rigs through the stone - and the four Eaters standing at the junction made the space around them quiet. Not the quiet of shelters filling. A different kind of quiet, the kind made by everything with a brain stem and an intact survival instinct removing itself from consideration.

The leader moved into the junction. The other three spread to the walls. Lyra heard - she would record this later, in the same notation she used for everything - a sound that had no reference in her research archive. Not a cry. Not a body impact. Something between a collapse and a release, the kind of sound a structure makes when the thing holding it together from the inside stops. The sound came from beyond the junction. It came once.

Then quiet.

"We should not be between them and where they are going," Orim said, from immediately behind Lyra's left shoulder, which was where he had been for the past three minutes without her having noticed it.

Lyra moved.

---

Orim had been scrying during their approach - she had seen him wet his fingertips with the contact sensitizer, press them together at the sternum, look at nothing for twenty seconds. She had not interrupted him. The minor scry took less than half a minute and the results were always more useful than the information she could have given him about the gorge-streets, because Orim did not look at roads but at what had used roads recently.

What he had found was the dockyard corridor: a service passage running between the secondary rig yard and the river loading bays, wide enough for Heloths moving river cargo, roofed over with iron lattice and tarpaulin, long enough that the far end disappeared into industrial haze. The Fjorheim withdrawal had pushed north along the eastern streets when the rigs drove them back. The Eaters had moved northeast, following the overflow creature. The corridor was the only route west that avoided both.

"There is a position at the far mouth," Orim said, when they reached the corridor entrance. His voice carried no inflection that would have suggested urgency to anyone who did not know him. "Two Fjorheim fighters, possibly three. They found the corridor in the last twelve minutes and they have the distance advantage." He paused. "The corridor is ninety meters long and has no cover."

"We take the position," Ardea said. She was reading the angles. "Move fast, split the approach before they can-"

"Three against three, in a roofed corridor with no cover," Rowe said. He was at her shoulder, looking past her at the far mouth. He had made the count without being asked and had delivered the number with the flat precision of someone who had done this calculation before. "And they hear us coming before we are halfway."

Orim had not moved from the entrance. His hand had found the Rod without her seeing him reach for it - he had the Rod the way other men had a walking stick, present without announcement. He was rotating the mineral tip in the iron housing, feel-first, a locksmith running his thumb down the tumblers. The groove found the notch.

"I catalogued this site eleven years ago," he said. "Sphere eleven. A cave complex in the deep rock of this sector. Colonial fauna, membrane-adjacent." He looked at the floor of the corridor at the point he had identified, which Lyra could not see but understood to be the membrane boundary at its thinnest in this location. "My documentation describes the fauna as: large colonial invertebrates, membrane-sensitive, aggressive on emergence." He stopped rotating the mineral tip. His thumb rested over the trigger. "The word *aggressive* is doing a great deal of work in that documentation."

Lyra looked at the corridor. Ninety meters. The Fjorheim position at the far end. The Eaters somewhere northeast and moving with purpose.

"Orim."

"Step back," he said. "Behind the entrance. Both of you. Specifically, Mr. Rowe, please step back." He said this the way he said everything: without drama, with the exact weight the information required and no more. He had already decided. He was giving them the distance they needed before he enacted the decision.

She stepped back behind the entrance wall. Ardea was beside her. Rowe stepped back without being told twice; he had learned what *step back* meant from Orim in specific.

Orim depressed the trigger.

The aperture opened four meters into the corridor, at the near end.

She could see the skin from the entrance - the membrane tissue stretched across the air in the shape of a wound, translucent, threaded with dark veins that pulsed once, twice, three times as the pressure built on the far side. The corridor's industrial haze made it visible in a way that open air would not have: the thing was there, in the middle of the passage, bulging slightly at the center where something pressed against it from the far side, wet and gold even through the haze - amber deepening to a burnt-honey dark at the heart of the swell - from somewhere a great deal deeper than the Schmalburg dockyard went.

The veins went dark. The skin parted, and for the half-second before anything came through it, the wound behind it caught the corridor's haze-light and threw it back gold - warm, clinging, gone the instant the first shape crossed it.

What came through came through all at once, slicked and glossy with the crossing, as if the wound had wrung it through wet before it let go. She had understood from Orim's previous explanations that things came through apertures sequentially, constrained by the opening's diameter. She had understood the aperture was small. Neither of those things prepared her for the way the creature organized itself in the two seconds after emergence - through the opening and oriented before the skin had finished parting behind it, like something that had been waiting at exactly this pressure point and had been very ready for the door to open.

It was pale as chalk all over. Not the pale of something underpigmented but the pale of something that had lived at such depth in complete dark for long enough that pigment had become a wasted resource and the body had reorganized accordingly. The legs - she began to count them and stopped, because the count was not the relevant fact in this moment - were too many for any architecture she had researched, and they moved in a coordination that had no reference in her existing library of locomotion. Not rippling, not alternating, not anything the Caul's natural history had produced a name for. They moved and the creature moved with them, and what struck her before anything else was the certainty. Not aggression - something worse than aggression, because aggression had urgency in it, and this had none. The settled, efficient attention of a thing that occupied the top of its environment so completely that threat was a category it had no practical experience of.

The eyes were on the sides of the broad, flat head, arranged in a curve - three to each side, she counted them, six - and they reflected the corridor's dim light wrong, throwing it back at an angle that was not the angle of reflection her reference material had given her for any compound eye structure she had studied. They found the far end of the corridor the way a surveyor's instrument finds a fixed point: without hurry, without uncertainty, with the quality of a thing that sees the problem and begins moving toward it in the same motion.

It moved toward the far end of the corridor.

Orim had stepped back from the entrance and turned away from it. He was counting under his breath. She could see his lips moving. Not prayers - he was not a praying man. He was counting seconds. He had forty-five of them, and he knew the count, and he had turned away because there was nothing he could do about the creature now and because he had learned, in fifty years, that watching what he opened the door for did not make it clean. It just made him the one who had watched.

The party moved.

"West wall," Ardea said quietly. "Against the stone. Fast and keep pace."

They entered the corridor. Lyra against the west wall, Ardea in front, Rowe behind, Orim last. The creature was thirty meters ahead and closing the distance to the far end with the certainty she had clocked at the entrance. The corridor haze was thicker here - the tarpaulin roof trapped the exhaust heat and it sat at chest height in a warm chemical layer that smelled of decades of Heloth discharge.

At the twenty-meter mark she heard the Fjorheim position react. Not the organized fire she had expected. The sound that came from the far mouth was the sound bodies make when the body and the mind disagree about whether to continue the current process. Then silence, briefly. Then something that was not silence so much as the absence of organized resistance, which was a different thing.

She kept pace.

At the thirty-five-meter mark she heard the sound again - the one she had heard from beyond the junction, the sound her research archive had no entry for, the sound a structure makes when what is holding it together from the inside stops. It came from the far end of the corridor. It was not quick.

She looked at the west wall and kept walking.

At the forty-five-meter mark the creature was at the far mouth. Not through it. At it. The tarpaulin overhead snapped once - something in the creature's transit had disturbed the air pressure in the enclosed space. Lyra felt it against her face.

At sixty meters Orim's count stopped. She heard no sound from the corridor's far end. She did not look back to establish why.

At ninety meters they came out into the rig yard.

Orim came out last. He put the Rod inside his coat, against his chest, where the mineral was warm. He looked at the ground for a moment. Then he looked at Lyra.

"The aperture closed at forty-seven seconds," he said. "Within normal parameters."

She nodded. She was not going to ask about the Fjorheim fighters. She had the data she needed and the data she needed was: the corridor was clear.

Rowe was looking back at the corridor entrance. His face had the expression it had when he was working something through before he was ready to give it words. He looked at Orim. He said: "That creature - it died in there?"

"Yes." Orim's voice was even. "They all do, when they come through. The atmosphere is wrong for them. Everything here is wrong for them. They have forty-five seconds before the biology fails." A pause. "I find that the specific number of seconds is not the detail that stays with people."

Rowe looked at the corridor entrance for another moment. Then he turned and looked at the yard.

"There is still movement, north," he said. "The withdrawal. They have not all gone."

He was right.

---

The rig yard was controlled chaos, which was better than the gorge-street's uncontrolled variety. Two of the three Atrament rigs had returned to refit; the damaged one, arm still hanging wrong at the coupling, stood at the forge-house wall while a crew worked the housing with hand tools and a lamp on a long cable. Forge-workers were moving equipment out of the rig paths. Someone had lit the ember-fungi lamp posts at the yard's perimeter - the orange bioluminescent glow turned everything into a composition of industrial light and shadow, the machines large and patient in it, the people small and purposeful.

In the scrap corner past the secondary rig bay, where the yard keepers piled what wasn't worth the sorting, something crouched low against the wall that Lyra's eye passed over twice before it registered as a shape rather than debris. Four-legged, low, wide across the shoulders, its forelimbs folded flush against its underside in a way that made them look, for a moment, like nothing so much as a stack of scaffold iron somebody had leaned there and forgotten. No unit stencil on the housing. No BR designation. Older stock, she judged, from the pitting on what plating she could see - the kind of thing a quartermaster stops re-numbering because nobody expects it to move again.

She catalogued it as scrap and kept walking. Rowe's optical receptor held on it a half second longer than the rest of the yard warranted. She noticed that, filed it without a category, and moved on, because the Fjorheim withdrawal's northern element had pushed through the yard's edge in the last twenty minutes and the evidence was in the brass shell casings scattered near the northern rail guide and the wide margin the forge-workers gave the northern quarter, and that was the more urgent fact.

Lyra read the yard and read the western approach: clear to the cooling channel, which was what she needed.

"We move for the channel," she said.

---

Ardea died in the Helothworkers' Lane, which ran between the main rig-yard access road and the secondary cooling channel behind the third forge complex. Lyra would record the coordinates later, the specific coordinates, because she was a researcher and recording was what she did. She would record the date, the time, the cause. She would not record what it felt like to hold Ardea Marten while Ardea Marten stopped being a person and became something the stone would keep its own record of.

They were moving for the secondary cooling channel when the Fjorheim withdrawal - the organized element, the one with a professional rear-guard - repositioned across the lane's northern end. Not breaking. Redirecting, in the manner of professional forces who redirect when something indestructible pushes into their crossfire. They had found the lane's junction and established a firing line covering the withdrawal's flank.

Rowe saw the geometry before anyone told him it was there. He had never received tactical instruction in this city's terms, nothing that would have taught him what the lane's junction meant. He had seven months of Verlaine training and three years of France before that, and both of them had built the same reflex into the understructure of his thinking: a line of fire had a solution, and the solution was someone who moved into the angle that collapsed it.

He moved toward the junction.

Ardea moved faster.

Thirty years of service in the body's memory, which was older and more reliable than the mind's. The body went to the right position before the mind consulted the rest of the available information - which was: this was an outdoor lane, the lane gave the crossfire limited cover, the lane gave the party even less, and there were four of them and two of the party had already had this idea. The body filed none of that and moved.

The round took her through the upper chest, left side, below the collar. She was already pulling Rowe back when it found her. The pull became a push. She went against the forge-house wall and sat down against it, hard, with the specific ungainliness of a body that has stopped being coordinated from the inside.

Lyra was there in - she did not count.

Ardea's eyes were open and tracking. That was the thing that mattered for the first three seconds: she was tracking Lyra's face. She was not speaking. Lyra put her hand on the wound without looking at it because looking was not the useful thing here and the hand was.

"Sergeant," Lyra said.

Ardea looked at her.

The blood came through Lyra's fingers at a rate that told her what she needed to know. She had administered enough field tinctures to read a rate. She was reading it now, and what it told her was not something she had a response to that would be useful, because her kit was in her satchel and getting to it required removing her hand from the wound, and removing her hand from the wound was not going to help with what removing the hand would reveal.

Ardea said one word. Not Lyra's name. It was the name of someone Lyra did not know - someone from before the current posting, from somewhere east, possibly still alive in the ordinary way that people who are not on research expeditions in foreign cities during Fjorheim raids remain alive. She said it without urgency, with the quality of someone placing a face they have just recognized. Then she did not say anything else.

She died the way people die. No declaration. No moment of clarity in the medically instructional sense. The body finishes what it is doing, and what it was doing was: working, then not working, and the distance between those two states is not marked by anything you can point to. Lyra had known this. She had not been holding the person before.

Thirty seconds. Perhaps forty.

Rowe was at the far wall. He had understood what happened before it finished happening - the sequence was not ambiguous in any language - and he was looking at the space where Ardea had been with an expression Lyra had no file for. Not grief in the Liturgy-document sense. Something older. She noted it. She noted it the way she noted things when she was functioning correctly, which she was. She would return to it when she had the resources to be human about it. She would return to it for the rest of her life, as it turned out, but that was not information she had yet.

She let go. Her hands were red to the wrist.

The Fjorheim firing line had not stopped. The rounds were still coming, high now, searching, the professional rear-guard's discipline holding even as the withdrawal it was covering thinned out ahead of it. Lyra was against the wall with a dead woman's blood drying on her hands and no cover between the lane and the junction that was not the wall itself.

"We move when the position clears," she said, because it was a sentence and she needed one.

"It is not clearing," Orim said. He was in the doorway across the lane, watching the junction. His voice had not changed, but something in the set of him had. "They have the geometry and they are not spending it."

---

Rowe had not moved from the wall.

Lyra looked at him because looking at him was easier than looking at Ardea, and what she saw was wrong in a new way, a way that did not belong to grief. His shoulders had gone level in a manner no living posture holds for more than a breath. The single oval receptor that did the work of his face had stopped tracking the junction, stopped tracking the lane, stopped tracking anything she could locate a referent for. He was looking at nothing with the specific attention of something that had found the thing it was looking for and no longer needed the eyes to hold it.

Then he folded down against the wall where he stood.

Not a fall. A fall has weight arriving somewhere it did not intend to arrive, and there is always a fraction of a second of the body trying to prevent it. This had none of that. The rifle slid from his hands and settled rather than clattered, the way an object settles when nobody is holding it with intention. He went down along the wall in stages, knees, hip, shoulder, with the economy of a marionette whose strings have been paid out rather than cut, until he was a small black shape folded against the stone with one oval lens catching the ember-fungi light and giving nothing back.

"Rowe." Lyra had her hand on his shoulder-joint before she had decided to move. Nothing under her hand responded to being touched. It had the particular stillness of a thing that has been switched off rather than a thing that is dying, and she had catalogued dying twice today and this was not that.

"Don't." Orim was already crossing the lane, faster than she had seen him move in the two years she had known him. "Don't try to wake him and don't move him more than you have to. Whatever this is, he is still doing it. Kneel over him. Sword up. Guard the shell."

"Guard it from what-"

"I don't know," Orim said, which she had never once heard him say about anything, and the honesty of it did more to move her than a plan would have. "I know what fear looks like on a fifty-year-old scrying arm, and I am telling you: guard him."

She knelt over the small black shape with her sword up and her back to the junction fire, which put her body between the Fjorheim position and the thing on the ground that had, one breath ago, been the third member of her party, and she did not have time to decide whether this made sense before the yard answered the question for her.

---

Across the lane, past the yard's edge, in the scrap corner where nothing had moved all evening, the four-legged shape came on.

There was no hum first. No light, no warning report from a housing built to announce its own activation the way the BR rigs did. There was only weight changing - the specific violence of mass deciding to move that had, a heartbeat before, been furniture. The forelimbs unfolded from where they had lain flush along its underside, and what came free of that fold was not a manipulator arm and not a rig's clumsy grasping claw but two curved blades the length of a broadsword each, serrated on the inner edge the way a working scythe is serrated before it ever touches a field, hinged at what would have been a shoulder if the thing had been built on any plan Lyra's anatomy training recognized. A mantis prayed with forelimbs built to that same geometry, on an insect small enough to sit on a woman's palm. This was the geometry scaled to something that could put a scythe through a wall.

It rose onto four legs, low and wide, the shoulders carrying the weight the way a bear carries weight - not tall, not proud of its height, built instead around the specific promise of a charge that does not need distance to become fast. Down its spine, plate by articulated plate, a tail unfolded and rose in an arch above the haunches, narrowing over its last yard to a barbed, curved point that was not decorative, because Atrament did not build decoration into siege iron. It was a housing. It was built to put something into whatever it touched.

It stood the height of the BR rigs and wore the height like an apology, low-slung and coiled instead of towering, and it turned its blunt iron head - no optical housing she could identify, nothing that looked back at her the way Rowe's small shell's single lens did - toward the junction where the Fjorheim rear-guard held its firing line.

Lyra had catalogued the Eaters' silence an hour before. This was a different silence: the silence of the yard's other machines, the two refitting rigs and their crews, going still around something none of them had authorized and none of them could countermand.

It moved.

She had seen the BR rigs move, deliberate, load-bearing, the pace of something built to be indestructible rather than fast. This did not move like that. It came off its haunches into the junction the way water comes off a broken retaining wall - committed the instant it started, no interval between deciding and arriving - and the first Fjorheim gunner had time to turn his weapon a quarter of the necessary arc before a scythe forelimb took him through the trunk and the firing line's discipline ended in the specific way discipline ends, which is not gradually.

The second gunner ran. The tail came down.

Lyra did not watch closely enough to record it in her later notation and made no attempt to correct that afterward. Some records did not need to be complete to be accurate.

The rear-guard's third position broke and did not reposition. The rounds stopped coming into the lane.

The quiet that followed was not the Eaters' quiet, the quiet of prey removing itself from consideration. It was the quiet of a firing line that no longer existed to make noise.

Something else moved in the margin of it - a single Fjorheim skirmisher, cut off from the collapsed rear-guard, running the wrong way in the dark and finding the mouth of the Helothworkers' Lane instead of the open street he wanted. He came around the corner with a sidearm already up and found, four feet in front of him, a woman kneeling over a small motionless black shape with her sword already swinging. She put him down before he had finished understanding what he had walked into, and knelt back over Rowe's shell with her arm shaking and her sword wet, and did not move again until the yard told her it was finished.

The four-legged thing stood in the ember-fungi light at the center of what had been a firing position, tail still raised, scythe forelimbs slack at its sides, and did nothing further, because there was nothing further in the junction that required it.

Then it went still, the way a held breath goes still before it is let out, and folded itself back down - forelimbs drawing in, tail lowering plate by plate, the crouch returning to something that looked, from thirty meters, like scrap again.

The small black shape under Lyra's guard took a breath.

It was not a mechanism she could name doing it. It was simply that one moment the shoulder-joint under her palm was inert stock and the next it had the particular give of a body remembering it had weight to hold. Rowe's head came up. The oval receptor found her face and held it the way it always did, and for three full seconds he did not move beyond that, a thing recalibrating itself against the fact of having a body again.

"M-101," he said.

It was, as it had been every time she had heard him say it, the one thing in her language he produced without a single wrong stress, without a gap between the thought and the sound.

---

She did not ask him what had happened. She had catalogued enough tonight to know that the asking would not produce an answer either of them could use yet, and that the not-asking was, for the moment, the more honest response available to her.

Orim came to stand over both of them, looking at the folded scrap-shape across the yard and then down at Rowe, and said nothing for a while, which from Orim was its own kind of statement. Then: "I have scried a great many things in fifty years. I have never scried that." He said it the way he said everything uncomfortable - without decoration, because decoration was not what the fact needed.

Rowe got his legs under him with the same careful, testing motion of someone checking that all the parts had come back attached correctly. He looked at the four-legged shape in the scrap corner for a long moment. Whatever he was assembling behind the single lens, he was not ready to give it words, and Lyra had learned by now that a shell gone quiet was not a shell with nothing behind it.

"Cannot leave it," he said finally, nodding once toward the shape. "If claimed. Cannot-" He stopped. The rest was not going to come, not tonight, and he let it go rather than force it.

"We're not leaving it standing in the middle of a rig yard for a forge crew to find in the morning," Lyra said, which was not quite what he had meant and was close enough to be useful. "Can you put it back where it was?"

He could. It took him longer than she expected - long enough that she understood, without being told, that whatever had happened tonight had a cost measured in something other than time, and that time was the cheapest part of the bill. When the shape had folded itself back into the scrap corner and gone as still and unremarkable as it had been an hour ago, he came back across the yard on his own legs, unsteady in a way she had not seen from him before, and did not say anything else.

---

She sat down beside Ardea Marten's body.

The Eaters had pulled off when the Fjorheim raid broke, withdrawing northeast in the specific manner of hunters who have finished their business and are not interested in ceremony about it. The thing from the Sinter overflow was somewhere in the east yard, dead. She had heard the sound it made when it died, which resembled nothing in her reference archive, and had filed it: *membrane overflow fauna, unidentified; Eater of Monsters as effective countermeasure, confirmed.* The Liturgy would want that data. She was not certain what she thought about being the one who carried it back, and she was even less certain what notation she would use for the thing that had come out of the scrap corner, and whether the Liturgy had a category for it at all, or whether she would be the one who had to invent one.

The bells had shifted from raid-warning to damage-assessment. The forge-yards were running again - the exhaust-pressure hum was back in the stones underfoot. Schmalburg had the quality she had observed once before, nine years ago, after a different Fjorheim incursion in the capital's secondary trade district: not calm, not controlled, but the quality of a population that has done this before and knows the shape of the next four hours and has arranged itself around that knowledge. Schmalburg had been *small fortress* for eleven hundred years. It knew what it was.

She took out Ardea Marten's service record.

Twelve pages, close notation, Ocipheus garrison format - date of enlistment, posting history, distinguished service marks, the research-secondment authorization that had moved Ardea from garrison duty to Lyra's party. The authorization was dated six months ago. Lyra had signed it. Her signature was at the bottom of the document, which meant her hand had been present at every step of the chain that had brought Ardea Marten to the Helothworkers' Lane.

She did not think about the chain. Thinking about the chain was not useful to the work of closing the record.

She filled in the date, the location, the cause. *Enemy action, crossfire withdrawal, Fjorheim raid, Schmalburg.* Formal notation. She signed the closing entry with her name and rank: Dame Lyra of Ocipheus, Templar, Liturgy of the Canon Immaculate.

The form's printed header ran across the top two lines of every service record page, in the canonical font pressed small enough that it did not compete with the content: *In service to the Canon Immaculate and the Liturgy's stewardship mission, the practitioner dedicates their body and their gift to the maintenance of the vigil, the protection of the membrane, and the lawful passage of all that moves between Spheres, for the benefit of the Caul and all it holds. Their service is their faith. Their faith is the record.*

She had copied this header into two previous closing forms without reading it. She read it now, with the blood still drying on her hands and the ink of the cause notation still wet. The header addressed the practitioner in the third person: *their,* not *your.* The institution did not speak to the person directly. It referred to them. As a category the record held.

The Liturgy had a theory about loss and its management and the process by which the living return to function. She had read it in the foundational documents, and she had never found fault with the theory before, because she had never before held the person between the person being alive and the person being a thing the stone kept record of. The theory addressed the aftermath. It had nothing to say about those thirty seconds. This was not a gap she had noticed before. It was there now. She filed it. She would return to it when she had a category for it.

She put the record back in the folio. Third from the top.

Rowe was across the lane, back against the wall, his rifle across his knees, not because he had recovered it but because Orim had put it there for him while his hands were still learning to be certain of themselves. He was looking at the place where Ardea had been, which was three feet away and permanently out of reach, and he was looking at it the way people look at things that are no longer there when looking is the only useful thing left. His face was still doing the thing she had no notation for. She looked at it and let it be what it was.

Orim closed the document folio for her when her own hands did not move to do it, and put it back in the satchel, and settled the satchel on her shoulder himself. He did not say anything about the scrap corner again. She understood that he would, eventually, and that eventually was not tonight.

She stood. She picked up Ardea's rifle from the ground where it had fallen and checked the mechanism from habit and put it over her shoulder. It was a weapon. It was functional. Leaving it served nothing.

Rowe's head came around toward the rifle riding her shoulder - not toward her face, the way an eye tracks a sound rather than a person - and when he spoke, the words did not come in his own order at all.

"Same as mine," he said. Then, with no pause where she had learned to expect one, in a register she had not heard from him before, flat and complete and entirely without the gaps that were, until this moment, the only thing she had ever known his voice to have:

> MAKE: Verlaine-issue bolt action, four-round magazine.
> SPHERE-SOURCE: catalogue match - Verlaine armory stamp, off-Caul manufacture.
> CONDITION: service wear consistent with 4+ years' issue. Coupling sound. No corrosion.
> LOAD: two rounds remaining. Chamber clear.

The voice shut off the way it had opened - no transition, a door closing under its own weight. Rowe's hand came up halfway toward his own chest, an aborted gesture at nothing, and the single lens did not find her again for a full second.

"That was not-" He stopped. Started over, and this time the halt belonged to him, human and searching, the words coming slower than the flat tin cadence that usually carried them. "The chassis. Did that. Not me. Did it to me again."

Not Fjorheim, and not Atrament either. Ardea had spoken of both an hour ago in the gorge-street, in her own voice, with her hands quiet at her sides - Fjorheim's long action against Atrament's shorter one, the same Sphere-source running two generations apart. She had never once said a word about the make she carried herself. Now the chassis had told Lyra where it came from instead, in no voice at all, cold and correct and grieving nothing, and the place it named was the one Rowe had been carried out of.

"West," she said.

Rowe looked up. His eyes moved to her, then to the yard, then back, the way they had before tonight and the way, she understood now, they would keep doing - the gap between what he had just shown them and what he could say about it not closing tonight, maybe not closing for a long time yet. He offered nothing that explained it. It was enough that he stood, and fell into step beside her, and did not look back at the scrap corner.

They walked out of the yard on their own legs, three of them where there had been four that morning, and the gorge-street outside had the quality of a city already composing itself around what had happened today and what would happen next - forge-workers moving an alchemical condenser back into its shed, a garrison soldier directing traffic at the eastern access road, a child at the mouth of a shelter, watching the yards, deciding whether it was finished. The ember-fungi posts cast their orange light across the cooling channels and the rig paths and the places where things had happened an hour ago that the city was already adjusting its arrangements to account for.

Lyra walked west, and did not look back at the lane, and did not look back at the yard, and carried both of them anyway, the way she understood now she would carry them for as long as there was a her left to do the carrying.

[Beat 16]
﻿THE FORGE HEARTH - WHAT SHE RECOGNIZES

The volcanic substrate had pushed up through the alpine soil in long dark extrusions - finger-shaped, basalt-colored, warm to the touch even through the cold morning. Lyra pressed her palm against one and left it there. The warmth rose through her hand steadily, without the variation of something that had merely spent a day in daylight. It came from below, continuous, moving upward because that was the direction things moved when they had nowhere left to go but up.

She knew what the Forge Hearth zone was before she arrived. She had read the institutional documents - the extraction reports, the geothermal surveys, the authorization history that ran in renewal intervals from Year 842 onward. Knowing had not prepared her for the particular quality of the warmth, which was less like heated stone than like a fever: persistent, patient, unaware of being noticed.

She took her hand away. The warmth stayed on her palm for a breath's worth of time before the autumn air took it back.

Orim walked behind her, hands loose at his sides, fingertips already faintly restless in the way that meant he was reading what was under the ground rather than watching where he put his feet. He had crossed this approach six times in thirty years, he had told her once, on a barge, in a different season, and he had said it the way he said most things about his own history: as a fact filed for later use rather than an offer of company.

Rowe walked on her other side, rifle slung, hands loose. He was watching the extraction valley below with the quiet, methodical attention he gave to everything he had not yet decided was worth remarking on. Six weeks since Bressant. He had been fluent in Caul since the shell first gave it to him - complete sentences, whether he wanted them or not - and he could have built any sentence the moment required. He rarely chose to. He spent what he was willing to spend, carefully, and no more.

Below, the extraction roads ran the switchback geometry Atrament used for heavy equipment access, carved into the volcanic rock. Heloth maintenance constructs moved on them at the smooth, unhurried gait of machines that had made the route enough times that the route had become part of them somewhere - not in a mind, because they did not have minds, but in whatever the Catalyst preparation put into the movement instead. Ember-fungi clustered orange at every maintenance station along the way, bioluminescent, drawn to the chemical exhaust the constructs vented at each cycle. At this distance they looked like small fires. They had looked like small fires for roughly four hundred years, which was how long the constructs had been working this section of the approach.

*

The warmth that came from the south was different.

She noticed it by increments - not a moment of recognition so much as an accumulation of evidence that produced, eventually, something she could no longer decline to acknowledge. The stone under her boots was warm because geothermal heat conducted upward through it; that she understood. The warmth from the south did not travel through stone. It arrived the way a sound arrives from a direction, dispersing, carrying something of its origin's character all the way to where she stood. She felt it in the chest cavity, below the sternum, in the same register where the Sinter tone had its address.

This was not the Sinter tone. The Sinter perimeter felt like standing in front of something that was awake and had noticed you. This felt like standing in front of something that had been asked to keep working long past the point where it had the strength to work. Not menace. Not attention. The specific exhaustion of a system running on the wrong side of its limits, because stopping would have required someone to make a decision nobody wanted to make.

She was a Transmutation researcher before she was anything else the Liturgy had made her. Membrane biology - wound-surface tissue properties, the recovery behavior of frequently opened sites, the long-term condition of substrate under sustained apparatus use. Four papers published, two of them cited in standing operating procedure. A fifth, unsubmitted, that she had been quietly assembling for two years out of her own predecessors' recovery logs and the small inconsistencies they had noted under *within acceptable range* and never returned to.

The feeling in her chest was evidence. She catalogued it, filed it, and kept walking.

*

The path widened at a shelf of flat volcanic stone - the overlook, wide enough to stop, wide enough to see clearly south. From there the approach road curved around the ridge's base and ran toward the Hearth-Three perimeter.

Six figures on the road. Long coats. Rifles slung.

Not a maintenance crew.

"Patrol," Orim said, before she had finished counting them herself. He had stopped beside her, and something in the flatness of his voice told her this was not a guess. "Rotation crew. They run the ridge on odd mornings - I have watched them do it on four separate crossings, always this stretch, always this hour." He considered the distance. "At that pace, the junction in perhaps twenty minutes."

"And us?"

"About the same. Perhaps five minutes ahead, if we do not stop again."

Going back over the ridge cost two days they did not have. The valley below was Atrament perimeter under a different command chain with a different tolerance for unauthorized transit, and the ridge path was the only way south that did not require Lyra to present credentials three weeks expired and flagged for watch beside her name. The junction ahead - where the ridge path met the Loera approach - was the only door.

She looked at the six coats a moment longer, then at Orim, who had already turned from the road and was looking at the ground at his feet with the particular stillness that came before a scry.

*

He did not announce what he was doing. He simply stopped, and by the time she turned to look he was already at the preliminary stages: the tincture vial from his satchel's side pocket, the careful wetting of two fingertips, the small deliberate motion of bringing them together at the sternum. She had watched him do this at the Ocipheus Station archive, reading the south passage - a place, an hour that no longer existed anywhere but in the seeing of it. She had watched him do it at the Veil River junction, reading the barge traffic three days gone and already lost again the moment his eyes came open. She could read the stages now the way you read the stages of any practiced ritual - the preparation, the settling, the two breaths that were not breathing so much as calibration.

He pressed his fingertips together. He breathed twice. He looked at nothing.

There was a period of perhaps forty seconds. Wind moved over the shelf. Somewhere below, a steam vent released its slow geological percussion.

Orim opened his eyes.

"No," he said.

Not a word of protest, and not a word aimed at the membrane, or at her, or at anyone standing on the shelf. A fact. The way a man says *no* when someone has asked him to describe what is on the far side of a wall he has been staring at his whole life, and when he finally looks there is no far side - there is only the wall, and then, worse, not even the wall.

He sat down on the volcanic stone. Carefully, the way old men sit when the joints have opinions and a man has learned not to argue with them. He set the satchel beside him. He looked at the south country for a long time and did not speak.

She had been in Orim Zebulun's company six weeks. She had seen him cold, and moving, and in the aftermath of danger, and in the long dead hours of barge transit when there was nothing to do but watch a river and think. She had seen him tired more times than she could count, and pained, occasionally, in the specific way of a man whose body has opinions about Heloth courier saddles. She had not seen him old. He looked old now. Sixty years working the membrane at the depth available to him - the near, the recent, the small clear impressions a site gives up to a practiced minor scryer - sixty years of that, and this was the first refusal. He did not say so. He did not need to. The way he sat on the rock said it for him.

Rowe looked at Orim. He looked at her. He said nothing, because he would not say it yet, but something had plainly happened that was not supposed to happen, and his face had gone still and careful in the way of a man reading a situation he had not expected to need to read.

She did not press Orim. A membrane refusal was not a physical shock, but it produced a similar effect on the body's regulatory sense - the site had been prepared to receive him, and then had not, and his nervous system needed a moment to accept that the preparation was simply over.

She looked instead at the Rod, still loose in his hand.

*

The mineral tip should have carried a faint warmth of its own - the low residual glow it kept between uses, the color of a coal a day after the fire. It did not. It was the dull grey of a thing that had given nothing back. Along the shaft, two of the seven graduated grooves - the fourth and the sixth, the channels he kept tuned to the near and the recent, the depths he worked most - had darkened past their usual tarnish into something the color of an old bruise.

She had read enough Liturgy practitioner literature on installation instruments to know a fouled channel when she saw one, and she had never seen one on Orim's Rod. Instruments fouled when they had been made to draw against something that gave back less than the draw cost. The literature had a term for the underlying condition, dry and precise and thirty years removed from anyone who had to stand where she was standing: *recovery gradient depression.* The site was returning less than it had been asked to return, for the same or greater cost of asking. The wound was not healing at the rate the apparatus assumed.

A wound heals inward from its edges. That was the basic principle, from the biology sections of her own research. The rate depends on depth, on the frequency of re-opening, on the condition of the surrounding tissue. A wound sutured and reopened and re-sutured develops scar tissue faster than the tissue around it can compensate. The scar does not have the same properties. It does not recover at the same rate. Eventually the surgeon's options narrow to nothing a surgeon can do.

The membrane under the Forge Hearth - the world's own caul, not a wall but living tissue with an inclination to close - had been opened and held open, repeatedly, for close to three hundred years. The extraction apparatus existed to hold the wound open against that inclination - an extraction cycle, then a recovery interval, then another extraction cycle, on and on since Year 842. Hearth-Four had run below production target for eleven years. Hearth-Seven for nine. She knew the numbers because they were in her own unfinished paper, pulled from records the Liturgy's assessment committee had filed under *calibration drift* three times on Hearth-Four and twice on Hearth-Seven, each time recalibrating an apparatus that was not the thing that had gone wrong.

The yield kept declining. It would keep declining. That was what a wound did when it was reopened faster than it could close.

"It is not calibration drift," she said.

Orim did not look up from the south country. "No," he agreed. "It has not been calibration drift for some time."

"There is a reporting channel for this. A Templar's technical observation, membrane-adjacent anomaly, forwarded through the regional Canon to the assessment committee." She heard herself say it in the flat cadence of the regulation itself, the cadence she used when she did not want the words to have weight yet. "I have filed fourteen of these in twelve years."

"And they were reviewed," Orim said. "And filed." It was not a question.

"Yes."

He was quiet for a moment. "Thirty years ago I stood at an aperture and saw something building that should not have been permitted to build, and I came back and told the people whose office it was to be told, and the phrase that came back to me was *no action indicated.*" He said the four words without inflection, the way a man recites something he has had thirty years to memorize whether he wanted to or not. "The committee that would receive your report is the same committee that signed the Year 842 authorization, and the Year 919 renewal, and the Year 1001 renewal, under a charter that pays it to keep signing. It was not designed to receive what you are about to tell it. It was designed to keep telling itself the answer it has been giving for six hundred years." He looked at her, finally. "I am not saying this to spare you the paperwork."

"I know what you're saying."

"I'm not going to report it," she said, mostly to herself.

He nodded, once, and looked back at the south country, and something in the set of his shoulders was not surprise and was not relief. It was closer to a man setting down a weight he had been carrying so long he had stopped noticing its shape - someone else, finally, arriving at the place he had arrived at thirty years before, so that he was no longer the only one who knew the door was gone.

She filed it. She did not examine it further. There was no time, and there would not be time for a long while yet.

*

She went and stood near him, not close enough to intrude.

"At Sinter," he said, without looking at her, "the tone is directional. Present. Something at that frequency I have never been fully certain is the membrane and not whatever lives below it." He paused. "I have crossed this approach six times in thirty years. The register here was always different - lower, slower, more diffuse. A bruise is different from a cut." He looked at the stone under his boots. "This is not that."

She waited.

"A new site will resist me. Cold, dense - unworked, the perceptual surface harder to read. But I can always find the surface. In fifty years I have always had enough to press into it and open it." His hands were very still on his knees. "Here there is nothing to press into."

"Not resistance," she said.

"Not resistance. Absence." He looked at her for the first time since the scry. "The way you cannot press your hand through a wall is not the same as the way you cannot press your hand through the space where the wall used to be. Different kind of nothing."
