The dock itself was old, built for sailing galleys long before anyone had needed a mooring built for anything else: hand-forged iron rings at two-meter intervals, sized for the hawsers of three-masted trading vessels, driven into lead sealant that had held for the better part of a century and showed no interest in doing otherwise.

The *Eirene-7* sat in it the way something built for a different kind of water always sat in a berth like that - competent, and slightly wrong for the frame around her, the way a good tool looks wrong in the hand of the wrong trade. Her hull was riveted steel and aluminum, tighter seams than any shipwright would use, an engineering compromise built to be light enough for air and strong enough to take the sea if the air ever failed her: low at the waterline, broad in the beam, the proportions of a machine that had decided the sea was a surface to land on, not a medium to move through. Above the hull rose the envelope - pale metal ribs under grey fabric, sixty meters of it, holding the shape that let her cross a channel like this one in hours where anything built for the water alone took days. The mooring lines running from her cleats to the old iron rings were nylon, not rope, and the sound they made under tension was a different sound from anything that dock had been built to hear, and the dock did not seem to mind.

Rowe stood at the head of the path and looked at her for a long time without saying anything.

Not reading her as engineering - that was a way of seeing he had never had cause to build, in any language, and it was not what he was doing. He was looking at her the way a man looks at something that forces him to revise a belief he had been carrying without examining it, and doing the revision fast, because he had learned, since the pluck out of the golden water, since the naming, to do that kind of revision fast or not survive the next thing that required it. He had seen something like this once. Not this - not at this scale, not finished this completely - but the family of it. A naval air station on a grey coast, a flying-boat hull up on the hard stand for repair, mechanics working the rivet lines, the thing ungainly on land and entirely correct in the air over the harbor. This was a later development of that same logic, carried further than his own world had managed to carry it, the way the bolt-action rifles in the Verlaine barracks were a later development of a logic his world had only started.

He did not say any of this. He said, "Ship," which was the whole of what he judged the moment worth, and correct, and then, after a moment, in the tone of a man testing a thought he had not yet decided whether to trust: "I could."

Orim looked at him.

Rowe did not finish it. He had spent the sentence he judged worth spending and stopped there, the way he always stopped exactly where his willingness ran out and not one word before, and something in his face - the flat black optic that did the work a face used to do - held very still, the way it held still when he was thinking about Turret Thirty-Four and did not intend to say so.

"Later," Orim said. Not a dismissal. A placeholder, filed for when there would be time to unfold whatever Rowe had just folded back up.

Two figures moved along the *Eirene-7*'s deck rail below, unhurried, the specific unhurried of men counting down hours on a posting nobody wanted them holding. The mooring watch. Two of them, rotating on a schedule that would, at some hour of some coming night, thin their attention down to the number of hours left rather than the vessel in front of them, the way attention always thinned on a watch nobody believed mattered.

Three days.

"Three days is enough," Lyra said.

"Three days has to be enough," said Orim. "Which is not quite the same claim."

---

They walked back up from the overlook in the dark, the anchorage below them settling into the particular quiet of a place that had already decided nothing was going to happen there tonight, and the soldier fell into step beside Lyra on the upper terrace path, where the view opened north again - the channel, black now instead of grey, Pallor invisible on the far side of it.

She had been quiet the whole walk down and the whole walk back, and Lyra had let her be quiet, because fourteen days had taught her which silences wanted company and which wanted room.

"I was posted for a recovery," the soldier said finally. "Three weeks, standard duration. We are on day fourteen, and the operation in front of us now is stealing a Liturgy vessel out of an impounded mooring to run down a party we don't yet have the authority to arrest, in a territory none of us have papers for." She said it the way she said everything operational - flat, without complaint, a navigator stating a bearing and letting the bearing speak for itself. "I am not qualified for what this has become. That isn't a statement about my nerve."

"I know what you mean by it," Lyra said.

The soldier reached inside her coat and drew out her field commission - the Ocipheus standing document issued to the unit before any of this, before Schmalburg, before Ardea, before the Forge Hearth's refusal and the three days of cliff road behind them that none of them had put into words yet. She held it for a moment without reading it, because she already knew what it said, and then she set it in Lyra's hand.

"You keep it," she said. "File it however you need to."

"Think carefully before-"

"I'm not leaving the service." The soldier drew the distinction with the precision of someone who had already argued it out with herself, more than once, before deciding to say it aloud. "I know what that calculation looks like and I am not making it tonight. I am asking to leave this. This mission. Tonight." She looked at the black water. "I have a cousin working cargo out of the lower anchorage, Heloth-bound. I have coin. I haven't seen him since my first rotation."

Lyra looked at her for a long moment - a woman with twelve years still ahead of her in the standard shape of a career, who had read a garrison manifest and understood exactly what it meant and had chosen, with her eyes open, not to be the one who helped steal the ship it belonged to.

She closed her hand around the commission.

The soldier held her gaze one moment longer - checking something she needed to be certain of, and finding it - then turned and went down the path toward the lower anchorage, where the commercial traffic moored and the Heloth freight lines took on their last Loera passengers before the evening tide.

Lyra watched her go until the path curved and there was nothing left to watch.

Orim had come up from somewhere below at some point during the conversation; she hadn't heard him arrive and didn't ask when. He stood a few paces back with the stillness of a man who had heard the shape of it and had known better than to insert himself before it was finished.

"Heloth," he said, once the silence had gone on long enough to need something in it.

"She has a cousin there."

"Good." He wasn't looking at Lyra. He was looking at the empty path. "The worse outcome would have been next week. Once she'd had time to think of a reason to resent it." He let that sit for a moment. "This way she gets to keep the reason she actually has."

There was nothing to say to that which would have improved on it. Lyra put the commission inside her coat, against the place where she still carried Vega's note, three days old now and unanswered, and did not take either of them out again.

---

They took a room above a chandler's on the second terrace for what remained of the night, the three of them, because a party of three drew less attention in a doorway than a party of four had, and because there was nothing left to arrange that daylight would improve.

Orim spread the House's chart across the table by the window and marked the anchorage, the switchback path, the terrace line that would keep them out of sight of the garrison post above the cove until they were nearly on top of it.

"Third hour of the watch," he said. "That's when attention goes soft on a posting like that one - not before, not after, the third hour specifically, every garrison in every kingdom, some things don't vary by House. We move on the third hour, or we wait a full rotation and lose most of a day we don't have."

"Two men," Lyra said. "One vessel. A watch that isn't expecting anyone, on a ship nobody's supposed to want."

"And someone who can put her in the air without four minutes of interface protocol we have no way of running," Orim said, and looked at Rowe, who was standing near the window with the black optic turned toward the dark shape of the cove two hours west, not quite visible from here and present anyway, the way a destination is.

Rowe didn't answer that directly. He touched the chart, once, above the mark that was the anchorage, and moved his finger north, across the channel, to the coastline that was Pallor.

"Fast," he said. "Before morning."

"Yes," Lyra said.

"And then-" His finger stayed on the chart, on the water between the anchorage and the far coast, on all three days of it. "Pallor."

"If the *Avandrey*'s schedule holds."

He nodded, once, the gesture he had learned did the work of a longer sentence, and looked back out at the dark toward the cove, toward the ship with two bored men walking her rail on a rotation that hadn't yet worked out what was coming for it, and said nothing else, which by now she had learned to read as its own kind of answer.

Three days.

Three days had to be enough.

She banked the lamp and lay down without undressing, the way she had every night since Ocipheus Station, and listened to the sea working the caves below the terrace, and did not sleep for a long time, thinking about a ship two hours west that did not yet know it was going to fly for them before the sun came up on the third day of a window that had never been built with them in mind.

[Beat 20]
﻿?THE ACQUISITION - EIRENE-7

The cliff path ran south along the coast at an elevation where the wind was indifferent to them but the cold was not. It came off the water in the particular way of sea cold at the end of the night - not the cold of an hour earlier, which had conviction, but the tired cold that preceded dawn, which had given up on announcement and simply continued.

Lyra had been on the limestone overhang above the cave mouth for the better part of an hour, watching the dock through the vigil-glass scope. The scope did other things - read the old scarring in the membrane at range, logged thermal signatures, performed functions she had appreciated when she salvaged it from the Schmalburg dockyard and appreciated differently now - but what she was using it for was the straightforward purpose of seeing in the dark, which it managed acceptably. The cave mouth was sixty meters below. The dock inside it was forty meters in. The ship was inside the dock, and she had confirmed the hull markings before the last light failed.

EIRENE-7. Checkpoint designation and maintenance code in the Liturgy's block letters, stenciled without decoration, because decoration was not what the designation was for. Atrament had taken it somewhere, garrisoned it, and gone on treating it as a piece of captured inventory rather than the fastest thing in the sky. That was the gap she intended to use.

She came down off the overhang and gathered the other two behind a limestone shelf that was big enough to hide them if no one from the garrison was actively looking, and small enough to guarantee the kind of cold that came from standing still in wind that had not gotten any warmer.

Orim scried from the shelf, crouched with his fingertips pressed together at his sternum, his eyes finding the middle distance. He did not use the Rod. He had not used it since the Forge Hearth, and he had not said why, and she had not asked, though she had noticed the Rod stayed in his coat now the way a hand stayed in a pocket that used to hold something warm. The range tonight was short, the question only where and not when, and his minor gift would do for that much, the cave funneling his attention toward the vessel like a throat. He took ninety seconds.

His eyes opened.

"Two soldiers," he said. "The one at the gangway foot is seated, rifle across the knees, watching the gate side of the dock, not the water. The one at the forward hatch is upright, moving - checking the mooring lines along the starboard rail. He'll finish that and go still at the hatch. Call it four minutes." He paused. "The hold is clear. I didn't go past it. There's something between me and the space behind the forward bulkhead I didn't want to push through."

He said this without alarm, the way he said everything he had found in nearly fifty years of scrying: as observed fact, not an invitation to interpret it.

"Engine housing," Lyra said. "Residual heat, maybe. Liturgy apparatus reads strange even asleep."

"Possibly." He did not sound convinced. He did not push it. He sat down on the shelf - not a controlled lowering, a sitting-down his body had decided on before he consulted it - and looked at the backs of his hands, and his breathing was the deliberate breathing of a man remembering what deliberate breathing was for.

She let him have it. She looked at Rowe instead.

He had the rifle slung and his hands were doing something at his belt she recognized without needing to watch closely: the bayonet catch, worked once, twice, to be sure it would give when asked. She had watched him use it once already, three days back in the monster corridor, when the bolt had jammed and there had been nothing else in his hands fast enough to matter. He was checking it now the way a man checks any tool he has learned, the hard way, not to trust blind.

"Two," she said. "Gangway and forward hatch. Quiet."

He looked at the cave mouth for three seconds, the way he looked at any ground before he committed to crossing it. "Aye," he said. Then, because the moment called for more than one word and he chose to give her more: "I go first. You after."

It was not much of a sentence. It was enough of one. She had stopped needing the rest of his sentences some weeks ago; the shape of what he meant arrived ahead of the words now; she had learned to read him the way Orim read the middle distance, taking what came and not requiring it to be more finished than it was.

They went down.

The south stairs were cut into the limestone and had been there long enough that the edges had rounded under salt water and foot traffic. The third step was exactly where memory said the previous one like it had been - drainage had worked at the stone beneath it, and the step flexed under weight in a way a normal step did not. She felt it, shifted her weight forward, kept moving. Behind her she heard Rowe take it the same way, without being told, because he had watched her do it and filed the information the way he filed everything: once, correctly, permanently.

The dock was planking over water. She could hear the cave's version of the sea under it - not surf, not current, just the particular movement of water in a contained space, the kind that did not go anywhere and knew it. The cold down here was not the cold above. Above, the cold had been sea cold, which had range, which came from somewhere and went somewhere else. Down here the cold was limestone cold, which had been sitting in the same dark since before the Houses had names and was not interested in sharing any of it with something as temporary as a person.

The Eirene-7 grew as she walked toward it. This was a habit of enclosed spaces - the ship had looked large from the cave mouth, and it looked larger now, and by the time she was thirty meters off it was simply the scale of the thing, which was the scale of a vessel built to carry supply loads for a Wall garrison over mountain terrain. Deep-bellied, high-shouldered, the hull practical without being ugly, built to take water-landings and mean it. The envelope structure sat against the cave ceiling as a darker solid in the dark, the rigid frame running fore to aft.

The gangway soldier was exactly where Orim had said. Seated on an upturned crate, rifle across his knees, chin drifting toward his chest in the specific rhythm of a man losing a fight with the end of a long watch. He was watching the gate end of the dock because there was nothing else, in four hours, that had given him a reason to watch anything else.

Rowe went to him from the water side, along the edge of the planking where the shadow of the hull kept the running lights off his approach, and Lyra watched the last four meters of it because someone had to and because looking away would not have made it not happen. He did not run. He closed the distance the way he closed any distance that needed closing without noise - weight forward, feet finding the planking's dry spots by a kind of memory that had nothing to do with having walked this dock before.

The soldier's head came up perhaps a quarter of the way, the beginning of an alertness that was going to arrive one half-second too late to be useful to him, and then it was over. Rowe's hand went to the jaw and the back of the skull in the same motion the blade went into the base of the throat, up and in, the same angle he had found once already and had not needed shown to him twice. There was very little sound. There was a great deal of weight, suddenly, in Rowe's other arm, and he took it, and lowered the man to the planking rather than let him fall, the rifle sliding off the dead man's knees and Rowe's foot arresting it before it could ring against the boards.

He held the position for three full seconds, one hand still at the man's collar, feeling for the second heartbeat that was not going to come and did not come.

Then he moved to the forward hatch.

The second soldier had finished with the mooring lines and was standing at the hatch coaming with the specific stillness of a man who had run out of tasks and was waiting for the next one to be assigned by the clock rather than an officer. He heard nothing. He felt an arm come across from behind and the same economical work at the throat, and Rowe took his weight the same way, set him down the same way, and stood over both of them for a moment with his hands loose at his sides.

Lyra reached him. She looked down at the two men on the planking, one at the gangway, one at the hatch, and she filed it - the word she used for putting a fact somewhere she could carry it without it costing her the next twenty minutes.

"Clear," Rowe said. He looked at his hands, turned them once, and did not say anything else about them.

They moved.

The gangway held their weight without complaint. At the top of it the cave's ceiling pressed close, the envelope's upper curve occupying most of the remaining clearance, and Lyra ducked under it out of a caution the space did not strictly require. Rowe found the cargo hatch release before she did - recessed into the hull plating, manual, a mechanism she recognized from Liturgy transport design. He had never been aboard a Liturgy vessel in his life. He had found the handle anyway, because a cargo hatch handle looked like a cargo hatch handle regardless of what was painted on the hull outside it, and machinery, whatever Sphere it came from, kept a family resemblance to other machinery. He had it open before she'd finished crossing the gangway.

The hold was wide and high-ceilinged and smelled of petroleum fuel and the particular dry-dust smell of Liturgy warehouse stock, which was uniform whether you were in Ocipheus or at the far end of the Wall. They stored things the same way everywhere. It was a form of institutional consistency she had always found comforting, and she noticed she was finding it comforting now, on a stolen ship, over two men she had just watched Rowe kill, and did not examine that too closely.

Orim came last, down the gangway at his own pace, carrying M-101's shell.

The small chassis rode over his shoulder the way you carried something that did not weigh what its size suggested it should - black, jointed, the oval optical receptor dark and flat, arms loose, the specific dead-weight looseness of a thing built to move under its own intention and currently possessing none. Rowe's own body, wherever Rowe's own body currently was, had walked itself down from the shelf under its own power and then simply stopped being a walking thing the moment the two of them started down the stairs, folding into stillness at the mouth of the path with the patient trust of a man leaving his coat somewhere he intended to come back for it. Orim had picked it up without being asked. This, too, had become a drill.

"Guard the shell," Orim said, more to himself than to her, setting the chassis down against a stack of cargo crates in the hold, upright, propped, the oval receptor facing the room like an eye that had not yet opened. He said it the way he said most things now that had to do with Rowe's other body: as an instruction that had stopped needing to be new.

"I have it," Lyra said. She put herself between the propped shell and the cargo hatch, because that was the door, and doors were where the taking-away happened.

Rowe was already forward, at the bulkhead that separated the hold from the control car - the narrow forward compartment where the instrument cluster sat dark on standby power, dials unlit, needles resting at zero the way a sleeping animal's limbs came to rest. He put his hands flat against the bulkhead the way a man tested a door for heat before opening it. He stood there for a moment doing nothing that Lyra could see.

Then he went still in a way that was different from standing still.

She had watched this happen once before, at Schmalburg, when the overlooked little unit she'd been traveling with for weeks had gone rigid at the base of an Atrament war-chassis and come up a moment later wearing it - steel and scythe and scorpion tail, a shape that made you recalculate everything you thought you understood about what you had been protecting. She recognized the same stillness now, except that this time there was no war-chassis in front of him, no obvious vessel his intention was reaching for. There was only the ship. The whole ship, forty meters of Liturgy iron and gas envelope and pressure lines, sitting in a cave with nothing occupying it but standby current and the residue Orim had felt and not wanted to name.

The oval receptor on the small shell went dark.

Not dimmed. Dark, entirely, the black glass giving back only the reflected shape of the hold around it, the way a window gives back a room once the light on the other side of it has gone out. The chassis did not fall. It had been propped correctly; it simply became what it was underneath the animation - a disposable Verlaine shell, empty, an object rather than a presence, leaning against a cargo crate in the dark.

For four seconds nothing in the hold moved at all.

Then the ship inhaled.

That was the only word Lyra had for it afterward, and she knew it was the wrong word, and she kept it anyway. The port outboard engine caught - not the cough-and-catch of an ordinary cold start, but a single decisive turn, the sound of something large remembering, all at once, exactly what it was for. The running lights came up along the gangway rail in a line, not flickering into being the way a failing bulb did, but arriving, each one a fraction of a second after the last, west to east, like the ship was opening its eyes down its own length one socket at a time.

Starboard outboard. Center pair. The cave took all four engines and threw the sound back from every wall at once, compressed, enormous, pressing on the chest the way sound did in a space that had never been built to hold it.

Lyra stood over the small dark shell with her hand near it, not touching it, the way you kept a hand near something that might need you and might not, and thought about Turret #34. She had heard the fragments of it from him in the weeks since Sphere 31 - not the whole shape, never the whole shape, but enough: a gunship, a line of shells crewed to its guns, and one gun, once, that had simply declined to fire. She had not understood, until this exact moment, watching an entire Liturgy supply vessel wake up and decide to become a getaway, what it had cost him to be small enough, then, to only refuse one gun. Tonight there was no crew to refuse alongside. Tonight the whole hull was his to decline or commit, and he had committed all of it, at once, to them.

Orim, from the crate he had already claimed as the one crate in the hold likely to hold his weight, said quietly: "That is a very large room to be lost in."

"He isn't lost," Lyra said.

"No," Orim agreed. "Not yet."

The ballast shift began without anyone announcing it - the hull finding where its weight sat and correcting, a slow reorientation she felt underfoot before she understood what she was feeling. The dock planking groaned against the hull's flank. Then the ship began, very deliberately, to back away from it.

Rowe - the small shell, dark and empty against the crates - did not move at all. There was no voice from any bulkhead speaking to her; there was only the ship's behavior, which was Rowe's behavior now, distributed into engines and ballast tanks and a rudder somewhere aft that was already answering a question no one in the hold had asked aloud.

She went to the cargo hatch, which she had left cracked for sight lines, and watched the dock recede - the two men on the planking growing smaller, the gate at the far end, the running lights of a posting that would not be manned again until someone came looking for the men who should have been on it. She did not close the hatch until the shapes were gone into the cave's dark.

The ship backed through the dock space, and the noise was worse moving than it had been standing still - engines bouncing off approaching walls, water pushing around the hull, the envelope's structural creak against a ceiling that was, at its narrowest point, exactly as close as Orim's scry had implied and no closer.

Then the envelope scraped the ceiling.

A long sound. Metal on stone, dry and sustained, the kind of sound that produced absolute silence in everyone who heard it, because everyone who heard it was calculating whether it was a scrape or a failure. It lasted perhaps two seconds. Then it stopped. The ship kept moving.

Lyra let a breath go through her nose that she had not noticed holding.

The cave mouth arrived in the forward viewport as a deeper darkness inside the general darkness - open air, open water, the specific pre-dawn quality of a sea that had not yet decided to be visible. The cliff went past on both sides, pale where salt had not darkened it. Then there was no cliff. There was only sky, and the ship was in it, backing water until the hull found the open swell and the acoustics changed the way acoustics changed when you left a cave - everything expanding at once, the engines becoming themselves again instead of the cave's compressed version of themselves.

The takeoff run began without ceremony. Speed building, the bow spraying water up at the hatch seam, the hull skipping once at the threshold between dragging and lifting, and then not skipping - lifting, bow first, then the whole body of the ship, the water-sound falling away entirely and the wind-and-engine sound taking its place. She felt the specific silence of a hull no longer touching the sea.

The ship climbed east along the cliff line, banked once, and leveled into open sky.

For nearly twenty minutes nothing in the hold changed. The small shell stood dark against the crates. The engines held a steady register. Orim slept, or performed a very convincing imitation of it, on his crate.

Then the oval receptor came alight again.

It happened the way the engines had caught - not a flicker building toward steadiness but a decision, arrived at whole. The small chassis's head came up off its chest by degrees, the joints finding their positions with the particular hesitance of a man standing after sitting too long in the cold.

"M-101," it said.

The voice was thin and mechanical, the tin voice-box flattening the word to no tone at all, and it was, in this moment, the most welcome sound Lyra had heard since the cave mouth.

"You're back," she said. It was not a question. She had learned not to make it one; he did not like confirming what was already visible.

He did not answer that. He looked, instead, at his own hands - turning them, the way he had turned them over the two men on the dock, except now the gesture read differently: a man checking that the hands in front of him were, in fact, his current hands, and finding, apparently, that they were. He held that position a moment longer than the check required.

"Ship," he said. He tapped two fingers against the bulkhead behind him, once. "Big." He looked toward the forward compartment he was no longer occupying and could not, from where he stood, entirely believe he had occupied at all. There were no words, in any language, for what the interval between casting out and casting back had felt like, going somewhere that large and finding the way back to somewhere this small, and Lyra did not ask him to find them. She had watched his hands. That would do for tonight.

Then he went forward, back through the bulkhead gap, toward the control car, and the small shell's hands went still against the frame the way they had before, and a moment later the oval receptor darkened again, and the ship - steady, level, already committed to its heading - kept flying, because it had never actually stopped being him.

Orim opened his eyes without otherwise moving. "He came back," he said, "and then he went again."

"He has to fly it the rest of the way."

"Yes." Orim considered this with the specific patience of a man who had spent forty years watching apparatus do things apparatus was not supposed to be capable of. "That is going to be the shape of the whole crossing, then. Gone. Back for a breath. Gone again."

"That's the shape of it."

"I don't love it," Orim said, "but I don't have a better one, so I'll allow it." He closed his eyes again.

The horizon at the forward viewport was beginning to gray at its lowest edge - not light yet, just the sky establishing that it was distinct from the sea, which it did every morning with the particular patience of something that did not need anyone's attention to accomplish it.

Lyra came forward and stood in the gap in the bulkhead and looked at the small dark shell propped against nothing now but its own stillness, and beyond it, through the frame, the instrument cluster running exactly as it should, needles where they belonged, the ship doing precisely what a well-flown ship did.

"You said Eirene was Old Caul," she said, to Orim, without turning around.

"Peace," Orim said, from his crate, eyes still shut. "The cessation of hostilities. The state after fighting has ended - not the cause, not the victory, not the surrender. What's left when neither of those is still happening."

She thought about that for the length of the horizon going from black to the barest suggestion of gray. The Liturgy had named this vessel for a state it administered on behalf of everyone else, the way it administered everything else: as a resource held rather than given, distributed through authorized channels, produced by apparatus its recipients were not authorized to examine. She had taken the vessel without authorization. She had watched Rowe kill two men to do it. She had filed the taking as an acquisition, in the ledger she kept in her own head for things that had to be carried without costing her the next hour, and the filing held, mostly, the way filings held when you needed them to.

Rowe came aft - not the shell, not yet; she meant it and caught the confusion in her own thought and let it go - no. The small shell's head lifted again, the oval receptor coming alight for the second brief surfacing, and this time he did not need to check his hands first.

"Two," he said.

He held up two fingers, in case the word alone had not carried, though it had.

She understood him. There was a word for what they were - murderer, or soldier, or something in between the two that no single word covered cleanly - and he had it, as fully as he had ever had anything in his own tongue, and he chose not to reach for it. He gave her the number instead, because the number was what he had decided to give her, stating a fact and making her the person who received it, not asking permission for it and not asking to be absolved of it.

"It was the operation," she said.

She heard herself say it. She heard the frame close around what he had told her - institutional language, the kind that took a fact and set it inside a category built to make the fact carriable. She knew what she was doing. She did it anyway.

He looked at her. Not accusing. Not anything she could easily name. He was deciding something about what he was seeing, and she stood still and let him decide it.

"I know," he said, quietly, in Caul that did not stop short of any of it this time.

Then the receptor went dark again, and the ship, which had never stopped holding its heading, kept holding it, because it had not needed him to be visibly present in order to be flying. She stood over the small still shell a moment longer than guarding it required, and then went back to it anyway, because that was the job, and because there was nothing else on this ship, right now, that wanted watching over more than this did.

The horizon went from gray to the first suggestion of color. The cave was thirty kilometers behind them. The channel was hours ahead, and somewhere in those hours Rowe was going to tell her something she needed to hear, and she was not prepared for it, and there was no preparing for it, and she had hours of flying left to find out whether that changed anything about how she meant to receive it.

It did not, yet.

Orim said, to no one, from his crate: "I'm going to sleep, if no one needs me for anything requiring being awake."

No one answered him. He took this as permission, which it was.

The ship flew east.

[Beat 21]
﻿THE CHANNEL - ROWE TESTIFIES

The channel at altitude smelled of nothing, which was the first thing that seemed wrong about it. She had expected salt. The sea was below them, gray and moving in the last dark before dawn, and the hull of the Eirene-7 was doing what hulls did over open water - a continuous argument with the pressure differential, the envelope and the wind each convinced it understood the other better than the other understood itself. She could hear the argument. She could not smell the sea it was being fought over. The membrane of the hull sealed all of that out, and what was left was the cargo hold's own history: machine oil, old rope, the particular metal smell of institutional supply transport. Liturgy stencils on the crates. Iron handholds bolted at intervals along the bulkhead, for a crew that had once expected turbulence and, evidently, gotten it.

Lyra had been awake since before they cleared the Sal Vento roofline. She had not been going to sleep.

The notebook was open on her knee, and the page had been blank for two hours. She had been trying to write Ardea's service record - the instrument that would let the Ocipheus garrison send the family their formal notification - and the words would not come in any order that resembled a sentence. She had the facts. Intake year. Posting history. Tincture certifications. The action in the field that had ended her. None of those were the words that were failing. What was failing was the first sentence, the one that had to come before the facts, the one that made a person into the subject of their own record instead of a list of administrative entries attached to a name. She had written that sentence for other people's sisters. She could not write it for her own.

She closed the notebook. She did not put it away.

The wheel by the aft bulkhead had not moved in three hours, and no hand was on it, and this no longer alarmed her the way it had at Sal Vento. Rowe had flown the Eirene-7 clear of the port with his own mind wearing her control channels the way a man wears a coat too large for him and does not care because the coat is warm - she had watched the small black shell go still and empty against the crate wall while somewhere above her the whole ship had turned, banked, and climbed, and she had understood for the first time what it meant that he could not be two places. Once they were out over open water, on a heading he trusted, he had come back. She had watched that too: the shell twitching upright, the oval eye brightening, and the one word he always said on return, flat and certain, the only word that had never once required him to search for it. The ship kept the heading now on her own held trim, the way a thing keeps a shape it has been given. It would hold it until something changed. He had told her that, in the flat, spare way he told her most things, and she had chosen to believe him because the alternative was watching the wheel all night herself.

Orim was at the forward porthole, fingertips flat against the glass, in the passive attunement position he used when he wanted to know if the air ahead had anything in it worth knowing about. He had been tired since the Forge Hearth. It was a different tired than the road kind. It had not resolved.

Rowe was at the starboard porthole. He had been there half an hour, looking down at water the color of something that had been cold a long time and did not expect to change, and whatever he was thinking was not, yet, available to her. She had learned this stillness over four months - the specific quality of a man who has already decided something and is waiting for the rest of himself to catch up to the decision.

He came away from the porthole.

He sat down across from her, against the bulkhead, at the distance he always kept in enclosed spaces - enough room to do something different if something different became necessary. His hands went to his knees. His rifle stayed slung across his back. He wore the expression she had filed, weeks ago, as *preparation.*

She put the notebook down behind her, against the bulkhead, and did not open it again.

He said, in Caul: "I want to tell you something."

The grammar was correct, the way it always was - the shell gave him that for free, complete sentences from the first hour in this world, whether he wanted them or not. What was rehearsed was the willingness, not the words. They came out flat, tin-flat, the chassis's exact and affectless delivery doing nothing to soften a sentence said enough times in private that it had worn a groove. He had been building to this since Schmalburg. She had clocked it in the questions he'd started asking afterward - not about danger, or food, or direction, but about sequence, about *then*, about things that had already happened and could not be changed by more accurate language.

She said: "I'm listening."

---

He started with the factory.

A city - he gave her its name in his own language first, and stopped, checking whether she needed the Caul for it. She didn't. Four months of hearing him had taught her the shape of his language well enough to simply receive it.

"It doesn't need one," she said.

He nodded. A textile city. Not the largest he'd known - he'd traveled to bigger ones for work. His own factory made small mechanical parts, springs and couplings, components that went into other men's machines. Six years there. "I knew the machines," he said, and there was weight in it, the specific weight of a fact that is also its own grief. "By sound. Different sound, different problem. You learn it."

He stopped. He looked at his hands.

"I was good at my work," he said. "I want you to know that. I am not telling you I was nobody." He said the word plainly, flat and exact, and let it sit before he went on. "I had a name. I had a place. People knew me."

"I know," she said.

"I don't think you do," he said, without offense. "You know I was Scried out of somewhere. You don't know I was someone there, first."

She held still with that. He was right, and she had not thought to separate the two facts before.

"Tell me," she said.

A Thursday in autumn. He knew the day because the morning shift overlapped the maintenance crew for forty minutes on Thursdays, and the overlap changed the floor noise into something else, two crews occupying the same iron for slightly different purposes. He knew the hour from the sound the factory clock made past the half-hour. He knew the season from the angle of light through the high windows. He had always tracked the conditions of the room he was in. It was not a special skill. It was simply how he'd been made to notice things.

"Then," he said.

He stopped.

"Then," he said again, and let the one word carry the weight of the three hundred that would follow it - everything between *half past the hour, Thursday morning* and *the floor was not the floor.* He had all the words. What he did not have, yet, was the nerve to spend them in order. His jaw worked once, the specific motion of a man arriving at the edge of what he was willing to say aloud and finding it a genuine edge, with a drop on the other side.

"Take it in pieces," she said.

He looked at her. He nodded.

The floor changed first. Not gone - wrong, a different quality under his boots, the kind of wrongness a man who works machinery all day is built to notice before he understands it. He checked himself: was he unwell? Had something failed in the structure beneath him? He was not unwell. The floor was not broken.

Then the stone room.

He told it the way he told hard things - evenly, the specific economy of a man who has learned what feeling costs and has decided, for the length of this telling, not to spend it. Dressed stone. Cold - not the factory's winter cold, which was the cold of bad heating, but the cold of stone that had been cold for centuries and intended to stay that way. Three figures in robes. He gave her the colors and she recognized them before he said the word: Ocipheus white, canonical gold at the hem.

"Ocipheus," he said, and said it the way he said accurate things. Not an accusation. A fact laid on the table between them. The word came out exactly like every other word he'd given her tonight - flat, level, uninflected - and that was what made it land as hard as it did. A fully articulate voice, saying the truth outright, in a register built to carry ammunition counts.

"Yes," she said.

"One of them held a book."

He was quiet a moment - not the quiet of having nothing left, the quiet of choosing which piece carried the most per word.

"I was not afraid," he said. "I want you to understand that. I have been afraid. I was in France fourteen months. I know what afraid does to a body." He paused. "This was a different thing. This was the feeling of a room whose rules no one had given me. Three people. Robes. No context for any of it. I did what a man does in that room. I made myself as legible as I could. Cooperative. I thought - if I am cooperative, someone will explain."

He looked at his hands.

"No one explained," he said.

She knew the intake protocol. She had read it her first year at the Station, the way new practitioners read the foundational instruments. She had read it again three years into her career, when the intake documentation project had looked like a season's worth of minor administrative writing. She had contributed to the literature on best-practice administration. She had written, herself, in her own hand: *oath administered prior to language acquisition minimizes resistive response in conscript cohort.* She had meant it as an observation about procedural efficiency.

"The words had a rhythm," he said. "Something official. I understood none of it." He looked at his hands. "There was a gesture. The one with the book made it toward me. I copied it." He showed her - a small incline of the head, a particular set of the right hand. She knew it. The acknowledgment position, from the Compact Oath. Formal assent to canonical authority. "I copied it because that is what you do, in a room with robed people making official sounds and you don't have the language. You look willing. You think: I will be willing until I understand, and then I will decide."

He paused.

"I never decided," he said. "I never understood fast enough."

Orim had not moved from the porthole. His fingertips stayed on the glass. He said nothing.

---

He gave her Verlaine in the same pieces.

The barracks were stone-vaulted, high-windowed, old - a category he recognized without being able to place it, and had stopped trying to explain once the recognizable and the wholly foreign had gotten too tangled to separate. The rifles were on racks along the wall, and he had known rifles; he'd carried one in France. "I knew the action," he said, and mimed the bolt, the cycling motion. "Not exactly. Later. Further along - like a later model of a thing you already know."

He looked at her.

"In my world there were men whose whole careers were tracing how a mechanism developed - who built the first one, who improved it, who licensed the design. You can follow forty years of small changes back to an origin. The rifles here don't belong to that history. They are past the end of it. But the lineage is there, if you know how to read a receiver housing." He held her eyes. "Someone had my world's designs, and time to take them further than my world has had."

"Someone had been watching," he said. "Long before they brought me here."

He waited.

"Yes," she said.

He nodded, the way he always did when she confirmed a hard thing before he'd go on to the next one - because confirmation told him he'd understood correctly, and he needed that before he could carry the weight of what came after it.

The barracks held conscripts from at least a dozen points of origin, by his count, and most of them shared no language. The House ran intake on a set of demonstrated commands that required no shared vocabulary at all. *Stand. Walk. Carry. Wait.* Those were the first words said to him in Caul, and the shell rendered them the instant they landed - he understood before he had decided whether he wanted to. The collar markers were administrative - a stamped disc, a sphere number, punched in during processing. He had seen his own: Sphere 31. He had seen Sphere 22. Sphere 14. A man from Sphere 8 worked the maintenance crews by then, competent in Caul, missing a fingertip to some machine that had closed too fast, and had looked at Rowe in his first week with an expression that said: *I know what this is doing to you, and it gets better in some ways and worse in others, and I can't tell you which way you're headed.*

Orim's fingertips pressed a fraction harder against the glass.

"What is the highest number you know," Rowe said.

"The canonical records reference Sphere sixty-one," she said. "Some practitioners believe the count goes further."

He said nothing for a moment.

"I saw thirty-one on my form. I counted, in the yard and the barracks. Nine spheres, at least, that I saw or heard of. Probably others I didn't." He paused. "How many people are in those files."

"I don't know," she said. "I processed forty-three intake cohorts, over six months. Fourteen persons average, per cohort."

He did the arithmetic she had not done for him. Six hundred, in one half-year window. The program had run, in its documented form, since Year 831 - three hundred years - and the practice before the documentation, the part the early canonical scholars wrote around rather than through, went back further than anyone had troubled to count, which was itself the point of never counting it.

"How many people know what the stone room looks like," he said.

"Intake staff. The records division. Practitioners who've studied the foundational instruments."

"And everyone else knows Myrmidons exist. That they serve. That they come from a membrane, another world, in the general sense." He paused. "Not the cold. Not the robes. Not the gesture."

"No," she said.

"Because the oath is given before the language."

She waited.

"On purpose," he said.

"Yes," she said. "On purpose."

---

He told her about the man from Sphere 22 next, because the numbers had opened a door he could walk the rest of the way through.

Two months further into his own time in Verlaine than Rowe was, mid-forties, the particular stillness of a man who has done physical work for twenty years and learned to conserve his motion. A missing fingertip of his own, on the other hand - a different machine, evidently, but the same category of loss. They had tried, some evenings, to find overlap between their own languages - not Caul, which the shells gave each of them freely and separately, but the tongues they'd each been taken out of, which no shell had ever been built to carry between two stolen men. The way people try when there is nothing else to do with an evening. You pointed. You said your word. Mostly there was nothing.

"We found one," Rowe said.

He paused.

"The word was *mother,*" he said.

Orim's tincture bottle made a small sound against the porthole housing, barely audible under the envelope's argument with the wind.

"In both languages, nearly the same word," Rowe said. "Close enough that you said it, and heard his version, and both of you knew what had been said." He looked at his hands. "I don't know what that means. Whether it says something about language, or about what's the same underneath every world it's spoken in." He was quiet. "I thought about it for months afterward. What that word was doing there, in both of us, without either of us having put it there."

There was another man, from Sphere 8, the one with the maintenance work and the missing fingertip and enough Caul to be dangerous to himself in a careful way. In his second year he had shown Rowe the inside of his boot - the lining worked thin and soft, and words scratched into the leather in a hand that wasn't his own, in a shape he couldn't read and had never learned to read, memorized instead the way you memorize a picture. He had gotten someone to write it for him, or copied the marks from someone who could, and kept them against his foot because a body that has nowhere else to keep a thing will keep it there.

He had recited it to Rowe, slowly, over several weeks, the way you teach a thing you don't fully understand yourself. Rowe had kept it the same way - not as scholarship, as ballast.

"He was built to serve," Rowe said now, and she could hear the quotation in it, the particular careful cadence of words being reproduced rather than made, exact and level, the way the chassis delivered everything. "He chose to refuse. That word made him more a person than the-" He paused there, not for the word but for what came before it. "Caul doesn't have one. Not for a group of men who decide things about a factory without ever touching the machines. Not the workers. Above them." He let the gap stand instead of filling it. "It doesn't matter. The line was about refusing."

Orim's face, at the porthole, did something small and quickly put away - not recognition exactly, or not only that, but the look of a man who has heard a chord struck before in a different room and cannot place which room.

"Who wrote it," Lyra said.

"I don't know," Rowe said. "He didn't know either. It came to him from someone else, the way most things came to us." He looked at his hands. "I've carried it seven months without a name on it. I don't think it needs one to be true."

Later, once he trusted himself to ask, he had put the stone room's actual question to a Verlaine intake administrator: what had the words meant. The man had told him it was the oath of service, binding him to canon law for the term of his conscription.

"I asked him if it was valid," Rowe said, "if I hadn't understood it."

She was already waiting for the answer.

"He said that was a theological question, and he didn't have the training to address it."

---

"The notation," Rowe said. "On the rifle."

"Yes," she said.

"I've seen the pattern before. In my world. Men who studied where designs came from could see the gaps - pieces that weren't native to a lineage, things adapted in from somewhere else, a long time back. We had theories about it. A lost civilization. Older than us. We thought: they built the first things, and disappeared, and we found what they'd left and carried it forward." He paused. "We were proud of that. The search for the origin. The respect for the debt."

He looked at his hands.

"We were right that the knowledge wasn't originally ours," he said. "We were wrong about the civilization being lost. You didn't disappear. You just kept taking."

She held very still.

"I'm not angry," he said, in the even way he said accurate things - not flattened by distance, simply stated without adding weight to a thing that was already heavy enough to carry on its own. "I don't have the strength for it today. I'm telling you because you have the most of the language, and because your institution wrote the forms I put my mark on, and processed the files that had me in them before I had a name for the Caul. I think you should know that one of the people in those files is telling you this himself."

"I know," she said.

"I have known, and not known, longer than I'd like to admit."

He looked at her with an expression she had no file for.

---

Orim turned from the porthole.

It was a small movement - he turned, and stood with his back against the hull, looking at neither of them in particular. His face had the quality she had once seen on a senior practitioner reviewing a misfiled Transmutation record twenty years old: the look of a man confronting something he had always known was there and could no longer keep at the distance of knowing it only abstractly.

"I have read intake reports for forty years," he said.

His voice was even - not flat, even, the way an instrument reads even when it is accurate on both sides of a scale. "Verlaine commissioning requests. No names. Sphere designation, skill classification, intake number." He looked at the hold floor. "I knew what I was reading. I knew what *M-cohort, Sphere thirty-one, intake ninety-seven* meant, the way a surgeon knows what a wound classification means without needing to see the patient." He paused. "I told myself I was being accurate. That someone else would do the work if I didn't. That knowing the cost made the cost somehow -" He stopped. "I don't have the word either. Not in Caul. Probably not in any language."

Rowe looked at him.

"I can no longer say I did not hear it," Orim said. "I'm telling you that so you know that I know."

He didn't say more. He turned back to the porthole, fingertips returning to the glass, the passive attunement settling over him like a posture he had held for fifty years because it was the shape that went with hearing things accurately, and accuracy was the only thing he had left to offer anyone.

Rowe looked at the back of Orim's head a moment. Then at Lyra.

She said nothing. There was nothing adequate, and she had stopped reaching for it.

"I'm not asking you to fix it," he said, after a while.

"I know," she said.

"I'm asking you to know it. There's a difference."

"Yes," she said. "There is."

The silence that followed it was a different silence than the one before - not comfortable, not meant to be, but the silence of a thing said and no longer capable of being unsaid, which sat instead of pressing forward. She sat with it. She was aware, in a specific way, of the notebook behind her, unopened through the whole of it - the first testimony she had ever taken down in nothing but memory, the kind no vigil rotation would ever get the chance to file away as no action indicated.

---

"What are we calling the ship," Orim said, from the porthole, without turning.

The question dropped into the quiet like a stone into still water. Nobody answered at once. The envelope kept its argument with the wind. The gray at the portholes had gone from night-gray to the paler gray that meant the sea had decided to be seen.

Rowe said a short phrase in his own language. She caught the shape of it more than the whole of it, and offered the nearest Caul she had.

"The ground undone."

He looked at her. The vowel was wrong; he adjusted it.

"The ground that undid," he said. The construction wasn't quite the shape his own language would have given it, but it was the shape Caul grammar allowed, and it carried the weight regardless - a thing that had been solid, and had stopped being solid, and never told anyone it intended to.

"She goes to Pallor under a new name," Orim said, still facing the water, in the tone of a man filing a report no one had requested.

Lyra found the notebook. She opened it. She wrote *The Ground Undid,* and below it, not as a research entry but as something she had no institutional category for yet: *Rowe. Sphere 31. Thursday.*

She closed it.

---

The Pallor coast came up on the northern horizon three hours later - first a darkening in the gray that was more solid than sky, the specific dark of a thing that had stood in the same place a long time and did not intend to move for the sea's convenience. Then the particular black of rocky coast, the Hebrides limestone she had studied in cartographic plates and was now seeing in the register reserved for things that have been theoretical a long while and are, abruptly, simply there.

The wheel by the aft bulkhead began, without a hand on it, to answer to a hand somewhere else - the Eirene-7 gathering herself for the descent to come, the way a held breath gathers before it's finally let go. Rowe would have to go back into her soon, to bring her down into the sea-loch by feel the way he had lifted her out of Sal Vento; the small shell that carried the rest of him would go still again, and someone would need to stand near it, because a shell with nothing in it was a shell anyone could claim. She had learned to think of that as a fact of travel now, the way one learns to think of weather.

Lyra looked at the coast.

She had recovered a Relic once before, from a situation that had complicated itself past reasonable parameters - a Verlaine provenance dispute, a mislabeled artifact, the bureaucratic tangle of proving a claim someone had worked hard to obscure. She had been thirty-two. She had been entirely certain. She had been wrong about two of three key facts and right about the outcome, and had filed the whole thing as a lesson in professional humility and moved to the next assignment.

She did not feel certain now.

She felt the specific condition of a person handed information that restructures everything standing behind it, obliged to walk forward into the restructured ground without having had time to survey it. She was trained for uncertainty. She had always found it easier when the uncertainty belonged to someone else.

Rowe came to stand beside her at the forward porthole.

He didn't speak at first. He looked at the coast. She looked at the coast.

After a while he said, in Caul: "What will you do with it. When you have it."

She knew he meant the Relic. Not a weapon, whatever Prelacze actually wanted from it. The founding canon called it the Eye That Remembers and never explained further, and in thirty years of Liturgy service she had not once asked why a thing built only to remember needed three locks, a vigil rotation, and a Canon's countersignature standing between it and anyone who might want to look.

"I don't know yet," she said.

"But not what they want you to do with it."

She looked at him.

"You would have given it to them, before Bressant," he said. "I don't know who *them* is. But you would have. You were the kind of person who gave things to the right authority."

"And now?" she said.

"Now you know I am in the authority's files."

She looked at Pallor, closer now, more specific - the cliffs resolving into individual faces of old-bone rock in the coming light, the moors somewhere above them she couldn't yet see but knew were there.

"Yes," she said.

He nodded. He turned from the porthole and went to find a place to settle before the ship began her descent - a place near enough to his shell that when it went still, someone guarding it would only have to look, not search. She watched him go.

The notebook was in her hand.

*The Ground Undid. Rowe. Sphere 31. Thursday.*

She had been, for thirty years, a person who knew who she was. The names she would not answer to were shorter than the names she would. She was an Ocipheus Knight. A Templar of the third infusion. A Transmutation researcher with a publication record and an institutional role and a frame the world had always obligingly organized itself into. She was also the person who had written *minimizes resistive response in conscript cohort* in a document that now sat in the same archive as every intake form bearing the notation *M-cohort, Sphere 31.*

Both of those people were her. She was not going to be able to file one of them away until she understood which one she meant to keep.

She closed the notebook.

Pallor was ahead.

[Beat 22]
﻿SHE GOES TO SINTER

The dispatch note arrived at the sixth hour.

Vega had been at her desk since the fourth, which was not unusual. She arrived at the fourth hour and departed at the fourteenth and had been doing so for three years, and the Liturgy's administrative schedule had arranged itself around this without comment, because the Liturgy's administrative schedule arranged itself around reliable people the way it arranged itself around good furniture: with gratitude that did not rise to acknowledgment. She had her desk. The desk faced two windows. The windows faced the courtyard. The courtyard was empty most days. This was the structure of her work.

The dispatch was from the Office of the Senior Scribe. Not Subcanon Malvar's office, not the Canon's administrative staff, not anyone in the vertical line she filed things upward to. The Office of the Senior Scribe was a horizontal function - advisory, archival, the Liturgy's institutional memory rather than its executive arm. It had been housed in the same three rooms since Year 872 and was staffed, currently, by one woman who had been in those rooms for forty-one years and had made herself, in that time, as indispensable as the furniture.

Senior Scribe Brentha Osel. Vega had met her twice. Once at the orientation for new Registry appointments, where Osel had delivered a three-minute address on the canonical responsibility of the witness function that Vega had found, at the time, professionally sound and personally uninspiring. Once in the corridor outside the map archive, where Osel had stopped her and asked, without preamble, whether she had found it interesting.

Vega had said: found what interesting?

Osel had said: *the work.* And walked on.

The dispatch note was brief.

*V. Athen-Moor - You are requested to present yourself at the Office of the Senior Scribe at the eighth hour. Bring your notary seal and travel instruments. - B. Osel*

She read it twice. She folded it and put it in her working-copy folder, which was not where it belonged - it belonged in correspondence - but she put it there because the working-copy folder was what she had been carrying with her everywhere for eight months, and the dispatch note was now part of the same pattern as everything else inside it, and the pattern was where it belonged.

She had two hours. She spent them at her desk, working through the routine instrument queue with the methodical focus that had always been her professional strength and had become, lately, something she leaned on more heavily than she used to. Methodical focus was the one thing she still had full access to. She could look at an instrument and see its form, assess its completeness, certify its disposition, even when - especially when - the instrument was part of a larger shape she was also trying to see. The two processes ran in parallel. She had learned to let them.

At the eighth hour she picked up her travel bag - packed before she came to work, which meant she had known before the dispatch arrived, which was a thing she chose not to examine - and went to the Office of the Senior Scribe.

---

Brentha Osel was seventy-three and looked it the way some people did: accurately, without distress, the years worn as information rather than apology. Her desk was smaller than Vega's and covered in materials that were not canonical instruments - reference compendiums, historical registers, the bound record of a review session from Year 1104 that Vega recognized by its binding stamp before she recognized anything written on it. Osel looked at her the way she had looked at her in the corridor: deciding how much of what she knew she was going to say.

"You've been keeping a working copy," Osel said.

It was not a question. "Yes."

"For how long."

"Eight months."

Osel was quiet a moment, turning the Year 1104 record over without opening it. "The current Canon Immaculate has been in his position for eleven years," she said. "Before him, Subcanon Treye Falconer held it for twenty-three years, retired in good standing, and is currently farming his family's land in Calyx's northern province. Before Falconer, Canon Immaculate Amaris Dort held it for thirty-one years and died at her desk." She set the record down. "During Dort's tenure the Forge Hearth extraction authorization was renewed. During Falconer's, the Sphere-intake quota was raised for the third time. During the current tenure, the Vigil line's garrison rotation was extended from eight months to twelve, and six months ago the Liturgy's permanent contingency reserve was unsealed under a standing order renewal certified by a Registry notary." She looked at Vega directly. "You certified it."

"Yes."

"Did you read the form?"

"It was correct."

"The form is always correct," Osel said. "The Canon Immaculate's office is very good at correct forms." Her chair creaked as she turned in it, an old sound from an old chair. "The deployment roster for the war construct evaluation cohort. Did it come through your queue?"

"No. The evaluation certifications came through. The roster was convened under Article Eighteen."

"Membrane-breach classification." Osel nodded once, as though confirming an answer she had already written down somewhere. "The breach they were authorized against has not been publicly announced." A pause, not for effect - for accuracy. "It's the Sinter Zone."

Vega said nothing. The working copy was in her bag. She had reached that conclusion three weeks earlier and had spent the three weeks since hoping it was wrong, which it was not.

"I can't stop it," Osel said. She said this plainly, the way people said things that were true and unpleasant and that they had lived with long enough to stop requiring feeling from. "I have been a notary for forty-one years and a Senior Scribe for twelve of those, and the thing I was given the authority to do is witness and record. I do not authorize. I do not command. I do not stop." She folded her hands on the desk. "What I do is write it down. And what the canonical instrument set will require, when it is convened at the Zone, is a notary present."

"The instruments haven't been convened yet," Vega said.

"No. They haven't." Osel held her eyes. "When they are convened and sealed, they cannot be unsealed. What a notary observes beforehand, in the conduct of her professional preparation function, is a different category of record entirely." She turned the Year 1104 record over once more, not opening it, the way a woman turns over a thing she has stopped needing to read because she already knows what is in it. "The Liturgy does not keep a vault to keep things safe, Athen-Moor. It keeps a vault to keep things unusable. I have watched it do that with smaller truths than this one for forty-one years." She let that sit. "I need a Scribe at the Sinter Zone before Prelacze convenes the instrument set. A Scribe who has seen what has been moving north. Whose seal is already on the account of what happened at the crater aperture before that account has been authorized for redaction."

"You're sending me to be a witness."

"I'm sending you to be the record," Osel said. "These are not the same thing."

She let this settle too.

Then: "Your sister is there, or will be. Dame Lyra." She said the name without particular affect. "Not because I arranged it. Because the recovery instrument pointed that direction, and so did your sister's competence, and so did the fact that she cannot, under any circumstances, leave alone a thing she has been told to leave alone." Something in Osel's face came as close to warmth as it appeared to be able to come. "I thought you should know."

Vega took a breath and let it go. "What do you need me to do when I get there."

"Go," Osel said. "See what happens. Write it down."

She said this in the tone of someone who had given the instruction before, or who had held it long enough that the giving of it had become necessary rather than merely practiced. Vega understood the difference. She had spent three years learning to hear it.

---

She walked out of the Registry at the ninth hour. She did not say goodbye to anyone, which took a certain amount of discipline to manage - she had colleagues, a deputy notary she had been training for seven months, a woman at the commissary who set aside the good bread for her on the mornings she arrived early enough to catch it. She knew their names and the shapes of their days. She had not said goodbye because there was no way to explain where she was going without also explaining why, and the why was not a thing she could put into words at present without the words themselves becoming a canonical incident. She carried her travel bag, her notary seal, and the working copy, which had no legal standing whatsoever and which she brought anyway, because Osel had said *write it down* and the working copy was where she wrote things down.

The convoy north was four supply wagons, a Heloth for heavy freight, and a garrison escort of six soldiers - standard north-road complement. She had a passenger notation on the manifest. The convoy commander reviewed it without comment. People from the administrative offices traveled on supply convoys occasionally. It was not unusual.

The road ran first through the alpine approaches - the particular quality of Ocipheus mountain light, the clean air, the juniper scrub on the lower slopes - and then into the middle passes, and then into the territory the Liturgy's administrative files classified as the Sinter Zone Perimeter Region, which was the designation for everything between Ocipheus's northern alpine boundary and the Pass's southern checkpoint. Vega had been as far north as the alpine ridge on three previous occasions, all administrative trips tied to garrison supply authorization. She had not been to the Pass.

She sat on the wagon bench with her travel bag between her feet and watched the terrain change. Not dramatically. Incrementally, the way things changed when something had been working on them for a long time. The rock formations at the roadside were subtly wrong - the strata did not lie the way Lyra would have described it, she thought, and then stopped thinking about how Lyra would describe things, because that was a road that went somewhere she could not afford to travel yet.

The first military column passed on the second day. Moving north. Atrament equipment - she recognized the housing pattern on the freight wagons from the inventory certification formats she had processed a hundred times. Forty soldiers, supply complement, three battle-rig carriers, each carrier a broad flat wagon with a specific hardpoint mounting whose rated capacity she had certified the paperwork for and had never once expected to see loaded. She counted the hardpoints as the column went by. The convoy commander noted her counting without comment.

The second column passed four hours later. Ocipheus garrison colors, a different freight format entirely: the canonical property mark of the Liturgy's permanent contingency reserve, stamp P-7-R, which she recognized because she had certified its unsealing herself, six months ago, on a standing order renewal she had processed and filed and not thought about again until the working copy made her think about everything again. The reserve had been unsealed three times in recorded canonical history. The first was the Year 1079 Sinter catastrophe, the event that had destroyed House Sinter and drawn the quarantine line. The second was the Pallor-Atrament border action of Year 1121, which had threatened the Vigil line's eastern garrison. The third was now.

The third column passed at dusk, moving slower, its cargo shapeless under the wagon covers - not battle rigs, not standard freight, something else she had no category for. She watched until it took the bend and was gone.

She opened the working copy and dated the page. She wrote the day, the columns, their complements, the freight marks. She wrote: *Three columns, this road, this convoy. By the pattern of the last six weeks' instruments, at least nine more are staged or already moving on roads I have not personally traveled. Twelve, if the pattern holds. I did not choose to count this way. The instruments taught me to.*

She wrote: *The contingency reserve mark I saw today is present in material form on this road. I am not the person who authorized its release. I am the person who certified the authorization.*

She looked at that sentence for a while.

She wrote: *I am the notary.*

She did not underline it. She did not need to.

---

The Pass checkpoint was a complex of three stone buildings set where the mountains pressed the road down to its narrowest point - older, she thought, than the current patrol corps, older perhaps than the Vigil designation itself: shelter someone had built at this pass a long time ago because the pass had needed sheltering, and the Order of the Watch had simply moved its roster desk into what was already standing. The checkpoint hut had a door that did not sit quite square in its frame and a desk bolted to a floor that had held desks bolted to it for a long time. The departure log was ruled paper, entries in a patrol officer's hand.

The officer on duty reviewed her Scribe credentials carefully, which was correct of him, and then reviewed them again, which was not quite correct but was understandable. "Scribes don't usually come north," he said.

"No," she said. "They don't."

He looked at the credentials, then at the road behind her where the columns had already gone through, then at her again - the look of a man confronted with something that did not fit any category he had for it but that appeared, on inspection, to be entirely in order. He stamped the log, wrote her name in the entry column, and said: "You'll want your cold-weather kit."

"I have it."

He nodded her through.

---

The first hour past the checkpoint, she understood what the vigil soldiers meant when they said the ground paid attention.

It was not an atmospheric change. The light was the same mountain light, the cold the same late-season cold, the sky the same pale grey. The road was the same road. What changed was more physical than any of that - the sensation of standing somewhere oriented toward something other than the sky, something lower than the ground itself, present the way a sleeping animal is present in a barn: not announcing itself, but making itself known through the quality of everything nearby. The cold was colder than the altitude explained. The air had a thickness to it that was not moisture. The rock at the roadside was wrong the way rock is wrong when it has grown around an intruding vein - not structurally false, only shaped by pressure from a direction it was never meant to bear.

She stood in the road and let herself feel it for a moment rather than trying to note it.

Then she took out the working copy. *Northern side of the Pass, first hour. The terrain carries a physical quality I will call pressure, for lack of a better word. Downward. It does not read as threat. It reads as attention.*

She paused, then wrote: *I understand now why the vigil rotation is eight months.*

She walked on.

The road into the Zone was a long descent, not steep but continuous, the ground dropping toward a basin the way ground drops toward a river that is no longer there. The mountain ecology thinned and changed around her. The juniper gave way to something with no entry in any Liturgy botanical catalogue she had ever copied - low, ash-pale, leafless in a way that was not seasonal dormancy but structure, each branch ending in a blunt point rather than a bud, as if it had grown to a decision and then simply stopped deciding. The ground between the pale growth wore a crust that was not frost and not rock and not anything she had a name for. She crouched and looked at it without touching it. She was a Scribe; she was also Lyra's sister, and the only practically useful consequence of that second fact was that she had grown up hearing, at length, the correct procedure for not touching things she could not categorize.

She did not touch it.

By the time the crater came into view she had walked four hours on the northern side of the Pass, and the sensation of downward attention had become a thing she carried the way one carries a low sound stopped consciously hearing but that has reorganized the shape of the silence around it.

The crater was not what she had expected. She had expected a wound, or a ruin. What she found instead was a place where the ground simply ended - not with a cliff or a visible edge, but the way a road ends when the cartographer has run out of what is known: a line, and beyond the line, the unknown standing where the known used to be. The basin's rim was no longer geological. The stone had been consumed back, drawn inward and downward over a long span by something that had been patient about it. What stood there now was organic - dark, textured, warmer to the eye than the rock around it, moving with a slow, almost imperceptible pulse, like something breathing in a very deep sleep, and wet: not with rain, there had been none, but a wetness that clung and beaded the way it does on a thing that is healing and cannot manage to finish healing, welling up dark along its folds and, where the folds pulled deepest apart, showing a thread of something paler beneath - amber, near gold, there and gone, the color of water stirred through with honey. The pulse ran down the walls of the basin until distance swallowed it. Whether there was a bottom, she could not see from where she stood.

She wrote: *Sinter basin rim. The crater is deeper than the vigil maps account for. The walls are not geological. The organic material has a visible pulse and a visible wetness, dark at the surface, paler and gold-toned where it shows through the deepest folds. The depth is not measurable by eye.*

She wrote: *This is what the contingency reserve was unsealed for. This is what twelve columns are pointed at.*

She looked at her own hand on the page, the small precise Registry letters she had shaped ten thousand times, and understood that what she was writing carried no authorization stamp, no counter-brief, no certification from the Canon's office to qualify it.
