The zone began on the other side of it in the specific way that no commission report she had read had ever managed to describe, and she had read most of the reports the vigil archive held. The standard language was: *anomalous atmospheric and biological conditions consistent with sustained membrane-adjacent activity.* Technically accurate. Also the kind of sentence built to survive contact with an auditor rather than contact with the thing itself.

The Vigil veterans had a shorter sentence. *The ground pays attention.*

The air changed before she had gone ten meters past the gate. Not colder. Not warmer. Thicker in a way that had nothing to do with humidity - the pressure of a room before a storm has committed to being a storm, when the light is already very slightly wrong and nobody has said so yet. Bluer than the light she'd been walking in for three weeks. Filtered, the way glass filters a color out of what passes through it without removing the light itself. The membrane tears let in daylight from somewhere else, each one rimmed faintly gold where the skin had never quite closed over it. She had written papers about this. The papers had not mentioned what it felt like to stand inside the sentence.

And then, not at any meter marker she could have pointed to, the ground began to pay attention.

Directional. Ahead and below. Steady, not pulsing - the quality of something that had been doing this for a very long time and would keep doing it regardless of whether anyone found it notable. It had noticed her instead. That was the order of things here.

"It started for me at the gate," Orim said, at her shoulder. He had not reached for the preparation in his breast pocket. She had expected him to. "I've heard the veterans describe this my whole working life. I assumed they were building it up." He looked at the ground the way a man looks at something that is looking back without eyes to do it. "They were not building it up."

"The Rod?"

"Still," he said. "Not the soft refusal I got at the Forge Hearth. That was a door closing. This is - still. Nothing pushing back because there is nothing to push against." He kept his hand in his pack, not drawing anything out of it. "In fifty years of contract work, that has not happened to me at a live site. Only at dead ones."

She had expected something like it. The Forge Hearth had refused him outright, and the Forge Hearth carried a fraction of what lay ahead of them now. A Rod-sensitized reading assumed the membrane was a surface - something with a face you could turn toward the light and read. Whatever this was, it had stopped being a surface a long time ago.

The trees were wrong in a way that took looking twice to locate. Longer between the lower branches. Straighter in the grain than growth should run. The leaves gave back a light that was fractionally different from the light that struck them, filtered through a spectrum she didn't have better words for than the Year 1104 survey's own - *evidence of progressive adaptation to anomalous light conditions* - which she had read as documentation and now understood as description.

Rowe came up on her right, saying nothing, doing the three-height horizontal scan she had watched him run in six environments: low, middle, high, repeat. The new shell ran the pattern a fraction of a second slower than the old one had, a hitch in the shoulder actuator on the turn from middle to high. He had not commented on it. She had not asked. Some things a man kept to himself even when the man was mostly voice-box and borrowed steel.

Wren moved behind them, breathing through her nose, watching the tree line with the same patient assembly she'd brought to every strange thing since Caer Glas Moor.

The preserved crossings began at the two-kilometer mark, which matched the Year 1104 survey's data on tear density this close to the Pallor approach. The first one she saw was half a meter across, crystalline, grown into the base of a tree that had closed around it over decades of ignoring what it could not digest. A small thing, from a smaller Sphere, caught mid-crossing in a state the membrane's own biology had sealed rather than let die properly: the moisture replaced with a clear, dense, membrane-adjacent mineral, holding the shape in exact suspension. She had read about the process in three papers. She had not been prepared for what it did to the shape - not the shape of a dead thing afterward, but the shape of a living thing in the instant of crossing, reaching toward something on its own side of the tear that was no longer there, and had been reaching that way for the better part of a century.

The veterans had names for the larger ones. The commission's assessors had clinical designations instead - codes, catalogue numbers - and she understood now why the veterans' names had outlasted the codes. A code categorized. A name looked at the thing and admitted what it had cost to look.

The Glass Whales came first in the deeper stands: six to nine meters, filter-feeders out of some high-methane Sphere no Caul biology had a word for, lying in the postures they had died in eighty, a hundred years past, encased where they fell. Through the crystal she could make out the long filtering structure, the organ mass that had supported it, the architecture of something built for an atmosphere she could not have breathed and had therefore never once considered in concrete terms. The membrane had given them two to four hours before this world's air killed them, the commission estimated. It had kept the shape since.

The Singing Spires had come through later - not in the Year 1104 survey, first noted in the Year 1178 emergency assessment, crystalline growth in clusters two to three meters, source Sphere unidentified. *They are still vibrating,* the assessment had said, and she had wondered at the phrase the way you wonder at an academic curiosity. She understood it now as a literal report. The Spires carried a resonance from wherever they had come from - some frequency of an atmosphere or a ground condition that was not here - and had not lost it in forty-eight years of being in the wrong world. She felt it in the sternum before she saw them: a vibration running outward from the cluster's center, parallel to the ground's attention and unaware of it, two frequencies sharing a chest cavity without troubling each other.

Orim went around the outside of the cluster rather than through it. She did not remark on it. He had fifty years of knowing when not to put a foot into something.

The Still Faces were the worst of the three, and the commission's own assessors had said so in three separate reports, attributing the effect to a humanoid-adjacent morphology tripping a threat-recognition response calibrated for faces. This was probably true. It did not make the Still Faces easier to walk past. Roughly half a meter, roughly ovoid, with a sensor arrangement that corresponded - in the loose sense of two entirely separate evolutionary paths converging on the same function - to a face: two bilateral structures that were not eyes, a central cluster that was not a nose, an aperture that was not a mouth, close enough to human proportion that the recognition ran before she could stop it, and then spent a long moment failing to reconcile what it had recognized.

They had preserved in the instant of dying, which the encapsulation evidence suggested had been nearly immediate.

They looked like questions. Not distress, or not only distress. The specific expression of something meeting a set of physical laws its body had never been built to survive, and discovering this had no category to be filed under.

She did not count them. She noted that she was choosing not to.

The zone smelled like the air before lightning - a detail no commission assessment she had read had recorded, which meant either the assessors had missed it or it had developed since. A hundred and forty-seven years of accumulated crossing residue. The membrane working continuously at a wound it could not fully close.

"There is an account in the Long Seat's archive," Wren said, coming up on her left. She had been quiet since the gate, building her picture from the outside in, and now she had enough of an outside to speak from. "Pallor's holding. Year 1079, the secondary eruption. The patrol soldier who got closest and lived wrote that the walls looked like they were breathing. The session chair filed it under colloquial." A pause, precise, not self-punishing. "I told Aldric once it was colloquial too."

Lyra looked at the wrong light coming back off the wrong leaves.

"It wasn't," Wren said.

"No," Lyra said. "It isn't."

A kilometer from what the surveys called the crater's edge, the walls came up above the treeline, and the party stopped without anyone giving the order, because it was the kind of thing that stopped everyone at the same moment.

The walls were not stone.

She had known this in the abstract - from the commission surveys, from the Year 1104 assessment, from the Long Seat's eighth theory, which she carried in the place she kept things that could not be taken from her by anything that only touched language. She had known the walls were changing. She had not understood what changed looked like from outside the treeline, under this particular grey sky, at this particular distance.

The sections visible above the trees were dark in a way that had nothing to do with basalt or volcanic glass, both of which she knew from the Forge Hearth approach. A different dark - the dark of a thing that had never been stone and was doing something stone does not do. At five hundred meters she could see the layering: grown up over the old rock the way anything grows when it has eleven hundred years and nothing to interrupt it, oldest strata at the bottom, compressed and still, the upper layers doing what the lower ones no longer did.

The upper layers were warm. She felt it at that distance, a warmth with no sun behind it, issuing from inside the mass - the specific signature of something that had been alive here long enough to build a metabolism at scale.

They pulsed. Not fast. Not on any rhythm she could count outright, the way you cannot quite watch a faint star without looking to the side of it. A slow motion from deep in the layer, outward, reaching the surface - and at the surface, where the pulse crested, a faint amber sheen, wet, gone again before she could be sure she'd seen it - fading, on an interval that answered to no Caul biology in any catalogue she had read.

Year 1 to Year 1138. Eleven hundred and thirty-eight years. She had used the number in papers, in census documentation, in the institutional shorthand that turns a number into a category so it never has to be held as an actual quantity.

She held it as a quantity now, five hundred meters out, and it did not get lighter for the holding.

Beside her, Rowe had gone very still, working the terrain the way he worked ground he could not categorize by prior reference - looking at the walls, at the ground, back at the walls, assembling his own picture the way Wren assembled hers. His face carried the look of a man who had gone to France and come home believing he understood what organized construction at obscene scale looked like in service of something enormous.

He had not understood it. He was learning it now, filed behind an oval receptor set a half-degree off true.

"The Rod has no designation for this," Orim said. Quiet. Not to anyone in particular. "I have scried forty-seven membrane sites in fifty years. I have a working sense of what the membrane produces with decades of activity behind it." A pause. "I do not have one for what it produces with eleven centuries."

Wren said nothing. She was looking at the walls the way a person looks at a piece of testimony they have carried a long time as documentation, in the process of becoming, in front of her, a fact.

The rim itself was still a kilometer off, past ground the surveys marked as if it were empty. It was not going to be empty. Whatever waited between the treeline and that dark, patient weight would have to be crossed on foot, in a daylight the zone had already made its own, and none of them mistook the quiet ahead for an absence of anything.

They went on into it, and the walls kept pace with them at the edge of sight - patient, the way patient things are when they have had eleven hundred years to learn there is no reason to hurry.

[Beat 26]
﻿GREYMARCH - THE EMPTY FARMSTEADS

The road came down off the pass in long easy switchbacks built for cart traffic that had not moved on them in longer than the ruts suggested, and at the bottom the land opened into cultivated ground the way cultivated ground always opened - hedgerows, a drainage ditch, a gate hung on leather hinges gone stiff with weather.

From the height of the switchbacks, before the land closed in around them, Lyra had looked south across the valley and seen it - the thing on the horizon too large to be a hill and too wrong in its slow, glistening shift to be anything the maps had a name for. Even at that distance it read as motion: a titan mound the color of exposed muscle left too long in weather, hauling itself forward by some process she couldn't name, cilia finer than hair working in a slow tide beneath its bulk, taking anything the mound's path crossed. A stand of trees near its leading edge folded inward and vanished into the mass while she watched, the trunks going down the way a man's leg goes down under a wave - without struggle, without sound she could hear from this far off. Wren had gone very still beside her. "That's not going to reach the road," she'd said, more to hear herself confirm it than because she doubted it. "Not today." Lyra hadn't asked what tomorrow looked like. She'd looked at it long enough to fix its position against the sun, filed it the way Rowe filed the readouts he didn't share, and gone on down into Greymarch, because there was nothing in six years of Vigil training that told her what a person was meant to do about a horror that size except keep walking and hope the walls between her and it held for longer than she needed them to.

Lyra had walked into a hundred farm districts in six years of Vigil postings. She knew the shape of this one before she knew its name. Orim found the name on a boundary stone half a kilometer back, moss-grown, the lettering shallow with age: GREYMARCH COMMONS, and under it, smaller, a magistrate's name long dead by the calendar such a stone implied.

"Farming district," Orim said. "Big enough for its own boundary stone, not big enough for its own garrison. There'd have been forty families here. Maybe fifty."

"Would have been."

"Yes."

The fields told her that before anything else did. Not fallow - fallow had a rhythm to it, a rotation a farmer chose. These had simply stopped, mid-season, the grain gone to seed and lodged flat in a weather event nobody had been alive to bind and stack afterward. A hay rick stood collapsed on one side, black with the kind of rot that took years, not one bad autumn. Whatever had happened here had happened long enough ago that the last hand to touch this ground had died before Lyra was born, and recently enough that the roof beams of the nearest farmhouse still held their shape rather than their collapse.

The quiet was the thing she noticed hardest, because farmland was never quiet. Even abandoned, it argued with itself - birds in the hedges, insects in the grass, a hundred small systems still running in the absence of the hands that had once directed them. Greymarch had none of that. It had the stillness of the zone itself, the stillness she'd been walking inside since the gate - not the absence of movement, but movement that had learned to happen only when it believed itself unwatched.

"Rowe."

"Nothing moving," he said. The oval receptor swept low, middle, high, the hitch in the shoulder actuator catching on the turn from middle to high the way it always did now, a fraction of a second he'd stopped apologizing for weeks back. "That's not the same as nothing here."

"No," she said. "It isn't."

They went in through the gate gap rather than over the hedge - Wren had checked the hedge first and found it grown through with something pale and faintly luminous none of them wanted to put a boot near - single file down a track that had once been wide enough for two carts abreast.

---

The first body was in the yard of the second farmhouse. Lyra saw it from thirty meters and thought, at that distance, that it was a scarecrow - the posture wrong for a person, arms out at an angle no living shoulder held for more than a few seconds, the torso reading flat and empty the way sun-bleached canvas read flat and empty stretched over a frame. She revised the thought at twenty meters, when the shape resolved into the unmistakable geometry of a ribcage opened outward like a door built to swing on hinges it did not have, and did not revise it again, because there was nothing left to revise it into.

The dead man - she made herself use the word, made herself hold him as a man rather than a specimen, the way Orim held the Still Faces by name instead of number - had been dead long enough that most of what identified him as a person had gone the way of everything left out in weather for years. Boots, mostly rotted. A belt buckle, corroded green. Hands folded in his lap in a posture someone had arranged him into, once, before whatever came afterward made the arranging meaningless.

His ribs had been opened from the inside.

The sternum had not been broken by force applied against it. It had been forced outward, in two clean hinged halves, the way a door is a door because it is built to open on a line rather than shatter along one - except that no human sternum was built to open on anything, and this one had learned to anyway, sometime after whatever had put it in the ground had finished with the rest of him.

Inside the cavity, where a heart and lungs had been doing the ordinary unremarkable work of keeping a farmer alive for however many decades he'd managed it, there was a nest.

She would not have called it that on sight. It took Orim's voice at her shoulder, flat with the specific effort of a man keeping his voice flat on purpose, to give it the word.

"Nest," he said.

She'd have reached for a different word, if he'd said nothing and let her sit with it a moment longer - something with less to do with what birds built and more to do with what a body did before it had finished deciding to be a body. But he said nest, and nest was a word with edges to it, a shape she could hold without holding what it implied, so she let it be nest and did not go looking for the truer one.

It was black - not the black of shadow, which is an absence, but the black of something wet that had dried into itself in layers, glossy where thin and matte where it had built up thick, draped over the ribs' interior curve like a hand had reached in and smeared something across the bone and let it set. Threaded through it in three or four places, small, curled, still, were shapes with the wrong proportion of a thing not finished growing. Not eggs. Not larvae, though the word wanted to apply itself and she let it stand, provisionally, until a better one arrived - fetal in the particular sense of underdevelopment rather than smallness, limbs folded in configurations that hadn't decided yet what they'd eventually be for, joints doubled where a joint should be single, slick with the same ichor that filled the cavity around them, as if the nest and the things inside it were not two categories but one continuous material at different stages of deciding to be something else.

"They use the chest," Orim said. "Not the whole body. The chest."

"Why the chest."

"I don't know." He said it plainly, without the reflexive authority of a man who'd spent fifty years being asked questions he was expected to answer. "Warmth, maybe, while there was still some to have. Room, maybe. I know the pattern. I have never known the reason for it, in fifty years, and I am not going to start pretending to you now."

Wren had gone to the doorway rather than the yard, and her voice came back low, controlled - the voice of someone who had decided a while back that surprise cost more than readiness.

"There are more inside."

There were four more inside, in postures the household had died in rather than been arranged into - a woman at what had been a hearth, a smaller shape in what had been a cot that Lyra made herself look at once and did not look at again, not out of weakness, but because she needed the part of her mind that catalogued horror to have room left for whatever came after. Each opened the same way. Each held the same black nest, in varying stages - one nearly empty, the ichor gone dull and cracked like old paint; one so recently disturbed that something had clearly hatched and gone, the black smeared wet down what remained of the ribs in a trail she did not follow past the doorway.

"How old," she said.

"The house?" Orim looked at the roof beams, the specific quality of rot in the thatch. "Twenty years since anyone kept it. Twenty-five."

"The nests."

"That's a different question, and I don't have an answer you'll like better than not having one." He crouched by the woman at the hearth, careful, not touching. "Some of these are old. Some are not old at all. Whatever's doing this hasn't stopped. It's just thinned, out here, farther from wherever it's thickest. This is the edge of the wound; not the wound."

Rowe had not gone into the house. He stood in the doorway with the rifle at low ready, doing the pattern, and when Lyra came back out to him the oval receptor was fixed on the dead man in a way that told her, before he spoke, that the readout had come again, unbidden. He didn't offer it aloud. She didn't ask him to. Something in the set of his shoulders - the new shell's, wider than his old ones, still not quite his - told her he'd rather carry this one a while before he set it down in front of anyone.

---

They found the face-worms in the ditch line an hour past the second farmhouse, and Wren saw them first, because Wren looked at ground the way the rest of them looked at tree lines and doorways.

"Don't," she said, before Lyra had registered anything to be warned off, and put a hand flat in the air between Lyra's boot and where her next step would have landed.

The ditch had gone to standing water, black-sheened like the nests, and moving in it were things the length of her forearm, pale where the black hadn't yet claimed them, worming with the blind undirected motion of creatures that had never had eyes to direct themselves by.

Except that they had faces.

Not the loose correspondence the Still Faces carried two kilometers back - two bilateral structures near enough to trip a threat-recognition response built for something else. These had the actual proportion: a jaw, a brow, the soft geography of a human face compressed and elongated onto a body with none of the structure a face required to mean anything. Mouths working at nothing. An expression that wasn't an expression, because there was no mind behind it to hold one - the physical residue of a shape that had once needed to mean something and no longer did.

They writhed. That was the whole of what they did, blind and powerless in the most literal sense the word offered, thrashing weakly against the black water and each other, present in the world only to go on writhing until whatever process had made them decided to make them into something else. A harrower wanted to kill her. These wanted nothing. Lyra found that worse.

"Leave them," Orim said, quiet. "There's nothing here to fight and nothing here to save."

She left them, and noted that she was choosing not to look back. Overhead, circling the third farmhouse high enough that she took them at a glance for crows, were the birds - and revised the thought when one dropped low enough to show her the wings.

They were not whole. Each wingspan carried gaps where the flight feathers should have run unbroken, the membrane torn through and healed wrong, so the wing read less like a wing than like a kite flown too hard into a hedge and patched with whatever came to hand - ragged at every edge, holding air by some margin she wouldn't have bet on. The beak was the part that stopped being mistakable for a crow's: hooked back on itself, sickle-curved, built for opening rather than pecking, and wet black - the same glossed sheen as the nests, seeping continuously from the base of the skull, the bird showing no sign of minding. Three of them lifted off the yard in a ragged scatter as the party came into the open, wingbeats loud and uneven, torn membrane snapping like wet canvas in a wind.

"Scavenging line," Rowe said. "Same work crows do anywhere." The receptor tracked them toward the tree line. "Doesn't mean they won't change their minds about what's worth scavenging."

She let that sit, and filed it where things went that she'd need again before the night was finished.

---

They saw the first of them across the ditch line, upright among the lodged grain of the fourth farmhouse's outer field, and for three or four full seconds nobody in the party said anything at all.

It stood the approximate height and proportion of a man, wrapped shoulder to boot in a suit sewn from - she made herself look long enough to be sure, because not looking would have cost her more later - hide. Not one hide. Dozens, patched and re-patched, seams run in every direction a desperate hand could manage, some clearly animal, coarse-haired, cured badly enough to still carry a shape-memory of the flank it had come from, and some - she made herself hold this the way Orim held the Still Faces, by fact rather than flinch - not animal at all. The stitching didn't care which was which. Neither, evidently, had whoever wore it.

The head was the wrongness that told her this was not a person in a suit doing sensible things in a bad place. It rose from a collar too large for the shoulders under it, bulbous, the whole curve of it studded with small wet gleams that took her a full breath to understand were eyes - not two, not four, a scattered handful set without symmetry or apparent plan, some blinking out of sequence with the others, none of them obviously looking at anything in particular and all of them, somehow, giving the impression of being looked at from every angle at once.

Limbs hung from a rig lashed about its torso - dead limbs, human by the joint and animal by the length, strapped on rather than worn, swaying loose with each step the way a coat's empty sleeves swing on a man walking fast. Not weapons. Not obviously anything. Tools, maybe, or trophies, or spares.

It was working at a section of collapsed fence with its own hands, the ones actually load-bearing at the ends of its own arms, a patience in the motion that had nothing frantic in it - the motion of someone completing a task they had already decided was worth completing.

"Rowe," she said, very quietly.

"Two more," he said. "East, working the tree line. Same gait."

"Weapons?"

"Tools. I think." A beat. "I can't tell the difference on these, and I don't like that I can't."

Orim had gone still in the specific way he went still around Rod readings that came back wrong. "I don't have a pattern for this. Not raiders. Not spawn. The gait's - deliberate. Purposeful. That's not a thing I've seen the zone produce on its own."

"Which means?"

"Which means I don't know if I'm looking at someone who walked in here on purpose, in that suit, for reasons of their own." He didn't finish it the easy way. "Or something the zone built that learned to wear the walking-in as a shape."

The nearer figure straightened from the fence, unhurried, and turned its bulbous, many-eyed head toward the ditch line, toward them, and held there.

Nobody moved. Wren's hand had found a dagger hilt without, Lyra was fairly sure, Wren having decided to put it there - the reflex ahead of the thought. Rowe's rifle stayed at low ready, not raised, the receptor doing its pattern across the figure with a patience that had none of the certainty it usually carried into a kill.

The figure held the look a four-count, five, longer than any animal would have spent confirming a threat and shorter than any person would have spent deciding whether to be afraid - and then it turned back to the fence and went on working, unbothered, as if the four of them standing at the ditch's edge with weapons half-raised had registered as nothing worth revising a task over.

"It's not coming for us," Orim said, low.

"No." Lyra didn't move yet either. "That doesn't mean I know what it is."

They backed off along the ditch line rather than the direct route, unhurried, nobody willing to call it a retreat and nobody willing to call it anything else, and the figures went on with their work behind them, tool-using, purposeful, and utterly unreadable, and Lyra carried the not-knowing with her the rest of the day the way she carried everything else Greymarch had put in front of her that she couldn't put down.

"Suits," Wren said finally, once the tree line had closed behind the last of them. "Someone made those. Sewed them. That takes hands and time and a reason."

"Or something learned to look like it did," Rowe said.

Nobody had an answer for either possibility that made the other one less likely, and nobody offered one.

---

The deer came out of the hedge line an hour before the light started to go, and for the length of one held breath Lyra thought, with something close to relief, that it was simply a deer - the kind of ordinary living thing this farmland should have held in abundance and had, until this moment, conspicuously not shown her.

Then it screamed.

Deer did not scream. She knew this in the body rather than the books, and the sound coming out of the animal crossing the field toward them was not the bleat or bark a startled deer sometimes made - higher, longer, a ragged edge at the top no deer's throat should have produced, and it did not stop. It went on screaming as it ran, as if the running and the screaming were two separate things happening to two separate wills sharing one body, and looking at it - really looking, past the first relieved misreading - she understood why.

The deer's own head thrashed at the end of its own neck in the posture of an animal in terror, eyes white-rimmed, legs driving in the desperate rhythm of a creature discovering its own body would not let it stop. And rising from its back, just behind the shoulder, where no deer had ever grown anything, was a second head.

It was small - perhaps the size of a large dog's - black, glossed with the same wet sheen as the nests and the beaks and the ditch water, the proportions unfinished, fetal, a face that hadn't decided what a face for this purpose ought to look like and had grown one anyway out of whatever the deer's own flesh had been repurposed to provide. It did not scream. Its stillness against the animal's thrashing panic told her which will was in control and which was a passenger in its own nervous system, watching from the inside as something else drove its legs and did not let it stop.

"Lyra," Orim said, low, urgent in a register she'd rarely heard from him. "That's not - the deer isn't the threat. Whatever's riding it is using more than half the nervous system. Look at the gait. It's not choosing where it's going. It's being driven."

"Toward us."

"Toward something. I don't think it knows the difference anymore."

Rowe raised the rifle and did not fire, and she understood why without asking: the second head sat close along the spine, angled such that a shot that took it would likely take the deer's own skull with it, and he was doing the arithmetic on a shot he did not yet have.

The deer screamed again as it came, close enough now that she could hear under the scream the animal's own labored breathing, ordinary and terrified and nothing wrong about it at all, buried under the wrongness riding on top of it. Its eyes found hers - she would think about this afterward, that whatever remained of its own mind used the last purchase it had over its own eyes to look at the thing that might end this - and in that look there was nothing she could call anything but a plea, wordless, entirely without the vocabulary to ask for what it wanted and entirely clear about wanting it anyway.

"Lyra-" Wren started.

"I see it."

She stepped into its path rather than away from it, shield up, and took the charge the way she'd have taken any charge - angled, turning rather than stopping - except this charge had two different things driving it, and neither fully accounted for what the other was going to do a half-second before it did it, so the impact came wrong, twisted, the deer's own legs trying at the last instant to shy from the shield in an animal instinct the rider hadn't suppressed fast enough. She felt both failures travel up through the shield at once - the deer's flinch, and under it, a half-beat later, the black thing's correction, forcing the flinch back into the line it wanted.

The Dissolution Blade went into the black head first - chosen deliberately, in the fraction of a second she had to choose anything, because whatever was riding this animal was the thing doing violence, and the animal underneath was the thing being murdered by degrees - and the Catalyst compound in the blade's channels found the wound and would not let it close. The black head made a short wet sound and went still.

The deer did not go still.

It kept screaming - the driving will silenced, its own body released back to a nervous system too damaged, too overridden to survive on its own - thrashing legs finding no purchase, blood coming dark and wrong from where the black head had rooted itself into the spine over some period of time none of them wanted to guess at.

Lyra put the blade through its throat in one motion, low, fast, the mercy stroke she'd have given a wounded warhorse on any battlefield she'd ever stood on, and the screaming stopped, and the quiet that came after it was the worst quiet she'd heard since the gate.

She stood over it a moment longer than the kill required.

"It didn't choose any of that," she said, to no one, or to Orim, or to the part of herself that kept a private ledger of things she'd had to end that hadn't asked to be what they were.

"No," Orim said. "It didn't."

Rowe said nothing. The receptor stayed fixed on the black head a long moment, and whatever readout came behind the dead lens, he did not offer it, and this time she did not think it was because he wanted to carry it alone. She thought it was because there was nothing in it that would have made this easier to have done.

---

They reached the barn an hour before full dark, on the fourth farmstead's holding - the biggest of the four they'd passed, a house with two chimneys and outbuildings enough to suggest a family that had done well before whatever ended them, and a barn set back on a rise that put its loft window above the tree line. A long room ran along one end of the ground floor, a bar counter still standing against the wall among the collapsed remains of trestle tables - the district's tavern, the kind of barn-and-bar a commons built when it was too small to need two separate buildings for harvest storage and harvest drinking, and had never expected to need either one abandoned.

"We shelter here," Lyra said. "Loft. One door up, ladder we can pull."

"Agreed," Orim said. "I'd rather have height and one way in than ground level and four."

The ladder held. The loft floor held, tested board by board before any of them put full weight on it, Wren going first with the patient method she brought to every unknown surface - contact, confirm, continue. The glassless loft window framed a darkening sky and, past the nearer tree line, a suggestion of something taller and darker that might have been distant hills and might, Lyra thought, have been the walls themselves, patient the way the ground had been patient since the gate.

They ate cold rations. Nobody suggested a fire.

"Rowe," Lyra said, once the last of the light had gone the color it went before it went entirely, "the rifle. Show me the lead again."

He did, without asking why she wanted it now - nothing else useful to do with the hour before full dark, and something ordinary to do with her hands was its own kind of ballast against everything the day had put in front of her. "It's not about the gun," he said, resetting her grip a half-inch back. "It's about where the thing's going to be, not where it is." She had spent six years learning where a blade needed to be right now. This asked something else of her, and she was slower at it than she wanted to be, and he told her so without unkindness, and she kept at it until the light failed entirely.

Wren took first watch on the ladder head, dagger loose in hand, and Orim settled against the loft's back wall with the Rod's case beside him, unopened, and did not sleep either, not at first, though Lyra noted the specific slowing of his breathing sometime past full dark that told her he had, eventually, gone under.

Rowe did not sit.

He took the window - the one opening that gave a line on the yard below, the dead man's fields, the tree line past them - and stood there through the whole of what followed, the oval receptor doing its low, middle, high, repeat, patient in the exact way the ground itself was patient. Lyra understood, watching him in the last of the light before she made herself close her own eyes, that he would still be standing there when she woke. He did not sleep. Had not slept once in the eight months since Verlaine built the shell that carried whatever remained of the man he'd been, and this - the standing, the watching, the pattern run through hours that asked nothing of him but to keep doing it - was the closest thing to rest the trapped mind inside that borrowed steel was ever going to get.

She did not know if that thought was meant to comfort her. It didn't, entirely. It also didn't leave her.

---

The loft held its silence for the better part of three hours, and it was a silence with texture to it - an old building settling, wind finding the gaps in the boards and singing through them low, the fields outside doing nothing at all, which after Greymarch's first farmhouse felt less like peace than like held breath.

Rowe spoke first, quiet, not turning from the window.

"Something's under the yard."

Lyra was awake before she'd finished deciding to be. Wren, at the ladder head, had already gone still in the particular way that meant she'd heard it before Rowe said it and had been waiting to see if anyone else had.

"Under."

"Not on it. Under it." The receptor held on one patch of yard, near the collapsed hay rick, where the ground in the last grey light showed nothing Lyra could see was different from any other patch of ground in Greymarch. "Vibration in the boards. Regular. Something moving through soil, not over it."

Orim was up, the Rod's case in hand, still unopened. "How many."

"I don't have a count for underground."

"Try."

Rowe was quiet a moment, the shell's stillness doing the work a held breath would have done in a body still made of the original meat. "More than one."

The first scorpion broke the yard's surface eleven seconds later - earth heaving upward not in a scatter, the way a burrowing animal's exit ordinarily threw soil, but in a clean cored punch, as if the ground had decided to open along a seam it had been keeping ready - and it was fully clear of the hole and moving, low, fast, segmented, black and slick with the same ichor sheen that coated everything else Greymarch had shown her that day.

It was built along a scorpion's general architecture the way the hijacked deer had been built along a deer's - recognizable at the first glance, wrong in every particular the second glance found time for. The tail carried too many segments, curling in a slow double coil rather than the single clean arc a scorpion's tail described, like a thing that had grown one joint too many and simply gone on using all of them. The pincers were underdeveloped, vestigial, folded close to a body that plainly did not need them for whatever its actual work was. And the whole of it was draped - not stained, draped, the way the nest had been draped over the dead man's ribs - in the same black vernix the creature had been grown in and never finished shedding.

"Two more," Rowe said, and fired, once, into the lead scorpion's forward segment before it had covered half the yard. The round went in and the creature kept coming, and Lyra understood, watching it close the distance at a speed that had nothing to do with its ungainly look, that whatever these things lacked in armor they made up for in a nervous system built to ignore damage that would have stopped anything Caul biology produced.

"Ladder," she said. "Wren-"

"On it." Wren was already moving to the loft's edge, blades out, watching the ladder head the way she watched everything - economy first, commitment second.

The scorpions did not come up the ladder. They came up the barn's own wall, through gaps in the old siding boards Lyra hadn't marked as gaps until something was moving through one, segmented legs finding purchase on wood weathered soft enough to give them grip, and the first crested the loft floor's edge eight feet from where Orim stood with the Rod's case still shut in his hand.

"Orim!"

He turned too slowly - not from fear, she understood afterward, but because fifty years of reading threat through the Rod had built a reflex that reached, first, for the case, and the case had nothing in it that would help him here. The half-second he spent discovering that was the half-second the scorpion's tail needed.

It struck him low, along the outside of the thigh, a single fast motion with none of the deliberate menace stories gave a scorpion's sting - economical, almost incidental, the way a man swats an insect without breaking stride - and Orim made a sound she'd never heard him make in six years of knowing him, short, surprised, more confusion than pain.

Then his leg went out from under him and did not answer when he tried to make it take his weight again, and the confusion in his face turned, over three long seconds, into something closer to the deer's own plea, wordless and without a vocabulary built for asking.

"Paralytic," Lyra said, already moving. "Don't fight it - Wren, cover him, don't let anything close-"

"Moving."

Rowe put two rounds into the scorpion over Orim before it fully turned to finish whatever it had started, and this time the shots found something that mattered - a seam in the segmented plating along the creature's underside, exposed as it reared to strike again, and it folded sideways off the loft's edge with a sound like wet rope snapping.

The second and third came over the wall together, and Lyra put the shield between them and Orim's body before either had fully cleared the boards, the impact traveling up through her shoulder in the same dense wrong weight every Sinterspawn strike had carried since the deer - too much mass for the visible size, a process that had never learned to build a body fast and light both.

Orim's hand found her ankle, grip weak, fingers not quite closing the way fingers should, but there - and when she spared him a half-second's glance, his eyes were open, tracking, entirely present behind a body that had stopped answering him.

"Can you speak."

"-yes." Thick. Slurred at the edges, the way a man's speech slurred with cold or with drink, though this was neither. "Can't - feel the leg. Arm's going."

"Stay down. Stay still. We've got you."

"Wasn't planning-" He didn't finish it. Whatever wit he'd meant to close the sentence with, the paralytic took before he could spend it, and Lyra understood the actual stakes of the night laid bare: not whether they could kill the things coming over the wall, but whether they could kill enough of them, fast enough, to keep a man who could not move his own legs from becoming the easiest kill in the yard.

Wren took the third scorpion the way she'd taken the small kin-creatures three days back - not meeting it, arriving where it wasn't going to expect her, both daggers finding the seam behind the foreclaw where the plating hadn't finished closing - and it went down in two pieces and did not reassemble the way, for one absurd half-second, some animal part of Lyra's mind had braced for it to.

"How many more," Lyra said.

"Don't know," Rowe said, and fired again, into the yard below rather than the loft, where the ground was still moving in places that told her the count Wren had asked for earlier didn't have a ceiling any of them could see yet.

---

The corpse-fusion things came up from the tree line while the scorpions were still working the barn's edge - a wrongness of silhouette against the last grey band of sky, too tall, too wide, moving with a lurch that had none of a single body's rhythm to it.

"Two bodies," Orim said from the floor, thick-voiced, still tracking even flat on his back with a leg that wouldn't answer him. "Maybe three. Fused." He'd seen it before she had, watching from an angle low to the ground that gave him, however useless the rest of him had become in the last two minutes, a clearer read on the thing's gait than her own upright vantage offered. "The Greymarch dead. Whatever does this doesn't need the chest cavity for everything."

The nearer of the two shambled into what light the loft's open window still gave the yard, and Lyra made herself look at it the way she'd made herself look at everything in Greymarch that day - not away, not through - and saw what Orim had already named: two human frames grown together at the torso, the join a mass of the same black glossed tissue that filled the nests, four arms moving in an uncoordinated syncopation that suggested two separate wills, or half a will split between two bodies never built to share one. The faces - one slack, one twisted in an expression that might once have been terror, both long past anything that could be called alive - hung at angles that put them looking two directions at once, tracking nothing, the aimless double gaze of something that moved because moving was what its combined mass had been repurposed to do.

It came at Rowe's flank, slow, and he put a bayonet through the join where the black tissue ran thinnest, and it did not fall so much as stop - the momentum going out of both halves at once, the ungainly structure settling to the loft floor's edge, the simple physical fact of a thing that had been moving and had, now, stopped.

The second one Wren took from the ladder head, low, before it had fully cleared the top rung - a dagger through what might have been a throat on either fused face - and it went back down the ladder the way it had come up, taking two scorpions with it in a tangle that ended, from the sound of it, somewhere in the yard below.

The ichor-birds came last, drawn by whatever in the night carried the sound and smell of a kill site to wherever they'd been roosting, three of them wheeling low over the yard on wings that shouldn't have held air and somehow did, sickle beaks catching what little light remained. They did not press the loft. They circled, patient, opportunist, waiting for whatever was going to be left when the fighting stopped - a quieter horror than the rest: scavengers who had learned that farmland like this reliably produced something worth waiting for.

---

The last scorpion came up through a gap in the loft floor itself, where the old boards had rotted enough at one corner to let something patient find its way through, and it was on Rowe before the receptor's pattern caught the motion - low, middle, high, and this came from beneath, outside the pattern's design.

He took the strike on the shell's forearm rather than anywhere the paralytic could reach him through whatever his borrowed nervous system did with a sting, and the readout came again - mid-strike, unbidden, printing behind the dead lens the way it always did - and this time he spoke two words of it aloud, flat, before the rest of the fight swallowed the moment whole.

> INTAKE - SUBJECT PARTIAL
> MASS: est. 40-60 kg, distribution segmented, tail-heavy
> LOCOMOTION: subterranean approach, no comparable entry vector in prior catalogue
> ARMOR: chitin-analog, ichor-sheathed, seam density irregular
> WEAK POINT: ventral seam, forward third
> SPHERE-SOURCE: - SIGNAL DEGRADED -
> CATALOGUE STATUS: UNRESOLVED. RE-SCAN ADVISED.

"Signal's bad," he said, and put the bayonet into the ventral seam the readout had found him before the thought had fully finished forming, and the scorpion folded around the blade and stopped moving. He did not say anything else about the readout. Some part of him - the part that was still a man from a mill town, not a war machine's clean accounting - had already decided the degraded signal was the barn's fault, or the night's, or the ichor fouling the lens, and not the first thread of a thing it would take a wider field and a bigger kill to make him stop being able to explain away.

Lyra didn't ask him what the readout said. She had Orim's weight against her side by then, dragging him back from the loft's open edge to the wall where the scorpions and the fused dead couldn't reach him without coming through the whole of the rest of them first, and there was nothing in the next several minutes that had room in it for anything but the fight in front of her.

---

It ended the way these things ended - not cleanly, by degrees, until there came a stretch of thirty seconds and then a minute and then five in which nothing new came up out of the yard or over the wall or through the rotted floor, and the ichor-birds, finding nothing yet worth the risk of landing, wheeled off toward the tree line and did not come back.

Orim's leg came back to him first, feeling returning as a deep ache rather than pain, pins and needles run all the way to bone; the arm took longer. By the time the grey had started to lift toward whatever passed for morning in the Sinter zone's filtered light, he could flex the fingers, slowly, watching his own hand do it with the clinical attention of a man reassuring himself an instrument still worked.

"I'm sorry," he said, to no one in particular, or to Lyra, or to the private ledger he kept the way she kept her own. "Fifty years, and I reached for the case."

"The case doesn't read this."

"No." He said it flatly, without self-punishment, which she'd learned by now was the truest register he had for admitting a thing had cost him. "It doesn't read any of it. That's twice today the Rod's told me exactly nothing, and both times it was because there was nothing there for it to find. I don't know which is worse - a thing the Rod refuses to look at, or a thing it looks at and simply has no shape for."

Wren cleaned her blades in the grey light, the same unbothered economy she always brought to it, though Lyra noted that she looked twice at the yard below before she started - at the collapsed shapes of the fused dead and the dark husks of the scorpions - longer than the cleaning strictly required.

"They were people," Wren said. Not a question. "The fused ones. Before."

"Yes."

"How many farms like this are there. Between here and the walls."

Nobody answered her, because it was not, Lyra understood, a question anyone in the loft was equipped to give a number to, and because the number - whatever it turned out to be, whenever the commission's own surveys finally admitted to counting it - was not going to be small.

Rowe stood at the window through all of it, the receptor doing its pattern, patient, unslept, the way he had stood there since full dark and would go on standing until the party was ready to move. When Lyra caught his eye across the loft in the thin new light, there was nothing in the dead lens that told her what the night had cost him to watch alone. She thought, not for the first time, that this was its own kind of mercy and its own kind of theft, in the same borrowed body, and that she had stopped being able to tell the two apart.

"We should move before full light," she said. "Orim, can you walk."

"I can walk." He got his feet under him, slow, testing the leg the way Wren had tested the loft floor - contact, confirm, continue. "I'd rather not run for an hour or two. Past that, I'll manage."

"Then we walk an hour or two." She looked out past the yard, past the collapsed shapes the grey light was just beginning to make ugly rather than strange, toward the tree line and whatever lay past it the walls had been patiently promising since the gate. "Greymarch's behind us either way. Whatever's ahead won't get gentler for our taking the morning slow."

She was right about that, though she would not know how right until the rope.

[Beat 27]
﻿SINTERSPAWN - THE EATERS AT THE PERIMETER

The first thing she found was a rope.

It was strung between two dead trees at boot height, old and grey and gone slack in the middle, and it took her a moment to understand what it was for: a trip-line, tensioned once to a bell that had long since rusted its clapper solid. Somebody had built this. Somebody had walked this ground before her and decided it needed watching, and then the watching had stopped, and the rope had stayed.

"Vigil-line," Orim said, crouching to look at the knot without touching it. "Old pattern. Twenty years, maybe more." He straightened, slow the way his knees made him slow, and looked past it into the trees. "They stopped maintaining this stretch."

"Why."

"Because the thing it was meant to warn them about started coming from somewhere the rope wasn't."

Lyra let that sit. The ground here was the ground she'd been walking for three weeks - packed earth, exposed root, the grey standing water of a wet autumn - except that it wasn't, not exactly, the way a word isn't exactly the word you meant when you've said it too many times and it's come loose from its meaning. The trees grew the way trees grew. The moss grew the way moss grew, except in patches where it didn't, where it had gone a pale and faintly luminous white and crept up past the root collar in a shape that had nothing to do with where the shade fell. She had stopped trying to catalogue every wrongness individually about two hundred meters back. There would be too many. She would need the rest of her attention for what came next.

Rowe was already moving in the pattern she'd learned to read as work rather than wandering - low, middle, high, repeat, the rifle carried at a low ready that put no strain on the wrist. The new shell ran a half-beat behind the old one on the turn from middle to high, a hitch in the shoulder actuator he had never once mentioned and she had never once asked about. He stopped at the middle height and held there a fraction of a second longer than the pattern called for.

"Rowe."

"Track," he said. One word. He had all the words he needed; he spent them like coin he wasn't sure would be honored.

She came up beside him and looked where the oval receptor was angled. The print was pressed into a patch of the pale moss, three points of contact - she recognized the shape from the patrol reports, the same shape Wren would name for her a day's walk from here, at a different bend in a different account of this same approach, though neither of them yet knew that the story of this crossing would be told twice, in two hands, and not agree with itself. Six feet. A pack, moving at pace.

But this print was wrong in a way the reports hadn't prepared her for. Too deep on the outside edge, shallow on the inside, as though the weight it carried didn't sit where a six-legged animal's weight ought to sit. As though something about its architecture put more of itself on one side than the other and had simply gone on living that way.

"Fresh," Rowe said.

"How fresh."

He crouched, the shell's knee joints folding with a small mechanical complaint he'd have flinched at once, months ago, and didn't now. He held two fingers an inch above the depression without touching it - she had seen him do this before, at Schmalburg, reading a boot print in mud with the same patience he'd have given a shell crater. "Ten minutes," he said. "No more."

Behind them, Wren had gone very still, the particular stillness of someone doing the thing Orim called reading the room from the outside in - cataloguing before committing to a reaction. She had one dagger half-drawn already, not from panic, from the plain economy of a person who had decided a while back that surprise cost more than readiness.

"How many," Wren said.

"More than the print shows," Orim said. He had the Rod's case in his hand, unopened, held the way he'd been holding it since the gate. "There's a smell under the smell."

There was. Lyra had noticed it and set it aside the way she set aside everything that wasn't immediately actionable - old iron, and something under the iron that the iron was trying and failing to cover, a rot that hadn't finished rotting because whatever process should have finished it had been substituted, somewhere upstream, for something else. It sat low in the chest and did not clear when she breathed through her mouth instead.

The ground here, the old Vigil hands would have told her, was paying attention.

She had read that phrase for six years at a desk in Ocipheus Station and filed it under soldier's shorthand, colorful and imprecise. She had crossed the marker post three days ago and stopped believing it was imprecise. She still did not fully believe it was shorthand.

"Positions," she said, and did not have to say anything else.

---

They came from the pale moss on both flanks at once, which told her before she'd finished turning that whatever hunted here hunted in a formation smarter than the six-legged crawlers the patrol reports had warned her about - those moved single-file, at pace, feeding on momentum. These moved in a pincer, deliberately, the way a unit moves when someone or something has decided the pincer works better than the file.

The first one crossed into the light enough for her to see it clearly for perhaps three quarters of a second before it closed the distance, and in that three quarters of a second she understood two things: that it was smaller than what she'd feared, roughly the size of a hunting dog bred too long and too hungry; and that its forelegs did not match each other. The left was jointed twice, like a crawler's. The right was jointed three times, ending in something closer to a hooked spade than a claw, and it did not move the way the left leg's twin should have made it move. It compensated. It had learned, or been built, or simply happened, to run crooked and hit true anyway. Its hide had the wrongness of a thing that hadn't finished drying - a slick black film clung to it in patches, oil-thick, pooling in the hollow of every joint instead of running off the way sweat or rain would have, and under that film the skin itself looked thin, unfinished, the kind of skin a thing wears before it has earned the right to be tough.

She took it on the shield, angled, and felt the impact travel up through her shoulder in a way that told her the thing weighed more than its size accounted for - dense, packed, wrong in the hand the way everything here was wrong in the hand. She put the Dissolution Blade into it on the return stroke, low along the flank where the plating gapped, and the Catalyst compound in the blade's channels did what it always did: found the wound and refused to let it close. The creature made a short, wet sound and folded sideways into the moss and did not get up. What came out of the wound wasn't blood by the time it reached the ground - black, thick, unhurried, the color of a bruise that never fades, pooling instead of soaking in, sitting on the white moss the way oil sits on standing water, and it did not dry.

"Two left flank," Rowe said, and fired before he'd finished saying it. One went down clean, a round through what she assumed was meant to function as a skull, though it took her half a second to locate anything skull-shaped on the creature at all. The other came in low and fast enough that the shot missed the vital mass and only opened a furrow along its back, and it kept coming, and Wren was already there.

She had watched Wren fight exactly four times before this, and each time she had come away with the same observation, refined a little further: Wren did not close distance so much as arrive at it, the four seconds of the fold used the way a good gambler uses a hand of cards - not often, not carelessly, and never as the whole of the play. She didn't blink through this one. She simply wasn't where it expected her to land and then was somewhere it hadn't accounted for at all, both daggers crossing in on the joint where a foreleg met the shoulder - the reversed grip putting the edge where the muscle mass ought to be thickest, and instead finding almost nothing there, an economy of tissue that told her, an instant too late to matter, this thing hadn't grown to be armored. It had grown to be fast and hoped that would be enough.

It wasn't. She left it in three pieces and rolled clear of the fourth before Lyra had entirely registered there was a fourth.

There were, in total, six of the small ones. It took less time to kill them than it had taken to walk the last two hundred meters, and at the end of it Lyra stood in a clearing gone quiet in the specific way a clearing goes quiet after violence - not peaceful, just spent - with her sword arm singing from six separate impacts and Wren wiping one blade clean on a fistful of the pale moss with the flat unbothered economy of someone doing laundry.

"Kin," Orim said. He had not moved from where he'd stood at the start, the Rod's case still shut, still against his ribs. "Testing pack. Same as the crawlers your reports described, except-" He stopped. He was looking past the bodies, into the deeper stand of trees, at something Lyra hadn't yet turned to see. "Except crawlers don't have kin that mismatched. Crawlers breed true within a litter. These don't match each other."

"Meaning."

"Meaning whatever's making these isn't finished deciding what they're for."

He said it the way he said most things - dry, unhurried, a man reporting weather - and it was worse for the dryness, not better. Lyra had spent six years learning to hear the difference between Orim's professional calm and Orim's fear, and this was neither. This was a third thing she hadn't catalogued before, that she would only later, holding the Relic at the rim, understand as the particular quality of a mind confronting a category it had spent fifty years assuming would never actually arrive.

The trees ahead of them moved. Not wind. Weight.

---

It came out of the stand at a height that made her recalculate, in the space of one held breath, every instinct the last three weeks of monster ground had built into her. It was not fourteen feet at the shoulder the way the harrower stories from the western garrisons described - smaller than that, if smaller was the word for something that still stood taller than a war-horse at the withers - but it carried itself with none of the ponderous deliberateness the stories gave the harrowers either. It moved like something still learning what its own mass was for, a hitch in the gait that wasn't injury, that was simply an asymmetry built in from the start: three forelimbs where a harrower's stories gave it two, the third shorter and set higher on the chest, curled close in a way that looked less like a limb at rest and more like a limb still deciding what it would eventually be used for.

The hide was not membrane-grey the way the western tales described. It was slick - wet in a way nothing living in dry autumn air had any business staying wet, a film of the same oil-black residue caught in every seam and hollow of it, the kind that never dried, the way a newborn colt comes out slick and stays slick until someone thinks to towel it down, and nobody here ever had. It was a mottled dark under that film, shifting at the edges, seams of pale blue-white light running along the plate joints, pulsing - not fast, not the frantic strobe of a frightened animal, but slow, patient, on an interval she found herself starting to count without deciding to and then made herself stop counting, because counting implied she expected the number to mean something, and she had learned not to trust that instinct here.

The eyes - there were eyes, she was almost certain of that, two dark wet patches low on what functioned as a face - did not track her the way the kin's had tracked. They didn't seem to be doing the looking. Something else about it was doing the looking, and the eyes were simply where it had ended up putting them.

"Don't run," she said, low, mostly to herself, because the animal reflex that told her to was old and strong and entirely wrong for the geometry of the thing in front of her.

It came at Rowe first - she would think about that afterward, why him first, and not arrive at an answer that satisfied her - a long low charge that ate the twenty meters between the treeline and the clearing in under two seconds, and Rowe did the thing his eight months in France and his three weeks in this world had drilled into a body that answered a half-beat late and reported nothing back: he read the geometry of the charge before it had committed, and moved to a line that put a fallen log between himself and the closing mass, and fired twice into the seam where the third limb met the chest.

The rounds did nothing he could see. The creature did not slow.

"Lyra-"

She was already moving. She put the shield into its lead flank at an angle designed to turn a charge rather than stop one dead - stopping it dead was a fool's proposition against something that outweighed her by a factor she didn't want to calculate mid-swing - and felt the impact travel through the shield's resin-hardened face in a shudder that told her nothing about where the thing was soft and everything about how little she understood its architecture. It did not have a reach problem the way a harrower had a reach problem. It had no problem she recognized at all. Every instinct honed on six weeks of monster corridors told her where to be and she was there and the geometry did not reward her for it.

Somewhere behind her, small and cold and precise, Rowe went very still.

---

The readout arrived the way it always arrived - uninvited, unbidden, the intake routine that had never once asked his permission and never would, printing itself behind the oval receptor's dead lens in the tin-precise voice that was the shell's and not his, cataloguing the thing that had just failed to notice two rifle rounds the way a man might fail to notice rain:

> INTAKE - SUBJECT UNRESOLVED
> MASS: est. 310-350 kg, forward-heavy
> LOCOMOTION: asymmetric triped assembly, no comparable gait
> ARMOR: chitin-analog plating, non-repeating luminescent seam
> WEAK POINT: INSUFFICIENT DATA - OFF-CATALOGUE, NO SPHERE MATCH, FIRST OBSERVATION ON RECORD

He had never lacked the words for this, only the willingness - the shell had given him fluent Caul from the first hour, the moment the Taking closed over him, and had never once asked whether he wanted it. He had grown up in a mill town where every machine on the floor had a maker's plate and a manual and a man three streets over who'd fix it if it broke, and he had crossed a membrane into a war that at least had the decency to number him, however cruelly, and now the one part of him that was still purely the shell's, the part he had never once asked for and had learned to live beside the way a man learns to live beside a chronic ache, told him in its cold clean voice that this thing had no maker's plate. No manual. No man three streets over who had ever laid eyes on it and lived to file the report.

Off-catalogue. He turned the two words over. In Verlaine's intake yard, on the day they'd stood him in a line of identical black shells and read out his number, someone standing at a lectern had explained - badly, in fragments, to men who did not yet have the language to understand they were being explained to - that every Sphere had a number, that the numbering was how the war kept track of what it was spending. He had learned, since, in the scraps and rumors of barracks talk, that the numbers ran higher than thirty-one, into ranges nobody in his hearing had ever named.

This did not have a number. This was the first thing in eleven months across two worlds that the machine wearing him had ever failed to place.

"No match," he said aloud, two words, flat, the shell's voice giving them no more weight than it gave anything else, and that flatness was its own kind of horror, because whatever lived inside the shell had understood, before he had, exactly what those two words meant standing at the edge of a wound eleven centuries deep.

---

Lyra didn't have time to ask him what he meant. The creature had recovered from the shield strike faster than anything its size had a right to and come around for a second pass, low, the short third limb unfolding from the chest for the first time - she saw it uncurl, saw the hooked terminus at its end catch the grey light, and understood in the half-second she had to understand it that the limb wasn't decorative and wasn't vestigial. It was a weapon nobody had built into it on purpose, that it had grown because growing it had, at some point in whatever process made things like it, simply worked.

She took the strike on the shield again and this time felt something give, a crack running the resin face that hadn't been there a minute ago, and the impact drove her back two full steps into ground that gave under her heel in a way solid ground didn't.

"Orim!"

"Not yet," he said, and she believed him, because she had learned to trust the precision of Orim's *not yet* more than she trusted most people's *now*.

It was Wren who found the seam.

She had gone quiet since the small ones died, watching this new thing with the same patient, outside-in attention she brought to everything, and when she moved it was not toward the head or the limbs but toward the flank, low, where the plating that ran in overlapping courses down the creature's side thinned and doubled back on itself at a point just behind the third limb's socket - a place where, Lyra understood later, the growth had run out of a clean pattern to follow and simply left a gap, the way a badly poured wall leaves a seam where two pours met and didn't quite fuse.

She didn't blink. She went in on her own legs, inside the reach the way the harrower stories said you had to go inside a reach, and drove both daggers into the seam to the guard.

The creature's response was not the controlled fall of a harrower taking a joint strike. It reared, the whole forward mass lifting, the short limb whipping wide and catching nothing because Wren was no longer where she'd been when she struck - and in the gap that opened, Rowe fixed the bayonet, closed the distance at a dead run with the shell's borrowed legs eating ground faster than the body they'd been built for was ever meant to, and drove the blade into the seam Wren had already opened, twisting it the same way he'd learned to in the monster corridor three days back: without grace, with everything he had.

The sound it made had no name.

Lyra had heard that description before - Old Watch shorthand, a soldier's economy for a thing they didn't want to describe more precisely - and understood now, hearing it, why nobody had ever tried harder than that. It was not a scream and it was not a roar. It was closer to the sound stone makes when it finally gives way after years of pressure, and under it, buried in it, something that might have been three or four separate notes if any throat built for singing had made them.

Then it stopped making any sound at all, and folded down into the pale moss, and did not get up. The black film it wore came away onto the moss in a wide dark stain that did not soak in, sitting on the white the way a slick sits on standing water, and underneath it, for the moment before Rowe stepped back from the body, the hide looked less like armor than like skin still waiting to be finished.

---

The silence afterward had the same quality the silence always had - spent, not peaceful - and it lasted exactly as long as it took Lyra to notice the second smell arriving underneath the first: woodsmoke, and oiled leather, and something else she placed a half-second later as blood that had already dried, carried in on someone's clothing rather than freshly spilled.

They came out of the treeline the way people move when they have done this so many times that caution has become a kind of laziness - not careless, just economical, four of them in leathers gone the same mottled grey-brown as everything else out here, carrying long-hafted pikes with barbed heads that told her, before any of them spoke, exactly what those pikes were for and how often they'd been used.

The one in front was a woman perhaps fifty, weathered past any easy reading of her age, a burn scar running from jaw to collarbone that had healed badly and long ago. She looked at the dead creature first - a long look, professional, cataloguing - and then at the party, and did not look surprised to find a Templar standing over a kill that wasn't hers to have made.

"You're off the reports," the woman said. Not a question. Her voice had the flat carry of someone used to being heard over distance and wind. "Vigil-code on the shield. You crossed the Pass checkpoint."

"Three days ago." Lyra kept the blade low, not sheathed. "You've been tracking this."

"Two days." The woman's eyes went back to the corpse, and something in her face changed - not fear, exactly, but the specific recalibration of a person whose whole professional confidence rests on having seen everything the ground can produce, meeting something the ground had not, in fact, already shown her. "We've taken eleven off this stretch since spring. Six-legs, mostly. Couple of the long-necked ones, west garrison's problem more than ours." She crouched by the body, careful, a pike's butt-end used to nudge the flank plating rather than a hand. "Not this."

"You don't know it."

"Nobody knows it." One of the others - younger, a boy who couldn't have been past twenty, with the same weathered economy of movement the woman had, learned rather than earned yet - spoke without being asked, and the woman didn't correct him for it, which told Lyra something about how this group ran. "We don't name what we haven't seen before. Naming it's a promise you'll get to tell somebody else about it."

"You'll want the biology," Lyra said. It wasn't a question either. She had read the patrol reports; she knew what the Eaters took and why, the arrangement the Liturgy's own filings referred to, when they referred to it at all, in language so bloodless it had taken her three readings to understand what it actually described: non-Liturgy Catalyst-adjacent compound, self-processed, unregulated. The institution's verdict on the people standing in front of her, as far as she had ever been able to determine from six years behind a desk, amounted to this: tolerated because they solved a problem the Liturgy had created and then declined to keep solving; unrecognized because recognizing them would require explaining, in some formal document with the Canon's seal on it, exactly why eleven hundred conscripted souls had been left at this perimeter for three weeks with instructions that amounted to survive if you can, and exactly what had been done, afterward, to the ones who had survived by methods the survey boards found distasteful to read about.

She did not say any of this. She had filed it years ago, the way she filed everything, and some fraction of that filing had lodged somewhere she couldn't fully name and had stayed there since.

"We'll want the biology," the woman agreed. "You want to walk on before we start. It's not a clean process."

"We've seen worse."

The woman looked at her for a moment - not unkindly, just assessing, the way Vaen would look at her weeks from now on a different field, though neither of them yet knew that comparison would ever occur to her - and something in the look might have been respect, or might only have been the recognition of one professional for another, doing different work on the same dying ground.

"Then you haven't seen this stretch in high summer," the woman said, and turned back to the kill, and that was the whole of the conversation the Eaters of Monsters were going to offer.

---

M-101 stood over the body a moment longer than the others, the oval receptor tilted at the angle Lyra had learned meant he was looking hard rather than merely looking, and the readout came again, unbidden, quieter this time, the way a second reading of the same instrument sometimes came back more certain than the first:

> POST-MORTEM APPRAISAL
> INTERNAL STRUCTURE: no organ analog matches prior catalogue entries, Caul or Sphere-sourced
> GROWTH PATTERN: rapid, unsupervised; gestation incomplete; age at death under two years
> CONCLUSION: NEW variant, not a mutation of known Sinterspawn stock - a distinct line, still being produced
> SPHERE-SOURCE: - NO MATCH - CATALOGUE HAS NO PROVISION FOR THIS ENTRY

He did not say this part aloud. He had the words for *still being produced*; he simply had no willingness to spend them here, and even having them changed nothing - he understood, some part of him understood, the part that was still a man from a mill town and not a war machine's clean accounting, that saying it would cost Lyra something he had no right to spend on her behalf, standing here, before whatever waited at the crater's edge had a chance to show her the rest of it herself.

He looked instead at the Eaters, already at work with knives that had done this before, efficient and unspeaking, and at the woman with the old burn scar directing them with small gestures rather than words, and filed the readout the only place he had left to file anything that had no other home: behind the dead lens, in the tin-precise voice that was not his, that would never entirely be his, that he had stopped fighting months ago because fighting it cost more than carrying it did.

Orim had not moved from where he'd stood through the whole of it, the Rod's case still shut in his hands.

"That's twice now," he said, to no one, or to Lyra, or to the part of himself that had been keeping a private count since the gate. "Twice something out here has told me it doesn't fit anywhere I know to look."

"The Rod?"

"Still nothing. I haven't raised it." He looked at the corpse, at the Eaters working over it with the unhurried competence of people for whom this was simply Tuesday. "I don't think I need the Rod to tell me what a thing this new means, this close to the edge. I think I need it to tell me how many more of them there are going to be before we reach the rim." He did not say the rest of it - that he had asked this question once before, thirty years ago, at a different distance from the same crater, and had been told the answer didn't require action. He carried that the way he carried everything: quietly, and alone, and for exactly as long as it took someone else to need it more than he did.
